Let \mathcal{B}=\{x_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m} be a (possibly overcomplete) basis of \mathfrak{g} that satisfies the relations (7) (respectively (8)) and \varphi:\mathfrak{g}\to\varphi(\mathfrak{g}) a Lie-algebra homomorphism. Define, y_{j}:=\varphi(x_{j})\in\varphi(\mathfrak{g}) . Then, since \varphi is a linear map and preserves the Lie bracket, one has for all y_{j},y_{k}\in\varphi(\mathcal{B}) : \displaystyle[y_{j},y_{k}]_{\varphi(\mathfrak{g})}=[\varphi(x_{j}),\varphi(x_{k})]_{\varphi(\mathfrak{g})}=\varphi([x_{j},x_{k}]_{\mathfrak{g}})=\varphi(\alpha_{jk}x_{\delta(j,k)})=\alpha_{jk}\varphi(x_{\delta(j,k)})=\alpha_{jk}y_{\delta(j,k)}. Hence, by application of Algorithm 1, the sets \mathcal{B} and \varphi(\mathcal{B}) generate the same graph. ∎ The observations in Theorem 26 and Corollary 27 are crucial, as they show that the graph-based approach developed here is widely applicable and not restricted to a narrow subclass of Lie algebras. In particular, it applies to all semisimple and reductive Lie algebras over algebraically closed fields. Recall that a Lie algebra is semisimple if and only if it admits a unique decomposition of the form \mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{s} , where each \mathfrak{g}_{j} is a simple ideal of \mathfrak{g} [111]. This algebraic decomposition has a direct analog in the graph-theoretic framework, as shown in the next proposition. Proposition 33. Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional semisimple Lie algebra over an algebraically closed field \mathbb{F} . Then \mathfrak{g} can be associated with a labeled directed graph G(V,E) that splits into multiple mutually unconnected subgraphs G(V_{j},E_{j}) , where each subgraph corresponds to a simple ideal \mathfrak{g}_{j}\subseteq\mathfrak{g} . In particular, if \mathfrak{g} is simple, then the associated graph consists of a single connected component. Proof. By the classification of semisimple Lie algebras over algebraically closed fields, any such Lie algebra admits a decomposition \mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{k} , where each \mathfrak{g}_{j}\subseteq\mathfrak{g} is a simple ideal of \mathfrak{g} , and k\geq 1 . These ideals are mutually commutative, i.e., [\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{\ell}]=\{0\} if j\neq\ell . By Theorem 26, each \mathfrak{g}_{j} is furthermore graph-admissible. Thus, for every \mathfrak{g}_{j} , there exists a labeled directed graph G_{j}\equiv G(V_{j},E_{j}) . These graphs must be mutually unconnected, otherwise there would exist elements x_{j}\in\mathfrak{g}_{j} and x_{k}\in\mathfrak{g}_{k} that do not vanish under the Lie bracket, even if j\neq k . If \mathfrak{g} is simple, then k=1 , and the graph G(V,E) consists of a single connected component. ∎ III Criteria for valid graphs In this section, we establish formal criteria for determining whether a labeled directed graph can be associated with a finite-dimensional Lie algebra. These criteria are essential for distinguishing proper and improper graphs and for understanding the structural constraints imposed by the Lie bracket. We focus primarily on minimal graphs associated with minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras, although some results generalize to redundant graphs as well. Note that we are here tackling the “reverse problem” to that tackled above. Definition 34. Let G(V,E) be a labeled directed graph. We call G(V,E) proper if it can be associated with a finite-dimensional Lie algebra; we call it furthermore proper-minimal if, in addition, it can be associated with a finite-dimensional Lie algebra as a minimal graph. We call G(V,E) improper if it is not a proper graph. A proper graph G(V,E) is said to be choice-independent if every edge e\in E can be translated into a valid Lie bracket [\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)]=\kappa\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) , for every \kappa\in\mathbb{F}^{*} . Otherwise, the graph is called choice-dependent, meaning that the validity of the bracket depends on specific values for \kappa . Simple criteria stemming from the antisymmetric property of the Lie bracket include the following: Proposition 35. Let G(V,E) be a labeled directed graph. Then G(V,E) is a proper graph only if all the following conditions are satisfied: (a) All edges e\in E must satisfy \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\neq\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e) . (b) The edge e=(\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)) belongs to E if and only if the edge e^{\prime}=(\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)) also belongs to E . (c) Each edge e\in E must be uniquely defined by \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e) and \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e) . That is, if two edges e,e^{\prime}\in E satisfy \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e^{\prime}) and \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)=\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e^{\prime}) , then it must follow that e=e^{\prime} . Proof. Let G(V,E) be a labeled directed graph and suppose it can be associated with a finite-dimensional Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} . Consider an edge e\in E . Then: • Condition (a): If \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e) , then the corresponding Lie bracket is [\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)]=[\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)]\propto\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) . This is not possible, since [x,x] must vanish for all x\in\mathfrak{g} , due to the antisymmetry of the Lie bracket. Therefore, such an edge cannot exist in the proper graph G(V,E) . • Condition (b): Suppose e=(\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e))\in E and e^{\prime}=(\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e))\notin E , then, by construction of an associated graph, [\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)]\propto\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) and [\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)]=0 . This violates the antisymmetry of the Lie bracket, since 0=[\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)]=-[\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)]\not\propto\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) , is impossible. • Condition (c): Suppose there exists two distinct edges e,e^{\prime}\in E with \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e^{\prime})=\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e) and \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e^{\prime})=\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e) but e\neq e^{\prime} , i.e., \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime})\neq\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) . This implies that the Lie bracket [\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)] would be simultaneously proportional to two distinct elements, namely \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime}) . Since \propto is a equivalence relation, it follows that \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\propto\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime}) , which is prohibited by Definition 28, as it would imply that the two distinct elements \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime}) lie in V and are proportional to each other. Hence, such ambiguity is not allowed in a proper graph. ∎ We can also attribute some properties to the entire associated graph: Proposition 36. Let \mathfrak{g} be an n -dimensional minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra. Then any minimal graph G(V,E) associated with \mathfrak{g} must satisfy the following properties: (a) The number of vertices equals the dimension of the Lie algebra: |V|=n ; (b) The number of edges satisfies the bound: 0\leq|E|\leq n(n-1) ; (c) For any vertex v_{\mathrm{s}}\in V , the number of outgoing edges from v_{\mathrm{s}} is bounded by: 0\leq|\{e\in E\,\mid\,\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=v_{\mathrm{s}}\}|\leq n-1 ; (d) For any vertex v_{\mathrm{e}}\in V , the number of incoming edges to v_{\mathrm{e}} is bounded by: 0\leq\{e\in E\,\mid\,\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=v_{\mathrm{e}}\}\leq n(n-1) . Proof. This can be shown straightforwardly and is therefore left to the interested reader. ∎ Proposition 37. The bounds stated in Proposition 36 are tight. That is, there exist minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras that can be associated with minimal graphs that saturate these bounds. Proof. We start by constructing an explicit example of a minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} whose associated graph achieves the upper bounds from Proposition 36. Let \{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\} be a set of linearly independent elements and set x_{0}:=0 by convention. Define the symmetric function: \displaystyle\delta:\{0,1,\ldots,n\}\times\{0,1\ldots,n\}\to\{0,1,\ldots,n\},\,(j,k)\mapsto\delta(j,k):=\left\{\begin{matrix}0&,\text{ if }j=k\text{ or }j=0\text{ or }k=0\\ 1&,\text{otherwise}\end{matrix}\right., and the antisymmetric matrix \displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathbb{F}^{n\times n}\quad\text{with}\quad\alpha_{jk}:=\left\{\begin{matrix}0&\text{, if }j=k\\ 1&\text{, if }j=1\text{ and }k>1\\ -1&\text{, if }k=1\text{ and }j>1\\ j-k&\text{ otherwise}\end{matrix}\right.. We now prove that these define the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g}=\langle\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} via the relations: [x_{j},x_{k}]:=\alpha_{jk}x_{\delta(j,k)} . Thus, we need to confirm that the Lie bracket is antisymmetric and satisfies the Jacobi identity. One has [x_{j},x_{k}]=\alpha_{jk}x_{\delta(j,k)}=-\alpha_{kj}x_{\delta(k,j)}=-[x_{k},x_{j}] for all j,k\in\{1,\ldots,n\} , since \boldsymbol{\alpha} is by construction antisymmetric and \delta symmetric. Now consider three indices j,k,\ell , all unequal to one. We compute: \displaystyle[x_{j},[x_{k},x_{\ell}]]+[x_{k},[x_{\ell},x_{j}]]+[x_{\ell},[x_{j},x_{k}]] \displaystyle=-\alpha_{k\ell}\alpha_{1j}x_{1}-\alpha_{\ell j}\alpha_{1k}x_{1}-\alpha_{jk}\alpha_{1\ell}x_{1}=-(k-\ell+\ell-j+j-k)x_{1}=0. Now, let at least one of these indices be one. Without loss of generality, let j=1 . Then, we have: \displaystyle[x_{1},[x_{k},x_{\ell}]]+[x_{k},[x_{\ell},x_{1}]]+[x_{\ell},[x_{1},x_{k}]] \displaystyle=\alpha_{1\ell}\alpha_{1k}x_{1}-\alpha_{1k}\alpha_{1\ell}x_{1}=0. Hence, by linear combinations, this constitutes a valid Lie algebra. We can now apply Algorithm 1 to obtain the graph G(V,E) which is associated with \mathfrak{g} . This graph has by construction n vertices. Next, we observe that the graph has n(n-1) distinct edges, as every distinct pair of indices (j,k)\in\mathcal{N}\times\mathcal{N} for which \alpha_{jk}\neq 0 induces a distinct edge. By construction, \alpha_{jk}=0 if and only if j=k . Thus |E|=n(n-1) . We also see that exactly n-1 edges start at x_{1} , and n(n-1) edges end at x_{1} . To show that the lower bounds are also tight, consider the abelian Lie algebra of dimension n . It is immediate to notice that any basis of this Lie algebra satisfies (7), and the graphs constructed by Algorithm 1 do not possess any edges. ∎ We can draw, as an example, the graph associated with the non-abelian Lie algebra constructed in the proof of Proposition 37 for the case n=3 , as shown in Figure 4 (a). In this visualization, every edge in the set E is explicitly drawn, resulting in a visually cluttered representation, showing that this approach is not practical and loses its visual simplicity. This is especially true for large algebras, as the number of edges grows quadratically with the number of vertices. To address this issue, we introduce the convention: For any pair of vertices v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{e}}\in V , if there exists at least one edge e\in E with \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=v_{\mathrm{s}} and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=v_{\mathrm{e}} we draw only a single edge from v_{\mathrm{s}} to v_{\mathrm{e}} , regardless of how many such edges exist. We then annotate this edge with all corresponding labeling vertices v_{\mathrm{l}} that appear in the original edge set. This convention is illustrated in Figure 4 (b). Figure 4: Depiction of the graph G(V,E) associated with the non-abelian Lie algebra from the proof of Proposition 37, for the case n=3 . In (a) visualized by drawing every edge in E explicitly, in (b) the graph is drawn using the convention of collapsing multiple edges with the same starting and end vertices into a single edge and annotating them with all relevant labeling vertices. We now proceed with our analysis. For the sake of simplicity we assume that \operatorname{char}(\mathbb{F})=0 for the remainder of this section. Theorem 38. Let G(V,E) be a labeled directed graph with three or more distinct vertices. Moreover let \mathcal{V}:=\{\tilde{V}\subseteq V\,\mid\,|\tilde{V}|=3\} be the set containing all subsets \tilde{V}\subseteq V of three distinct vertices and define, for a given set \tilde{V}\in\mathcal{V} , the edge set \tilde{E}(\tilde{V})\equiv\tilde{E}:=\{e\in E\,\mid\,\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in\tilde{V}\} containing all edges in E , where starting vertex, labeling vertex, and end vertex belong to \tilde{V} . Then G(V,E) is a proper-minimal graph only if for every \tilde{V}\in\mathcal{V} , the subgraph G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E})\subseteq G(V,E) is equivalent to one of the graphs depicted in Figure 3. Before proceeding with the proof of this claim, we observe that Theorem 38 implies several conditions for the edges of a proper graph that can be verified without exhaustively analyzing all subgraphs G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E})\subseteq G(V,E) composed of three distinct vertices and all edges e\in\{e\in E\,\mid\,\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in\tilde{V}\} . These conditions are, e.g., the ones listed in Proposition 35. It is also important to emphasize that Theorem 38 provides only a necessary condition for a graph to be a minimal graph associated with a finite-dimensional Lie algebra. It does not constitute a sufficient condition, as demonstrated in Examples 39 and 40. Remark: Theorem 38 does not provide suffiecnet conditions Example 39. Graphs of Type VIII (see Figure 3) satisfy all conditions stated in Theorem 38. However, as demonstrated in the upcoming Corollary 41, such graphs are improper graphs, meaning they cannot be associated with a finite-dimensional Lie algebra as a minimal graph. Example 40. The graph depicted below clearly satisfies all conditions from Theorem 38, as the subgraphs G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) containing three distinct vertices and all edges e in E with \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in\tilde{V} are all equivalent to graphs of Type I or II. Nevertheless, the Lie bracket relations yield: [a,[b,c]]=0 , [b,[c,a]]\propto e , and [c,[a,b]]=0 , which implies [a,[b,c]]+[b,[c,a]]+[c,[a,b]]\neq 0 , constituting a violation of the Jacobi identity. Figure 5: Illustration of an improper labeled directed graph that satisfies all conditions from Theorem 38. Figure 6: Visualization of all possible valid three-vertex subgraphs G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) of a proper graph G(V,E) , where the edges e\in\tilde{E}\subseteq E are those that satisfy \varpi_{\mathrm{(}}s)(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in\tilde{V} , considered up to topological equivalence. The eleven subgraphs are furthermore classified according to the following criteria: (a) proper versus improper graphs; (b) choice-independent versus choice-dependent, in case the subgraphs are proper; and (c) an algebraic classification of whether these graphs can be associated with abelian, nilpotent, solvable and non-nilpotent, and simple Lie algebras. A legend is provided to indicate these classifications using three colored dots per graph33 3 Note that the convention for drawing graphs has changed compared to the previous figures. This adjustment was made to distinguish general graphs from those graphs associated with specific Lie algebras, where the vertices represent particular elements. In the second convention, the label of a vertex is placed beside the vertex rather than inside.. Proof. Let G(V,E) be a labeled directed graph with three or more distinct vertices that is proper-minimal. Since any proper-minimal graph is, by definition, proper, one can invoke Proposition 35, which implies that only two types of edges can occur: • Wedge-type edges: These are edges e\in E for which all the three vertices \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) are pair-wise distinct. Such edges are called wedge-type edges because, as shown in Proposition 35, an edge e belongs to E if and only if the edge e^{\prime}=(\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)) also belongs to E . The graph G(\{\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\},\{e,e^{\prime}\}) , depicted in Figure 7 (a), resembles a wedge when the three vertices are placed at the corners of an isosceles triangle. • Loop-type edges. These are edges e\in E , where either \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e) or \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e) , with \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\neq\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e) . These are called loop-type edges because: (i) an edge e belongs to E if and only if the edge e^{\prime}=(\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)) also belongs to E , and (ii) one of those edges is of the form (a,b,a) . In other words, one of those edges is a loop, connecting a vertex to itself. The graph G(\{\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\},\{e,e^{\prime}\}) is depicted in Figure 7 (b). It is immediately evident that wedge-type and loop-type edges are distinct, and no graph isomorphism exists that maps one to the other. Figure 7: Depiction of wedge-type edges in (a) and loop-type edges in (b). Note that we use a different convention for visualizing vertices, if the figure is intended to convey a general feature and not a particular Lie algebra, as before. We proceed here with the proof of the theorem. Consider every subgraph G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E})\subseteq G(V,E) , where \tilde{V}=\{a,b,c\}\subseteq V is a set of three distinct vertices, and \tilde{E}=\{e\in E\,\mid\,\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in\tilde{V}\} is the set edges whose source, label, and target all lie in \tilde{V} . Due to the edge-rule discussed in Propositions 35 and 36 , the number of edges is even and bounded from above, such that one has |\tilde{E}|\in\{0,2,4,6\} . We now analyze these cases separately, and use upper-case roman indices to refer to the corresponding graphs in Figure 3: (i) \boldsymbol{\tilde{E}=0} . Here, there is only one possible graph: G_{\mathrm{I}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{I}}) , where \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{I}}=\emptyset . (ii) \boldsymbol{\tilde{E}=2} . Here, there are nine possible graphs, divided into two groups, on group of three graphs with only wedge-type edges, and another group of six graphs with only loop-type edges. The three graphs with only wedge-type edges are the ones with \tilde{E}=\{(a,b,c),(b,a,c)\} , \tilde{E}=\{(b,c,a),(c,b,a)\} , and \tilde{E}=\{(c,a,b),(a,c,b)\} . These are clearly all equivalent, as any one of those can be transformed into the other two by a simple relabeling of the vertices. The six graphs with only loop-type edges are those with \tilde{E}=\{(a,b,a),(b,a,a)\} , \tilde{E}=\{(a,b,b),(b,a,b)\}) , \tilde{E}=\{(a,c,a),(c,a,a)\} , \tilde{E}=\{(a,c,c),(c,a,c)\} , \tilde{E}=\{(b,c,b),(c,b,b)\} , and \tilde{E}=\{(b,c,c),(c,b,c)\} . These are also all clearly equivalent among themselves, but not equivalent to the previous wedge-type graphs. Thus, we identify two distinct yet equivalent graph-types: G_{\mathrm{II}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{II}}) and G_{\mathrm{III}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{III}}) , with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{II}}\equiv\{(a,b,c),(b,a,c)\} and \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{III}}\equiv\{(a,c,c),(c,a,c)\} respectively. (iii) \boldsymbol{\tilde{E}=4} . This case is subdivided into three distinct subcases, depending on the types of edges involved. (iii.i) All four edges are wedge-type. Here, there is only one class of inequivalent subgraphs in this category, namely subgraphs of the form G_{\mathrm{IV}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{IV}}) , with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{IV}}\equiv\{(c,b,a),(b,c,a),(c,a,b),(a,c,b)\} . (iii.ii) All four edges are loop-type. Here, one finds three inequivalent subgraphs: • G_{\mathrm{XII}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{XII}}) , with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{XII}}\equiv\{(a,c,c),(c,a,c),(c,b,b),(b,c,b)\} , • G_{\mathrm{V}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{V}}) , with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{V}}\equiv\{(a,c,c),(c,a,c),(b,c,c),(c,b,c)\} , • G_{\mathrm{VI}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{VI}}) with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{VI}}\equiv\{(c,a,a),(a,c,a),(c,b,b),(b,c,b)\} . Notably, subgraphs of the type G_{\mathrm{XII}} are incompatible with the Jacobi identity. Specifically: [b,[c,a]]\propto b , while [a,[b,c]]\propto[a,b]\in\spn\{V\setminus\tilde{V}\} . If [a,b]=0 , then [a,[b,c]]+[b,[c,a]]+[c,[a,b]]\propto b , which violates the Jacobi identity. If instead [a,b]\propto x , where x\in V\setminus\tilde{V} , then [c,x] must be proportional to an appropriate linear combination of x and b , which is prohibited for a minimal graph since all bracket results must are elements of V , which must be a basis. Hence, graphs containing subgraphs of type G_{\mathrm{XII}} cannot be associated with finite-dimensional Lie algebras as minimal graphs. (iii.iii) Two wedge-type and two are loop-type edges. Here, one finds the following two inequivalent subgraphs: • G_{\mathrm{VII}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{VII}}) , with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{VII}}\{(a,c,c),(c,a,c),(a,b,c),(b,a,c)\} , • G_{\mathrm{VIII}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{VIII}}) , with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{VIII}}\equiv\{(a,c,c),(c,a,c),(b,c,a),(c,b,a)\} . (iv) \boldsymbol{\tilde{E}=6} . This last case is divided into four subcases based on the composition of wedge-type and loop-type edges. (iv.i) All six edges are wedge-type. There is only one inequivalent subgraph in this category, namely G_{\mathrm{IX}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{IX}}) with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{IX}}\equiv\{(a,b,c),(b,a,c),(b,c,a),(c,b,a),(c,a,b),(a,c,b)\} . (iv.ii) All six edges are loop-type. Here, there exist two inequivalent subgraphs: • G_{\mathrm{XIII}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{XIII}}) , with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{XIII}}\equiv\{(a,c,c),(c,a,c),(c,b,b),(b,c,b),(b,a,a),(a,b,a)\} , • G_{\mathrm{XIV}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{XIV}}) , with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{XIV}}\equiv\{(c,a,a),(a,c,a),(c,b,b),(b,c,b),(a,b,b),(b,a,b)\} . We will now show that in both cases the graph G(V,E) cannot be associated with a finite-dimensional Lie algebra as a minimal subgraph. For G_{\mathrm{XIII}} , one would have that [a,[b,c]]\propto a , [b,[c,a]]\propto b , and [c,[a,b]]\propto c . Thus, the Jacobi identity cannot be satisfied as one assumes that the three elements a,b,c are linearly independent and can therefore cannot be combined linearly and vanish. Also, graphs with subgraphs of type G_{\mathrm{XIV}} are prohibited, since this would imply [a,b]=\kappa_{1}b , [b,c]=\kappa_{2}b , and [c,a]=\kappa_{3}c , where \kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3}\in\mathbb{F}^{*} . The Jacobi identity in this case would read: [a,[b,c]]+[b,[c,a]]+[c,[a,b]]=\kappa_{2}\kappa_{1}b+\kappa_{3}\kappa_{2}b-\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}b=\kappa_{3}\kappa_{2}b\neq 0 , which contradicts the very definition of the Jacobi identity. (iv.iii) Four wedge-type edges and two are loop-type edges. Here, there is only one inequivalent subgraph, namely G_{\mathrm{XV}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{XV}}) , with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{XV}}\equiv\{(a,c,c),(c,a,c),(a,b,c),(b,a,c),(b,c,a),(c,b,a)\} . This configuration implies: [a,[b,c]]+[b,[c,a]]+[c,[a,b]]\propto a , which is again a violation of the Jacobi identity. (iv.iv) Two wedge-type edges and four are loop-type edges. Here, there are three inequivalent subgraphs: • G_{\mathrm{XVI}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{XVI}}) , with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{XVI}}\equiv\{(a,c,c),(c,a,c),(a,b,c),(b,a,c),(c,b,b),(b,c,b)\} , • G_{\mathrm{X}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{X}}) , with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{X}}\equiv\{(c,a,a),(a,c,a),(c,b,b),(b,c,b),(a,b,c),(b,a,c)\} , • G_{\mathrm{XI}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{XI}}) , with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{XI}}\equiv\{(a,c,c),(c,a,c),(b,c,c),(c,b,c),(a,b,c),(b,a,c)\} . Subgraphs of the type G_{\mathrm{XVI}} would imply [a,[b,c]]\propto c , [b,[c,a]]\propto b , and [c,[a,b]]=0 . Thus, a,b,c cannot satisfy the Jacobi identity under the assumption that they are linearly independent. This concludes the case-by-case analysis of all possible three-vertex subgraphs. Each valid configuration corresponds to one of the eleven graphs depicted in Figure 3, and any deviation from these possible configuartions leads to a violation of either the Jacobi identity, bilinearity, antisymmetry of the Lie bracket, or the minimality condition. ∎ Corollary 41. All graphs depicted in Figure 3 are proper subgraphs, except those of Type VIII. Furthermore, all proper graphs depicted in Figure 3 are choice-independent, except those of Type X and XI. Proof. The edges of the graphs depicted in Figure 3 define the Lie bracket relations among the three vertex elements, up to irrelevant nonzero proportionality constants. In this context, and without loss of generality, we here treat the graphs in Figure 3 as standalone graphs themselves, not as subgraphs of a larger graph. Consequently, missing edges imply that the corresponding Lie bracket vanishes since the bracket cannot connect to a vertex outside the graph. To determine whether a graph is proper, it suffices to verify whether the Jacobi identity is satisfied, since the vertex set V spans a vector space and the Lie bracket is antisymmetric by construction. To determine whether a given graph is choice-independent, one must similarly verify if the Jacobi identity holds irrespective of the choice for proportionality constants associated with the Lie bracket defined by the edges. We now analyze each graph type individually. • Type I: The Lie bracket of all basis elements and therefore of all elements vanishes. Thus, Type I graphs can be associated with the three-dimensional abelian Lie algebra, making them proper and choice-independent. • Type II: The only non-trivial Lie bracket is [a,b]\propto c . Thus [a,[b,c]]=0=[b,[c,a]]=[c,[a,b]] , making graphs of Type II proper and choice-independent. • Type III: The only non-trivial Lie bracket is [a,c]\propto c . Thus [a,[b,c]]=0=[b,[c,a]]=[c,[a,b]] , making graphs of Type III proper and choice-independent. • Type IV: The only non-trivial Lie brackets are [b,c]\propto a and [a,c]\propto b . Thus [a,[b,c]]=0=[b,[c,a]]=[c,[a,b]] , making graphs of Type IV proper and choice-independent. • Type V: The only non-trivial Lie brackets are [a,c]=\kappa_{1}c and [b,c]=\kappa_{2}c , where \kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in\mathbb{F}^{*} . Thus [a,[b,c]]=\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}c , [b,[c,a]]=-\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}c , and [c,[a,b]]=0 . Hence [a,[b,c]]+[b,[c,a]]+[c,[a,b]]=0 , making graphs of Type V proper and choice-independent. • Type VI: The only non-trivial Lie brackets are [c,a]\propto a and [b,c]\propto b . Thus [a,[b,c]]=0=[b,[c,a]]=[c,[a,b]] , making graphs of Type VI proper and choice-independent. • Type VII: The only non-trivial Lie brackets are [a,c]\propto c and [a,b]\propto c . Thus [a,[b,c]]=0=[b,[c,a]]=[c,[a,b]] , making graphs of Type VII proper and choice-independent. • Type VIII: The only non-trivial Lie brackets are [a,c]=\kappa_{2}c and [b,c]=\kappa_{3}a with \kappa_{2},\kappa_{3}\in\mathbb{F}^{*} . Thus [a,[b,c]]=0 , [b,[c,a]]=-\kappa_{2}\kappa_{3}a , and [c,[a,b]]=0 . Hence [a,[b,c]]+[b,[c,a]]+[c,[a,b]]\propto a , making graphs of Type VIII improper. • Type IX: The non-trivial Lie brackets are [a,b]\propto c , [b,c]\propto a , and [a,c]\propto b . Thus [a,[b,c]]=0=[b,[c,a]]=[c,[a,b]] , making graphs of Type IX proper and choice-independent. • Type X: The non-trivial Lie brackets are [a,b]=\kappa_{1}c , [a,c]=\kappa_{2}a , and [b,c]=\kappa_{3}b , where \kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3}\in\mathbb{F}^{*} . Thus [a,[b,c]]=\kappa_{1}\kappa_{3}c , [b,[c,a]]=\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}c , and [c,[a,b]]=0 . Hence [a,[b,c]]+[b,[c,a]]+[c,[a,b]]=\kappa_{1}(\kappa_{2}+\kappa_{3})c . To satisfy the Jacobi identity, one needs, therefore, to require \kappa_{2}=-\kappa_{3} , making graphs of Type X proper and choice-dependent. • Type XI: The non-trivial Lie brackets are [a,b]=\kappa_{1}c , [a,c]=\kappa_{2}c , and [b,c]=\kappa_{3}c , where \kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3}\in\mathbb{F}^{*} . Thus [a,[b,c]]=\kappa_{1}\kappa_{3}c , [b,[c,a]]=-\kappa_{2}\kappa_{3}c , and [c,[a,b]]=0 . Hence [a,[b,c]]+[b,[c,a]]+[c,[a,b]]=\kappa_{3}(\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{2})c . To satisfy the Jacobi identity, one needs, therefore, to require \kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2} , making graphs of Type XI proper and choice-dependent. ∎ Corollary 42. Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with a labeled directed graph G(V,E) , where the vertices satisfy [v_{j},[v_{k},v_{\ell}]]=0 for all j,k,\ell with j\neq k\neq\ell\neq j . Then, for any choice of three distinct vertices \{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}=\tilde{V}\subseteq V , and considering only edges e\in\tilde{E}=\{e=(v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}},v_{\mathrm{e}})\in E\,\mid\,v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}},v_{\mathrm{e}}\in\tilde{V}\} , the resulting subgraph G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) must be of Type I, II, III, IV, VI, VII, or IX, depicted in Figure 3. Proof. This statement follows from the same computations performed in the proof of Corollary 41. ∎ Corollary 43. The proper graphs in Figure 3 can each be associated with at least one real three-dimensional Lie algebra. The naming conventions used below for these Lie algebras follow the classification given in the literature [130, 140]. We have: • Type I: The abelian Lie algebra 3\mathfrak{l}_{1}=\mathfrak{l}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{l}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{l}_{1} , also known as Bianchi I. • Type II: The Heisenberg algebra \mathfrak{l}_{3,1}\cong\mathfrak{h}_{1} [103, 81], also known as Bianchi II, determined by the nontrivial Lie bracket: [e_{2},e_{3}]=e_{1} . • Type III: The Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{2,1}\oplus\mathfrak{l}_{1} , also known as Bianchi III, determined by the nontrivial Lie bracket: [e_{1},e_{2}]=e_{2} . • Type IV: The solvable Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{3,5}^{\beta=0} , also known as Bianchi VII, determined by the nontrivial Lie brackets [e_{1},e_{3}]=\beta e_{1}-e_{2} and [e_{2},e_{3}]=e_{1}+\beta e_{2} with \beta=0 . • Type V: The Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{2,1}\oplus\mathfrak{l}_{1} , also known as Bianchi III, determined by the nontrivial Lie bracket: [e_{1},e_{2}]=e_{2} . • Type VI: The solvable Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{3,3} , also known as Bianchi V, determined by the nontrivial Lie brackets [e_{1},e_{3}]=e_{1} and [e_{2},e_{3}]=e_{2} . • Type VII: The Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{2,1}\oplus\mathfrak{l}_{1} , also known as Bianchi III, determined by the nontrivial Lie bracket: [e_{1},e_{2}]=e_{2} . • Type IX: The simple Lie algebra \mathfrak{gl}_{3,7} , also known as Bianchi IX, or \mathfrak{so}_{\mathbb{R}}(3)\cong\mathfrak{su}(2) [75], determined by the nontrivial Lie brackets [e_{2},e_{3}]=e_{1} , [e_{3},e_{1}]=e_{2} , and [e_{1},e_{2}]=e_{3} . • Type X: The simple Lie algebra \mathfrak{gl}_{3,6} , also known as Bianchi VIII, or \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}) [87], determined by the non-trivial Lie brackets [e_{1},e_{2}]=e_{1} , [e_{2},e_{3}]=e_{3} , and [e_{1},e_{3}]=2e_{2} . • Type XI: The Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{2,1}\oplus\mathfrak{l}_{1} , also known as Bianchi III, determined by the nontrivial Lie bracket: [e_{1},e_{2}]=e_{2} . This statement does not claim completeness of identification or classification. For instance, the Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{3,4} , also known as Bianchi VI, determined by the nontrivial Lie brackets [e_{1},e_{3}]=e_{1} and [e_{2},e_{3}]=\alpha e_{2} , with -1\leq\alpha<1 and \alpha\neq 0 , can be associated with a Type VI graph. Proof. First note that by Corollary 41, the graphs listed above are all proper graphs depicted in Figure 3. Most of these graphs can be furthermore directly obtained by applying Algorithm 1 to the respective bases of the Lie algebras stated above. However, this is not the case for the graphs of Type V, VII, and XI. To demonstrate that these graphs can be associated with the Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{2,1}\oplus\mathfrak{l}_{1} , which is defined by the nontrivial Lie bracket [e_{1},e_{2}]=e_{2} , while e_{3}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{l}_{2,1}\oplus\mathfrak{l}_{1}) , we consider the following alternative bases: For Type V, one uses the basis \{e_{1},e_{1}+e_{3},e_{2}\} . Applying Algorithm 1 to this basis yields a graph of Type V. For Type VII, one uses the basis \{e_{1},e_{2}+e_{3},e_{2}\} , and for Type XI, the basis \{e_{2},e_{1}-e_{2},e_{1}+e_{2}+e_{3}\} , which both yield the respective graphs. ∎ Lemma 44. Let G(V,E) be a proper graph associated with a finite-dimensional Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} . Let G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) be a subgraph of G(V,E) with |\tilde{V}|\geq 3 . Then G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) is a proper subgraph associated with a Lie subalgebra \mathfrak{h}\subseteq\mathfrak{g} if \tilde{E}:=\{e\in E\,\mid\,\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in\tilde{V}\} . Proof. This result is evident from the construction of the associated graphs and requires no further argument. ∎ Corollary 45. Let G(V,E) be a proper-minimal graph associated with a real finite-dimensional Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} . If G(V,E) contains a subgraph of Type IX or Type X, then \mathfrak{g} contains a simple three-dimensional Lie subalgebra, i.e., a Lie subalgebra isomorphic to \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}) or \mathfrak{su}(2) , rendering \mathfrak{g} not solvable. Proof. By Lemma 44, the Lie algebra associated with the graph G(V,E) contains a subalgebra \mathfrak{h}\subseteq\mathfrak{g} that is associated with a subgraph of Type IX or X. Let this subgraph be denoted by G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) . Since G(V,E) is proper-minimal, the three vertices in \tilde{V} are linearly independent. Furthermore, one observes that \tilde{V}\subseteq[\tilde{V},\tilde{V}] , which implies that the subalgebra \mathfrak{h}:=\spn\{\tilde{V}\} is not solvable. According to the Bianchi-classification, the only non-solvable real Lie algebras of dimension three are the simple Lie algebras \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}) , and \mathfrak{su}(2) [28]. Therefore, \mathfrak{g} contains a simple three-dimensional Lie subalgebra, which makes it not solvable. ∎ Proposition 46. Let G(V,E) be a labeled directed graph. For G(V,E) to be a proper-minimal graph, it must satisfy the following condition: For every subset \tilde{V}=\{a,b,c\}\subseteq V of three distinct vertices, one of the following must hold: (a) There exist three directed trails C_{1}=(a,e_{11},x_{1},e_{12},y) , C_{2}=(b,e_{21},x_{2},e_{22},y) , and C_{3}=(c,e_{31},x_{3},e_{32},y) , where x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},y\in V and the labeling conditions are \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{22})=\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{31})=a , \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{11})=\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{32})=b , \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{12})=\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{21})=c . (b) There exists a bijection \phi:V\to V that acts on the set E of edges as follows: \phi:E\to\phi(E) , with concrete action \phi:e=(v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}},v_{\mathrm{e}})\mapsto\phi(e):=(\phi(v_{\mathrm{s}}),\phi(v_{\mathrm{l}}),\phi(v_{\mathrm{e}})), and two directed trails C_{1}=(\phi(a),\phi(e_{11}),\phi(x_{1}),\phi(e_{12}),\phi(y)) and C_{2}=(\phi(b),\phi(e_{21}),\phi(x_{2}),\phi(e_{22}),\phi(y)) , where x_{1},x_{2},y\in V , and the labeling conditions are: \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(\phi(e_{22}))=\phi(a) , \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(\phi(e_{12}))=\phi(b) , and \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(\phi(e_{11}))=\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(\phi(e_{21}))=\phi(c) , while there exists no directed trail C_{3}=(\phi(a),\phi(e_{31}),\phi(x_{3}),\phi(e_{32}),\phi(y)) , where x_{3}\in V , \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(\phi(e_{31}))=\phi(b) , and \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(\phi(e_{32}))=\phi(c) . (c) For every v\in\tilde{V} there exists no edges e,e^{\prime}\in E such that \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=v , \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\in\tilde{V}\setminus\{v\} , \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e^{\prime})=\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) and \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e^{\prime})\in\tilde{V}\setminus\{v,\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\} . Figure 8: Visualization of the three structural conditions described in Proposition 46. Note that this figure presents an abstraction and does not necessarily depict the actual graphs, since the Lie brackets involving the vertices \tilde{V}=\{a,b,c\} may be proportional to the vertices themselves, e.g., [c,a]\propto c . Proof. Every set \{a,b,c\}\subseteq V of distinct vertices must satisfy the Jacobi identity: \displaystyle[a,[b,c]]+[b,[c,a]]+[c,[a,b]]=0. Since all vertices satisfy relations (7) and are linear independent elements, there are three distinct possibilities for how the Jacobi idenity can ve satisfied: (i) All three nested brackets are proportional to the same nonzero element: [a,[b,c]]\propto[b,[c,a]]\propto[c,[a,b]] . (ii) Two nested brackets are proportional to the same element, while the third one vanishes. Without loss of generality: [a,[b,c]]\propto[b,[c,a]] , while [c,[a,b]]=0 . (iii) All nested brackets vanish: [a,[b,c]]=0=[b,[c,a]]=[c,[a,b]] . These cases are the only algebraic configurations that satisfy the Jacobi identity under the given assumption. It is immediate to observe that \displaystyle\text{Case }(i) \displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\text{Condition }(a),\; \displaystyle\;\text{Case }(ii) \displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\text{Condition }(b),\; \displaystyle\;\text{Case }(iii) \displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\text{Condition }(c), as verified by the application of Algorithm 1. ∎ We can also simplify the procedure to check every triple of vertices by the following simple observation: Proposition 47. Let G(V,E) be a labeled directed graph with vertices V=\{v_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} and edges E=\{e_{p}\}_{p\in\mathcal{P}} that satisfies the properties (a) - (c) from Proposition 35. Define the map \delta:(\mathcal{M}\cup\{0\})\times(\mathcal{M}\cup\{0\})\to\mathcal{M}\cup\{0\} with concrete action \displaystyle\delta:(j,k)\mapsto\delta(j,k):=\left\{\begin{matrix}\ell&\text{ if }\exists\text{ edge $e\in E$ with }e=(v_{j},v_{k},v_{\ell})\\ 0&\text{ otherwise}\end{matrix}\right.. (15) Then: (i) G(V,E) is a proper choice-independent graph if \delta(j,\delta(k,\ell))=0 for all j,k,\ell\in\mathcal{M} ; (ii) G(V,E) is a proper-minimal graph only if \left|\left\{\delta(j,\delta(k,\ell))\,\middle\mid\,j,k,\ell\in\mathcal{M}\,\wedge j\neq k\neq\ell\neq j\right\}\setminus\{0\}\right|\leq 1 . Proof. Suppose G(V,E) is a labeled directed graph satisfying the conditions stated above, i.e., properties (a) - (c) from Proposition 35. We begin by observing the following: The map \delta is well-defined by property (c); it is symmetric by property (b); and satisfies \delta(j,j)=0 by property (a) from Proposition 35. Now consider the vector space \spn\{V\} equipped with the bilinear operation [\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,] defined on the basis elements via [v_{j},v_{k}]=\alpha_{jk}v_{\delta(j,k)} , where v_{0}:=0 and \boldsymbol{\alpha} is chosen to be antisymmetric. One can for example define \alpha_{jk}:=(j-k)(1-\delta_{\delta(j,k),0}) , ensuring that \boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\mathrm{Tp}}=-\boldsymbol{\alpha} . By construction, the bracket [\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,] is antisymmetric. Thus, one is left verifying that [\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,] satisfies the Jacobi identity. Observe that if \delta(j,\delta(k,\ell))=0 for all j,k,\ell\in\mathcal{M} , then \displaystyle[v_{j},[v_{k},v_{\ell}]]+[v_{k},[v_{\ell},v_{j}]]+[v_{\ell},[v_{j},v_{k}]] \displaystyle=\alpha_{j,\delta(k,\ell)}\alpha_{k\ell}v_{\delta(j,\delta(k,\ell))}+\alpha_{k,\delta(\ell,j)}\alpha_{\ell j}v_{\delta(k,\delta(\ell,j))}+\alpha_{\ell,\delta(j,k))}\alpha_{jk}v_{\delta(\ell,\delta(j,k))}=0, since each term is proportional to a vertex of the form v_{\delta(j,\delta(k,\ell))}\equiv v_{0}=0 . This proves claim (i). Note this conclusion does not require the assumption that V is a linearly independent set. Let us proceed to prove claim (ii). Suppose the contrapostive, i.e., that \left|\left\{\delta(j,\delta(k,\ell))\,\middle\mid\,j,k,\ell\in\mathcal{M}\,\wedge j\neq k\neq\ell\neq j\right\}\setminus\{0\}\right|\geq 2 . Without loss of generality, assume that \delta(j,\delta(k,\ell))\neq\delta(k,\delta(\ell,j)) , where \delta(j,\delta(k,\ell)),\delta(k,\delta(\ell,j))\in\mathcal{M} , and j\neq k\neq\ell\neq j . Then, the terms \alpha_{j,\delta(k,\ell)}\alpha_{k\ell}v_{\delta(j,\delta(k,\ell))} and \alpha_{k,\delta(\ell,j)}\alpha_{\ell j}v_{\delta(k,\delta(\ell,j))} are linearly independent elements in \spn\{V\} , since \operatorname{char}(\mathbb{F})=0 . If V is taken to be a basis, then the third term \alpha_{\ell,\delta(j,k))}\alpha_{jk}v_{\delta(\ell,\delta(j,k))} cannot cancel both of the previous terms. This shows that violating the condition \left|\left\{\delta(j,\delta(k,\ell))\,\middle\mid\,j,k,\ell\in\mathcal{M}\,\wedge j\neq k\neq\ell\neq j\right\}\setminus\{0\}\right|\leq 1 implies that G(V,E) cannot be associated with a minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra as a minimal graph. ∎ Here, one needs to mention that Propositions 46 and 47 only provide weak criteria for checking the validity of graphs that are based on the Jacobi identity, since they are only applicable for minimal graphs and only provide necessary conditions. However, they are not sufficient to distinguish between choice-dependent and choice-independent graphs, which is essential in some cases, especially when this classification is also affected by the characteristic of the field over which the algebra is defined. For further remarks on this topic see Appendix A. IV Structural properties of finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebras via associated graphs We are now equipped with all the necessary tools to identify structural properties of a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} using only the graphs G(V,E) associated with \mathfrak{g} . Among the most common structural classifications of Lie algebras are those based on the notions of abelian, nilpotent, solvable, simple, semisimple, or reductive Lie algebras. These classifications rely typically on algebraic constructions such as the lower central series, the derived series, or the identification of ideals or central elements. In what follows, we explore how these structural features can be inferred directly from the graph representation, without requiring explicit computation of the Lie bracket beyond its graphical encoding. For simplicity, we proceed by assuming that the characteristic of the field \mathbb{F} is \operatorname{char}(\mathbb{F})=0 , and continue thereby with the same assumption introduced in the previous section. IV.1 Identifying central elements Lemma 48. Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the labeled directed graph G(V,E) . Let W\subseteq V be the set of all vertices with no outgoing edges. Then \spn\{W\} is a subalgebra of the center \mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) of \mathfrak{g} . Proof. Let w\in W\subseteq V be a vertex with no outgoing edges. Then, by the construction of Algorithm 1, there exists no element v\in V such that [v,w]\neq 0 . Since the set V spans the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , any element g\in\mathfrak{g} can be written as a linear combination: g=\sum_{v\in V}c_{v}v with some coefficients c_{v}\in\mathbb{F} . Therefore, for any such g , one has [w,g]=0 for all g\in\mathfrak{g} , implying that w\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) . This argument holds for every vertex w\in W that has no outgoing edges, and all their linear combinations. Thus \spn\{W\}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) . ∎ It is important to note that Lemma 48 does not guarantee that the set of all vertices with no outgoing edges spans the whole center \mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) of the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} . That is, while such vertices always lie in the center, they may contain additional elements not represented directly by single vertices without outgoing edges. To illustrated this, consider the real Lie algebra visualized in Figure 9. Figure 9: Graph associated to the real Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , spanned by the basis elements x_{1} , x_{2} , x_{3} satisfying [x_{1},x_{2}]=0 , [x_{2},x_{3}]=x_{3} , and [x_{3},x_{1}]=x_{3} , which is known as the Lie algebra \mathfrak{aff}(\mathbb{R})\oplus\mathbb{R} [140, 9]. In panel (a), the graph G(V,E) constructed from this basis has no vertices without outgoing edges, i.e., the set W from Lemma 48 is empty. Nonetheless, the center of the Lie algebra is clearly nontrivial, as \mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g})=\langle\{x_{1}+x_{2}\}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} . In panel (b), we constructed the graph using the modified basis \mathcal{B}=\{\tilde{x}_{1}:=x_{1},\tilde{x}_{3}:=x_{3},\tilde{x}_{2}:=x_{1}+x_{2}\} . Here, one has clearly \spn\{W\}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) . We can, however, remedy this problem with the following result: Lemma 49. Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra with non-trivial center. Then \mathfrak{g} admits a labeled directed graph G(V,E) such that the set of all vertices with no outgoing edges spans the center \mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) . Proof. Let G(V,E) be a graph associated with a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , and suppose \mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g})\neq\{0\} . Let V=\{v_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m} denote the set of vertices spanning \mathfrak{g} . Let C=\{c_{j}\}_{j=1}^{k} be a basis for the center \mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) . If any c_{j}\in C is not proportional to an existing vertex v\in V , one can simply add c_{j} to V . Since each c_{j}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) , it follows that [v,c_{j}]=0 for all v\in V , and hence the modified set V is a valid set of vertices, according to Definition 16, and hence the subset of vertices in V with no outgoing edges spans the center \mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) . ∎ Lemma 50. Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with a labeled directed graph G(V,E) . Then \mathfrak{g} is abelian if and only if the set of edges E is empty. Proof. Let G(V,E) be a graph associated with a finite-dimensional Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} . Suppose E is empty. Then there exists no edge e\in E , and hence no triple (v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}},v_{\mathrm{e}})\in V^{\times 3} such that [v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}}]\propto v_{\mathrm{e}} . Thus [v_{1},v_{2}]=0 for all v_{1},v_{2}\in V . Since, \spn\{V\}=\mathfrak{g} , it follows by linearity [\spn\{V\},\spn\{V\}]=[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]\subseteq\{0\} , implying that \mathfrak{g} is abelian. Conversely, suppose \mathfrak{g} is abelian. Then any (possibly overcomplete) basis \mathcal{B}=\{\tilde{x}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} satisfies [\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{k}]=0 for all j,k\in\mathcal{M} . By the construction of Algorithm 1, the graph associated with \mathcal{B} contains no edges, i.e., E=\emptyset . ∎ Lemma 51. Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra. If \mathfrak{g} admits a labeled directed graph G(V,E) that splits into two unconnected subgraphs, then \mathfrak{g} is the direct sum of two mutually abelian subalgebras. Proof. The claim follows immediately from the definitions of the associated graphs. ∎ We conclude with the following observation. In Lemma 49 we established that for any finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra, one can construct a graph that faithfully represents the center \mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) . That is the set of vertices with no outgoing edges spans the center. However, the provided construction does generally not yield a minimal graph since it involves adding new vertices to represent central elements not already present in the original basis. This raises a natural question: Is it possible to construct a minimal graph that faithfully represents the center of the Lie algebra if it is minimal-graph-admissible. The following conjecture addresses this question. Conjecture 52. Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra with non-trivial center. Then \mathfrak{g} admits a minimal graph G(V,E) , where the set of all vertices with no outgoing edges spans the center \mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) . IV.2 Computing the derived series Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra with a basis \mathcal{B}=\{x_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n} that satisfies the Lie bracket relations (7). Let G(V,E) denote the labeled directed graph associated with \mathfrak{g} , constructed via Algorithm 1 using the basis \mathcal{B} . The first derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{g}=[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}] is spanned by the set \{\alpha_{jk}x_{\delta(j,k)}\}_{j,k=1}^{n} , which is clearly linearly dependent, as for, for example, the zero element belongs to the set, since 0=\alpha_{jj}x_{\delta(j,j)} for all j\in\mathcal{N} . To refine this set, we proceed as follows: We remove all elements \alpha_{jk}x_{\delta(j,k)} for which \alpha_{jk}=0 or respectively \delta(j,k)=0 . We remove, furthermore, duplicate elements. That is, we remove all but one element for which \delta(j,k)=\delta(p,q) and \alpha_{jk}\neq 0\neq\alpha_{pq} . In particular, due to the antisymmetry of \boldsymbol{\alpha} and the symmetry of \delta , the elements \alpha_{jk}x_{\delta(j,k)} and \alpha_{kj}x_{\delta(k,j)} are proportional to each other. To formalize the recursive computation of the derived series, we define the following sequence of index sets: \displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell+1)}:=\delta\left(\left\{(j,k)\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\times\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\mid\;\alpha_{jk}\neq 0\right\}\right)\quad\text{for all }\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0},\quad\text{where}\quad\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(0)}:=\mathcal{N}. (16) This recursive definition can be analogously applied not only to minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras but also for redundant-graph-admissible ones and bases of redundant graphs. The key observation is that the following result implies that \mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{g}=\spn\{x_{j}\mid\,j\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}\} , which allows to compute the entire derived series by this recursive procedure via \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g}=\spn\{x_{j}\mid\,j\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\} . Lemma 53. Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra. Then, the derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} is also graph-admissible for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} . Furthermore, if \mathfrak{g} is minimal-graph-admissible, then \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} is also minimal-graph-admissible for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} . Proof. Let \{\tilde{x}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} be an overcomplete basis of the finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} that satisfies the Lie bracket relations (8). We define recursively the sets: \displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell+1)}:=\delta\left(\left\{(j,k)\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\mid\;\tilde{\alpha}_{jk}\neq 0\right\}\right)\quad\text{for all }\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0},\quad\text{where}\quad\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(0)}:=\mathcal{M}. (17) By virtue of Definition 16 of \delta it is immediate that \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell+1)}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)} for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} . Furthermore, one notes the following: • Suppose z\in\mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{g} . Then there exist pairs of elements x_{j},y_{j}\in\mathfrak{g} , indexed by a finite non-empty index set \mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1} , such that z=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}[x_{j},y_{j}] holds44 4 To realize that, recall that z\in[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}] if and only if z\in\spn\{[x,y]\,\mid\,x,y\in\mathfrak{g}\} . Thus, any z\in\mathcal{D}\mathfrak{g} can be written as z=\sum_{j,k}c_{jk}[x_{j},x_{k}] , where j,k denote indices from suitable index sets and c_{jk} are appropriate coefficients. However, due to the bilinearity of the Lie bracket, one can write z=\sum_{j}[x_{j},y_{j}] with y_{j}=\sum_{k}c_{jk}x_{k} , establishing the validity of the initial claim.. Since \{\tilde{x}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} spans \mathfrak{g} , there exist coefficients \mu_{jp}\in\mathbb{F} and \nu_{jq}\in\mathbb{F} , with p,q\in\mathcal{M} , such that x_{j}=\sum_{p\in\mathcal{M}}\mu_{jp}\tilde{x}_{p} and y_{j}=\sum_{q\in\mathcal{M}}\nu_{jq}\tilde{x}_{q} . Then: \displaystyle z=\sum_{p,q\in\mathcal{M}}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mu_{pj}\nu_{qj}[\tilde{x}_{p},\tilde{x}_{q}]=\sum_{p,q\in\mathcal{M}}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mu_{pj}\nu_{qj}\tilde{\alpha}_{pq}\tilde{x}_{\delta(p,q)} Thus z\in\spn\{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}\} , by the definition of \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)} , and the convention that \delta(p,q)=0 whenever \tilde{\alpha}_{pq}=0 . • Suppose z\in\spn\{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}\} . Then there exists coefficients \lambda_{j}\in\mathbb{F} with j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)} such that z=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}}\lambda_{j}\tilde{x}_{j} . By the definition of \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)} , there exists indices p_{j},q_{j}\in\mathcal{M} such that [\tilde{x}_{p_{j}},\tilde{x}_{q_{j}}]=\tilde{\alpha}_{p_{j}q_{j}}\tilde{x}_{j} with \tilde{\alpha}_{p_{j}q_{j}}\neq 0 . We now choose a particular pair of (\hat{p}_{j},\hat{q}_{j})\in\mathcal{M}^{2} that satisfies [\tilde{x}_{\hat{p}_{j}},\tilde{x}_{\hat{q}_{j}}]=\tilde{\alpha}_{\hat{p}_{j}\hat{q}_{j}}\tilde{x}_{j} with \tilde{\alpha}_{\hat{p}_{j}\hat{q}_{j}}\neq 0 . Then: \displaystyle z=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}}\lambda_{j}\frac{1}{\tilde{\alpha}_{\hat{p}_{j}\hat{q}_{j}}}[\tilde{x}_{\hat{p}_{j}},\tilde{x}_{\hat{q}_{j}}]=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}}\left[\frac{1}{\tilde{\alpha}_{\hat{p}_{j}\hat{q}_{j}}}\tilde{x}_{\hat{p}_{j}},\tilde{x}_{\hat{q}_{j}}\right], which implies that z\in\mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{g} . Hence, we conclude that \mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{g}=\spn\{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}\} . Given the recursive definition \mathcal{D}^{\ell+1}\mathfrak{g}=[\mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g},\mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g}] of the elements of the derived series, it follows immediately by means of induction that \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g}=\spn\{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\} . Consequently, one observes that \{\delta(j,k)\,\mid\,j,k\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell+1)}\cup\{0\} , since \mathfrak{g} is assumed to be graph-admissible, and \{\tilde{x}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} is an appropriate overcomplete basis satisfying the bracket relations: \displaystyle[\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{k}] \displaystyle=\tilde{\alpha}_{jk}\tilde{x}_{\delta(j,k)}\quad\text{ for all }j,k\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}, where \delta(j,k)=0 if \tilde{\alpha}_{jk}=0 and otherwise \delta(j,k)\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)} for all j,k\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)} . This confirms that every derived algebra is graph-admissible. The second claim follows analogously by replacing \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)} with \mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)} and noticing that the sets \{x_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\} are linearly independent as they are subsets of the basis \{x_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{N}} . ∎ Let us now denote the graph associated with the \ell -th derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} by \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E)\equiv G(\mathcal{D}^{\ell}V,\mathcal{D}^{\ell}E) , and investigate the relationship between \mathcal{D}^{\ell-1}G(V,E) and \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) . Constructing a graph associated with the \ell -th derived algebra is achieved by applying Algorithm 1 to the set \{x_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\} , where \{x_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{N}} is basis of \mathfrak{g} satisfying the bracket relations (7). A detailed implementation of this procedure is provided in Appendix B, specifically in Algorithm 6. In analyzing the graph \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) associated with the derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} , it becomes necessary to understand the conditions for an index j to belong to \mathcal{N}^{(\ell-1)}_{\mathrm{D}} but not to \mathcal{N}^{(\ell)}_{\mathrm{D}} . Consider therefore the definition of the sets \mathcal{N}^{(\ell)}_{\mathrm{D}} , provided in (16). If an index j belongs to \mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell-1)} but not to \mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)} , this implies that there exists no pair (p,q)\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell-1)}\times\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell-1)} such that [x_{p},x_{q}]=\kappa x_{j} with \kappa\in\mathbb{F}^{*} and j\in\mathcal{N} . In other words, the element x_{j} does not appear as a nontrivial Lie bracket of any two basis elements from the previous derived set. Translated into graph-theoretic terms, this condition implies that all vertices with no incoming edges must be removed when constructing the graph \mathcal{D}^{\ell+1}G(V,E) from \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) . Due to their importance, and to differentiate them from abelian elements, we introduce the following notation: Definition 54. A vertex e with no incoming edges but at least one outgoing edge is called loose end. To maintain the validity of the graph structure, it is mandatory to remove every edge in \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) that is labeled by one of the eliminated vertices. All other vertices and edges persist since their corresponding elements are evidently part of \mathcal{D}^{\ell+1}\mathfrak{g} . This “pruning” procedure is formalized into Algorithm 2. Algorithm 2 Algorithm for generating a labeled directed graph associated with the \ell -th derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} for a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} Data: A (possibly overcomplete) basis \mathcal{B}=\{\tilde{x}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} of the finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , satisfying the Lie bracket relations (8). Result: A sequence of labeled directed graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) associated with the derived algebras \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} of the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} for all \ell\geq 0 , constructed with respect to the given basis \mathcal{B} . 1 \mathcal{D} \leftarrow \emptyset /* Initialize the set containing all graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) associated with each derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} */ 2 V \leftarrow \mathcal{B} /* Initialize the vertex set for G(V,E) with the basis \mathcal{B} */ 3 E \leftarrow \emptyset /* Initialize the edge set for G(V,E) */ 4 foreach (j,k)\in\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M} with j<k do /* Add all relevant edges to edge set E */ 5 if [\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{k}]=\tilde{\alpha}_{jk}x_{\delta(j,k)} with \tilde{\alpha}_{jk}\neq 0 then 6 E \leftarrow E\cup(\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{k},\tilde{x}_{\delta(j,k)})\cup(\tilde{x}_{k},\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{\delta(j,k)}) /* Add edge to edge set E ; each edge is an orderd triple: (start vertex, edge-label, end vertex) */ 7 \mathcal{D} \leftarrow \{G(V,E)\} /* Add initial graph associated with \mathfrak{g}=\mathcal{D}^{0}\mathfrak{g} to \mathcal{D} */ 8 foreach \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1} do 9 foreach vertex v\in V do 10 if there exists no edge e\in E such that \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)=v then 11 V \leftarrow V\setminus\{v\} /* Remove v from V */ 12 foreach edge e\in E do 13 if v\in e then /* i.e., edge is of the form (\cdot,\cdot,v) or (\cdot,v,\cdot) or (v,\cdot,\cdot) */ 14 E \leftarrow E\setminus\{e\} /* Remove e from E */ 15 V^{(\ell)}\equiv\mathcal{D}^{\ell}V\leftarrow V 16 E^{(\ell)}\equiv\mathcal{D}^{\ell}E\leftarrow E 17 \mathcal{D} \leftarrow \mathcal{D}\cup\{\mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E)=G(V^{(\ell)},E^{(\ell)})\} /* Add updated graph \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) */ 18 return \mathcal{D} /* Return set of all graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) associated with the derived algebras \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} for all \ell\geq 0 */ Lemma 55. Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the labeled directed graph G(V,E) . Then, the sequence of graphs obtained via Algorithm 2 can be associated with the Lie algebras of the derived series of \mathfrak{g} . Proof. This claim follows directly from the preceding discussion, since the logic does not change when replacing the basis \{x_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{N}} —satisfying the bracket relations (7)—with an overcomplete basis \{\tilde{x}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} —satisfying the bracket relations (8). ∎ In order to highlight the working mechanisms of Algorithm 2, it is instructive to apply it to a specific example of a solvable minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra, as demonstrated in Example 56. Remark. It is important to note that this method is not restricted to minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras and minimal graphs. It also applies to redundant-graph-admissible Lie algebras and redundant graphs. In the case of redundant graphs, however, the set of vertices V is a set of linear dependent elements spanning the associated Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} . Consequently, when performing Algorithm 2 to compute \mathcal{D}^{\ell+1}G(V,E) starting from \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) , it is possible that certain vertices v_{\ell} with indices \ell\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)} are removed, even though the corresponding elements \tilde{x}_{\ell} belong to \mathcal{D}^{\ell+1}\mathfrak{g} . This occurs whenever \ell\notin\delta(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)},\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}) but \tilde{x}_{\ell} is linearly dependent on other elements \tilde{x}_{\delta(j,k)}\in\mathcal{D}^{\ell+1}V . If it is desirable to retain such elements in the graph, one can modify the Algorithm 2 to reintroduce these vertices back into the set \mathcal{D}^{\ell+1}V , along with all corresponding edges into \mathcal{D}^{\ell+1}E . This modification does not affect the correctness of the procedure, as the algorithm remains valid regardless of whether the initial basis is overcomplete or not. The modified version is presented in Appendix B as Algorithm 7. Example: Application of Algorithm 2 Example 56. To illustrate Algorithm 2 we apply it to the real Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , defined on the seven-dimensional vector space spanned by the basis \mathcal{B}=\{x_{0},\ldots,x_{6}\} whose elements satisfy the following Lie brackets: \displaystyle[x_{0},x_{1}] \displaystyle=x_{1},\; \displaystyle\;[x_{0},x_{j}] \displaystyle=-(7-j)x_{j}\quad\text{for }j\in\{2,3,4,5,6\},\; \displaystyle\;[x_{1},x_{j}] \displaystyle=x_{j+1}\quad\text{for }j\in\{2,3,4,5\}, which are the only non-vanishing ones. This Lie algebra is part of a family of solvable Lie algebras that can be faithfully realized within the skew-hermitian Weyl algebra \hat{A}_{1} , where the first derived algebra is non-abelian [87]. Applying Algorithm 2 to this algebra yields a sequence of directed graphs, each corresponding to a derived algebra in the series \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} . The graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) are constructed with respect to the basis \mathcal{B} and according to the pruning procedure of Algorithm 2. The respective graphs are depicted in Figure 10. Figure 10: Graphs associated with the derived series of the Lie algebra from Example 56. In panel (a): The initial graph \mathcal{D}^{0}G(V,E)\equiv G(V,E) associated with the full Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} . In panel (b): The graph after removing all loose ends and isolated vertices, along with the corresponding edges, which can be associated with the first derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{g} . In panel (c): The graph \mathcal{D}^{2}G(V,E) , and in panel (d): The graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) for \ell\geq 3 . We are now able to state the following fundamental results: Theorem 57. Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra, and let G(V,E) be any labeled directed graph associated with \mathfrak{g} . Then, \mathfrak{g} is solvable if and only if the series of derived graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) constructed by Algorithm 2 terminates after a finite number of steps. Before proceeding with the proof, we need to mention that the series of derived graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) is said to terminate, if for some \ell_{*} , the corresponding derived graph \mathcal{D}^{\ell_{*}}G(V,E) is trivial, i.e., both the vertex and edges sets are empty: \mathcal{D}^{\ell_{*}}V=\emptyset=\mathcal{D}^{\ell_{*}}E . We adopt furthermore the convention that the trivial graph induces the trivial Lie algebra: \langle\emptyset\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{}=\spn\{0\} . Proof. This follows immediately from Lemma 55 and the observation that each derived algebra satisfies \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g}=\langle\mathcal{D}^{\ell}V\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} , where \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E)=G(\mathcal{D}^{\ell}V,\mathcal{D}^{\ell}E) is the graph associated with the \ell -th derived algebra. The termination of the derived graph sequence corresponds precisely to the vanishing of the derived series, which characterizes solvability. ∎ We can further establish some graph-theoretic criteria for solvability that solely rely on the structure of the graph associated with the original Lie algebra, without needing to compute the explicit computation of its derived series. Proposition 58. Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the labeled directed graph G(V,E) . Then, \mathfrak{g} is non-solvable if G(V,E) contains a non-empty self-contained subgraph G_{C}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) that is induced by a cycle C 55 5 Note that every cycle is, by Definition 11, a directed walk. Thus a graph induced by a cycle is analogously defined as a graph induced by a directed walk (cf. Definition 12).. Proof. Let G(V,E) be a labeled directed graph associated with the finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , and suppose it contains a non-empty self-contained subgraph G_{C}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) induced by a cycle C . By definition, this means that G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) is a labeled directed graph, where every edge e\in\tilde{E} is labeled by a vertex v\in\tilde{V} . The set of vertices V spanning \mathfrak{g} is a possibly overcomplete, albeit finite, basis of \mathfrak{g} . We can therefore define V=\{v_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} . Without loss of generality, we may relabel the vertices such that \tilde{V}=\{v_{j}\}_{j=1}^{s} and C=(v_{1},e_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{s-1},e_{s-1},v_{s},e_{s},v_{1}) , where s\leq m=|\mathcal{M}| . Consequently, the Lie brackets of the subgraph elements satisfy: \displaystyle[v_{j},v_{k_{j}}] \displaystyle=\alpha_{jk_{j}}v_{j+1}\quad\text{for all }j\in\{1,\ldots,s-1\}\quad\text{and}\quad[v_{s},v_{k_{s}}]=\alpha_{sk_{s}}v_{1}, where k_{j}\in\{1,\ldots,s\} and \alpha_{jk_{j}}\in\mathbb{F}^{*} for all j\in\{1,\ldots,s\} . It is therefore clear that v_{j},v_{k_{j}}\in[\spn\{\tilde{V}\},\spn\{\tilde{V}\}] for all j\in\{1,\ldots,s\} , and hence \spn\{\tilde{V}\}\subseteq[\spn\{\tilde{V}\},\spn\{\tilde{V}\}] . It follows consequently that \spn\{\tilde{V}\} is contained in every derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{k}\mathfrak{g} for all k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} . Since \tilde{V}\neq\emptyset by assumption, the derived series of \mathfrak{g} does not terminate, making \mathfrak{g} non-solvable. Figure 11: Visualization of the Lie bracket relation [v_{j},v_{k_{j}}]\propto v_{j+1} , where v_{j},v_{j+1} are two consecutive vertices of a cycle C connected by the directed edge e_{j} . The vertex v_{k_{j}} is the labeling vertex of the edge e_{j} , i.e., \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{j})=v_{k_{j}} . ∎ Lemma 59. Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the labeled directed graph G(V,E) . If there exists a self-contained subgraph G_{C}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E})\subseteq G(V,E) induced by a directed cycle C , then \dim(\mathfrak{g})\geq 3 . Proof. Let G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) be a self-contained subgraph of G(V,E) that is induced by a directed cycle C , where G(V,E) is a labeled directed graph associated with the finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} . Without loss of generality, let \tilde{V}=\{v_{j}\}_{j=1}^{s}\subseteq V=\{v_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} , and suppose the brackets satisfy: [v_{1},v_{k_{1}}]=\alpha_{1k_{1}}v_{2}\neq 0 , …, [v_{s},v_{k_{s}}]=\alpha_{sk_{s}}v_{1}\neq 0 , where each k_{j}\in\{1,\ldots,s\} and \alpha_{jk_{j}}\in\mathbb{F}^{*} for all j\in\{1,\ldots,s-1\} . We now consider the following cases: • \boldsymbol{s=1.} This implies that the cycle consists of a single vertex with a self-loop, implying [v_{1},v_{1}]\propto v_{1}\neq 0 . This is clearly prohibited by the antisymmetric property of the Lie bracket. • \boldsymbol{s=2.} This implies, by the same logic as the previous case, [v_{1},v_{2}]=\alpha_{12}v_{2}\neq 0 , and [v_{2},v_{1}]=\alpha_{21}v_{1}\neq 0 . However, the antisymmetry of the Lie bracket implies [v_{2},v_{1}]=-[v_{1},v_{2}]=\alpha_{12}v_{2} , which contradicts v_{2}\not\propto v_{1} as demanded by Definition 16. • \boldsymbol{s=3.} Here we suppose that \{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\} is a linearly dependent set. Without loss of generality, let v_{1}=\beta_{2}v_{2}+\beta_{3}v_{3} with \beta_{2},\beta_{3}\in\mathbb{F}^{*} , since v_{j}\propto v_{k} if and only if j=k . One must now have [v_{1},v_{k_{1}}]=\alpha_{1k_{1}}v_{2} . Assume k_{1}=2 , which leaves [v_{2},v_{3}]=\alpha_{23}v_{3} and similarly [v_{3},v_{1}]=\alpha_{31}v_{1} as the sole valid options for k_{2} and k_{3} . However, [v_{3},v_{1}]=[v_{3},\beta_{2}v_{2}+\beta_{3}v_{3}]=-\beta_{2}\alpha_{23}v_{3}\neq\alpha_{31}v_{1} , a contradiction. The remaining case k_{1}=3 can be treated analogously and is similarly prohibited. Thus, \{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\} must be linearly independent. • \boldsymbol{s\geq 4.} This case, similar to the case s=3 , must be considered, if the graph is not minimal, and the vertices contained in \tilde{V} only span a one- or two-dimensional subspace. Suppose, therefore \dim(\tilde{V})<3 . If \dim(\spn\{\tilde{V}\})=1 , then all vertices are scalar multiples of one another: v_{1}=\beta_{2}v_{2}=\beta_{3}v_{3}=\ldots=\beta_{s}v_{s} for some \beta_{j}\in\mathbb{F}^{*} , which is forbidden by the construction of V , since v_{j}\propto v_{k} if and only if j=k . Hence, we are left with the case \dim(\spn{\tilde{V}})=2 , and we may write: v_{3}=\lambda_{13}v_{1}+\lambda_{23}v_{2} , …, v_{s}=\lambda_{1s}v_{1}+\lambda_{2s}v_{2} with \lambda_{1,j},\lambda_{2,j}\in\mathbb{F}^{*} for all j\in\{3,\ldots,s\} . Without loss of generality, consider the two subcases: (a) Suppose [v_{1},v_{2}]=\alpha_{12}v_{2}\neq 0 . Then: [v_{2},v_{1}]=-\alpha_{12}v_{2}\not\propto v_{3} . Hence, one must have [v_{2},v_{j}]=\alpha_{2j}v_{3} for some j\in\{3,\ldots,s\} . However, [v_{2},v_{j}]=[v_{2},\lambda_{1j}v_{1}+\lambda_{2j}v_{2}]=-\lambda_{1j}\alpha_{12}v_{2}\neq\alpha_{2j}v_{3} , which leads to a contradiction. (b) Suppose [v_{1},v_{3}]=\alpha_{23}v_{2}\neq 0 . Then: [v_{1},v_{3}]=[v_{1},\lambda_{13}v_{1}+\lambda_{23}v_{2}]=\lambda_{23}\alpha_{12}v_{2} . Thus, [v_{2},v_{1}]=-\alpha_{12}v_{2}/\lambda_{23}\not\propto v_{3} and [v_{2},v_{j}]=[v_{2},\lambda_{1j}v_{1}+\lambda_{2j}v_{2}]=-\alpha_{12}\lambda_{1j}v_{2}/\lambda_{23}\neq\alpha_{2j}v_{3} , which leads to a contradiction. Therefore, in all cases with \dim(\spn\{\tilde{V}\})<3 , the presence of a directed cycle has been ruled out. Thus, one needs to have \dim(\mathfrak{g})\geq\dim(\spn\{\tilde{V}\})\geq 3 . ∎ We can further generalize Proposition 58 by extending the criterion from subgraphs induced by a directed cycle to those induced by closed directed walks. We begin with an observation concerning graphs that do not contain self-contained subgraphs induced by closed directed walks, under the assumption that they are associated with a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra: Lemma 60. Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the labeled directed graph G(V,E) . Then, G(V,E) contains no self-contained subgraphs G_{W}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) induced by closed directed walks W if and only if, in every subgraph G_{W}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) that is induced by a closed directed walk W , there exists a vertex w\in\tilde{V} such that for all edges e\in E with \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=w , either the source \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\notin\tilde{V} or the label \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\notin\tilde{V} . While this lemma might appear self-evident at first glance, it is not trivial, as demonstrated by the example below. Remark: Discussion of Lemma 60 Example 61. Let G(V,E) be a labeled directed graph associated with a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} that does not contain any subgraphs that are self-contained and induced by a closed directed walk. Envision a situation in which the subgraph G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) , depicted in Figure 12, is a subgraph of G(V,E) that is induced by a closed directed walk W . In this case, at least one edge must be labeled by a vertex from V\setminus\tilde{V} . For illustration purposes, we assume here that two such edges exist and have colored them orange in the figure. Additionally, we can now suppose that for every vertex v in \tilde{V} , there exists an edge e\in E such that both \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\in V and \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\in V . These edges, which do not belong to the walk W , are colored blue in Figure 12. Figure 12: Depiction of a subgraph G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) that is induced by the closed directed walk W . The edges of W are colored black if the labels are vertices from \tilde{V} and orange if this is not the case. Edges that do not belong to the walk W , but to the larger graph G(V,E) , are colored blue. At first glance, it may appear that G(V,E) contains a different closed directed walk W^{\prime} that induces a self-contained subgraph G(\tilde{V}^{\prime},\tilde{E}^{\prime}) , as illustrated in Figure 13. However, upon closer inspection, some edges in this walk may be labeled by vertices that do not belong to \tilde{V}^{\prime} . This suggests that, even if no subgraph of G(V,E) is induced by a closed directed walk that is self-contained, in principle it might still be possible to encounter subgraphs where, for every vertex, there exists an edge e\in E such that the starting, labeling, and ending vertex of e belong to the subgraph. In the proof that follows, we show that such a configuration is not possible. Figure 13: Overlay of the subgraph G(\tilde{V}^{\prime},\tilde{E}^{\prime}) , induced by the closed directed walk W^{\prime} , on top of the subgraph G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E})\subseteq G({V},{E}) from Figure 12. The edges of G(\tilde{V}^{\prime},\tilde{E}^{\prime}) are colored black if the labels are vertices from \tilde{V}^{\prime} , and orange if this is not the case. Vertices and edges that do not belong to the walk W^{\prime} but to the walk W are colored gray. Proof. Suppose that in every subgraph subgraph G_{W}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) induced by a closed directed walk W there exists a vertex w\in\tilde{V} such that all edges e\in E with \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=w satisfy either \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\notin\tilde{V} or \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\notin\tilde{V} . Then, by Definition 13 no such subgraph G_{W} can be self-contained, since at least one edge of the induced subgraph G_{W} violates the condition that all components of the edge belong to \tilde{V} . Conversely, suppose that G(V,E) contains no self-contained subgraphs G_{W}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) induced by closed directed walks W . Let W=(w_{1},e_{1},w_{2},\ldots,e_{s},w_{1}) be a closed directed walk in G(V,E) that induces the subgraph G_{W}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) . Then, by assumption, there exists at least one edge e_{\ell}\in\tilde{E} such that \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{\ell})\notin\tilde{V} . We start now with the case that for all but one edge e_{\ell}\in\tilde{E} , one has \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{\ell})\in\tilde{V} . Without loss of generality, let e_{1} be this one edge in the walk W that is not labeled by a vertex from W . If there exists another edge e\in E\setminus\tilde{E} such that \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=w_{1}=\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{1}) , \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=w_{2}=\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{1}) , and \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\in\tilde{V} , the graph induced by the modified closed directed walk W^{\prime}=(w_{1},e,w_{2},e_{2},\ldots,e_{s},w_{1}) would be self-contained, against the hypothesis. Now suppose there exists an edge e^{\prime}\in E such that: \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e^{\prime}),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e^{\prime})\in\tilde{V} , \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e^{\prime})\neq w_{1}=\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{1}) , and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime})=w_{2} . Without loss of generality, we can write \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e^{\prime})=w_{p} and \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e^{\prime})=w_{q} , where 2<q<p\leq s , since, by Proposition 35, an edge e^{\prime\prime} belongs to E if and only if the edge (\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e^{\prime\prime}),\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e^{\prime\prime}),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime\prime})) also belongs to E . It follows that there exists the closed directed walk W_{1}:=(w_{2},e_{2},\ldots,w_{q},\ldots,w_{p},e^{\prime},w_{2}) in G(V,E) , where \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e^{\prime})=w_{q} is a vertex belonging to the induced graph G_{W_{1}}\equiv G(\tilde{V}_{1},\tilde{E}_{1}) . Note that \tilde{V}_{1}\subsetneq\tilde{V} , since every vertex from the walk W_{1} is a vertex from the walk W and w_{1}\notin\tilde{V}_{1} . If there exists an edge e_{j}\in\tilde{E}_{1} such that \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{j})\notin\tilde{V}_{1} , we know by Proposition 35 and the assumption that e_{1} is the only edge from \tilde{E} that is labeled by a vertex from V\setminus\tilde{V} , that there exists an edge e_{j}^{\prime}\in E such that \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{j}^{\prime})=\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{j}) , \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{j}^{\prime})=\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{j}) , and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{j}^{\prime})=\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{j}) . We can now collect all such edges e_{j}\in\tilde{E}_{1} for which \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{j})\in\tilde{V}\setminus\tilde{V}_{1} into the set S_{1} . That is: \displaystyle S_{1}:=\left\{e_{\ell}\in\tilde{E}_{1}\,\middle\mid\,\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{\ell})\notin\tilde{V}_{1}\right\}. Define the set \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(S_{1}):=\{\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{j})\,\mid\,e_{j}\in S_{1}\} , which is therefore comprised of vertices w_{\ell}\in\tilde{V}\setminus\tilde{V}_{1} . Next, we choose the vertex w_{\ell_{*}}\in\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(S_{1}) such that: \displaystyle\ell_{*} \displaystyle=\left\{\begin{matrix}1&,\text{ if }w_{1}\in\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(S_{1})\\ \max_{w_{\ell}\in\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(S_{1})}\{\ell\}&,\text{ otherwise}\end{matrix}\right.. Then, G(V,E) contains the closed directed walk: W_{2}:=(w_{2},e_{2},\ldots,w_{q},\ldots,w_{p},\ldots,w_{\ell_{*}},e_{j}^{\prime}w_{j+1},\ldots,w_{p},e^{\prime},w_{2}), which induces the subgraph G_{W_{2}}\equiv G(\tilde{V}_{2},\tilde{E}_{2}) . By construction, all edges in \tilde{E}_{1} belong to \tilde{E}_{2} and are labeled by vertices from \tilde{V}_{2} . Similarly, all edges in \tilde{E}_{2}\setminus\tilde{E} , are labeled by vertices from \tilde{V_{2}} . Thus, only edges from \tilde{E}_{2}\setminus(\tilde{E}_{1}\cup\tilde{E}) may be labeled by vertices from \tilde{V}\setminus\tilde{V}_{2} . If this is not the case, then G(V,E) would contain a self-contained subgraph that is induced by a closed directed walk, which contradicts the initial assumption. We can therefore repeat this procedure and observe that \tilde{V}_{j}\subsetneq\tilde{V}_{j+1}\subseteq\tilde{V} , as one adds at least one vertex v\in\tilde{V}\setminus\tilde{V}_{j} to obtain \tilde{V}_{j+1} . Nevertheless, since the set \tilde{V} is finite, this procedure must eventually (after a finite number of steps) terminate in a self-contained subgraph of G(V,E) that is induced by a closed directed walk, which is prohibited. The idea of this iterative construction is illustrated in Figure 14.
