Algorithm 5 Algorithm for generating a labeled directed graph associated with an \mathcal{M} -magma-graded Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} Data: An abelian magma (\mathcal{M},\delta) and a Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} such that \mathfrak{g} is \mathcal{M} -magma-graded with \mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{M}}\mathfrak{g}_{j} and [\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{k}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{\delta(j,k)} Result: A labeled directed graph G(V,E) associated with the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , constructed with respect to the magma \mathcal{M} . 1 V \leftarrow \{\mathfrak{g}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} /* Initialize the vertex set with the subspaces \mathfrak{g}_{j} */ 2 E \leftarrow \emptyset /* Initialize an empty edge set */ 3 foreach (j,k)\in\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M} with j\leq k do /* Add all relevant edges to edge set E */ 4 if [\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{k}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{\delta(j,k)} and [\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{k}]\neq\{0\} then 5 E \leftarrow E\cup\{(\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{k},\mathfrak{g}_{\delta(j,k)})\} 6 E \leftarrow E\cup\{(\mathfrak{g}_{k},\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{\delta(j,k)})\} /* Add directed edge to edge set E ; each edge is an ordered triple: (start vertex, edge-label, end vertex) */ 7 return G(V,E) /* Return labeled directed graph G(V,E) associated with \mathfrak{g} */ It is now straightforward to observe the following structural correspondence: Proposition 98. Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra, and let \mathcal{B}=\{e_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{N}} be a basis satisfying the relations (7). Then, there exists an abelian magma (\mathcal{M},\delta) such that \mathfrak{g} is \mathcal{M} -magma-graded with \mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{M}}\mathfrak{g}_{j} , [\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{k}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{\delta(j,k)} , and \mathfrak{g}_{j}=\spn\{e_{j}\} . Moreover, there exists a bijection \Phi that maps the graph G(V,E) , obtained by applying Algorithm 1 to the basis \mathcal{B} , to the graph G^{\prime}(V^{\prime},E^{\prime}) , obtained by applying Algorithm 5 to \mathfrak{g} and \mathcal{M} , by identifying the labels e_{j} with the corresponding subspaces \mathfrak{g}_{j} . This result can be shown analogously to Lemma 96, by modifying the \delta -map dictated by the relations (7), following the same construction outlined in the proof of Lemma 96. The remainder of the claim follows from the preceding discussion. A similar argument yields the following result: Proposition 99. Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional minimal-magma-graded Lie algebra, and let (\mathcal{M},\delta) be an abelian magma such that \mathfrak{g} is \mathcal{M} -magma-graded with \mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{M}}\mathfrak{g}_{j} and [\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{k}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{\delta(j,k)} . Then, there exists a basis \mathcal{B}=\{e_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} of \mathfrak{g} satisfying the relations (7). Moreover, there exists a bijection \Psi that maps the graph G^{\prime}(V^{\prime},E^{\prime}) , obtained by applying Algorithm 5 to \mathfrak{g} and \mathcal{M} , to the graph G(V,E) , obtained by applying Algorithm 1 to the basis \mathcal{B} , by identifying the labels \mathfrak{g}_{j} with the corresponding basis elements e_{j} . These two propositions demonstrate that the results established for minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras can be extended to finite-dimensional minimal-magma-graded Lie algebras by simply replacing the associated graphs constructed via Algorithm 1 with those obtained using Algorithm 5. An important observation is that Algorithm 5 is not restricted to abelian magmas \mathcal{M} and \mathcal{M} -magma-graded Lie algebras \mathfrak{g} satisfying |\mathcal{M}|=\dim(\mathfrak{g}) . It applies to any magma for which a given Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} admits an \mathcal{M} -magma-gradation. For instance, every Lie algebra is trivially \{0\} -magma-graded, where \{0\} denotes the trivial magma. In this case, the associated graph consists of a single vertex labeled by the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} itself, and if the Lie algebra is non-abelian, a single directed edge labeled by \mathfrak{g} that points from the vertex to itself. Here, a modification of the original Algorithm 1, which has not been discussed in detail, is essential. In the original algorithm, only pairs of basis elements (e_{j},e_{k}) with j<k were considered, due to the antisymmetry of the Lie bracket, since [e_{j},e_{k}]=-[e_{k},e_{j}] and [e_{j},e_{j}]=0 . However, for subspaces, the bracket is symmetric by definition, i.e., [\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{k}]=[\mathfrak{g}_{k},\mathfrak{g}_{j}] , and the bracket of two identical subspaces does not generally vanish [111]. Therefore, in the step of the algorithm, where the edges are added to the graph (cf. lines 3-6), the modified Algorithm 5 must consider pairs of subspaces (\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{k}) with j\leq k , not n<k . While this construction is formally valid, it provides no meaningful visual insight into the internal structure of \mathfrak{g} , except for distinguishing between abelian and non-abelian Lie algebras. It is evident that the increasing the size of \mathcal{M} generally enhances the level of detailed captured by the associated graph. This motivates the following definition: Definition 100 (granularity). Let \mathfrak{g} be a Lie algebra and (\mathcal{M},\delta) an abelian magma such that \mathfrak{g} is \mathcal{M} -magma-graded. We define the granularity of the \mathcal{M} -magma gradation of \mathfrak{g} as the cardinality of \mathcal{M} , and denote it by \operatorname{gra}(\mathfrak{g},\mathcal{M}) , i.e., \operatorname{gra}(\mathfrak{g},\mathcal{M}):=|\mathcal{M}| . Furthermore, we define largest possible granularity of an \mathcal{M} -magma gradation of \mathfrak{g} as the finest granularity of \mathfrak{g} , and denote it with the symbol \operatorname{fg}(\mathfrak{g}) . For a finite-dimensional Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , this is given by: \displaystyle\operatorname{fg}(\mathfrak{g}):=\max\left\{\operatorname{gra}(\mathfrak{g},\mathcal{M})\,\middle\mid\,\mathfrak{g}\text{ is $\mathcal{M}$-magma-graded}\right\}. (27) It is noteworthy that the concept of granularity is closely related to the notion of fine gradings, which are defined via refinements of a given grading [70]. Specifically, let \mathcal{M} be a \mathcal{M} -magma-gradation of the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} with \mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{M}}\mathfrak{g}_{j} . Then, an \mathcal{M}^{\prime} -magma-gradation of \mathfrak{g} with \mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}\mathfrak{g}_{j}^{\prime} is called a proper refinement if, for every k\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime} , there exists a j\in\mathcal{M} such that \mathfrak{g}_{k}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{j} , and the inclusion is proper for at least one k\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime} . An \mathcal{M} -magma-grading is called fine if no proper refinements exists [70]. It is easy to convince oneself that an \mathcal{M} -magma-gradation of a finite-dimensional Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} satisfying \operatorname{fg}(\mathfrak{g})=\operatorname{gra}(\mathfrak{g},\mathcal{M}) is a fine grading in the sense defined above. This establishes a concrete link between the concept of minimal-magma-grading, finest granularity, and fine grading. Lemma 101. Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional Lie algebra. Then: (a) 1\leq\operatorname{fg}(\mathfrak{g})\leq\dim(\mathfrak{g}) ; (b) \operatorname{fg}(\mathfrak{g})=\dim(\mathfrak{g}) if and only if \mathfrak{g} is minimal-graph-admissible. We omit the proof of this statement, as it is straightforward to verify and leave it to the interested reader. This shows that non-graph-admissible Lie algebras, as well as graph-admissible Lie algebras that are only redundant-graph-admissible Lie algebras, always satisfy \operatorname{fg}(\mathfrak{g})<\dim(\mathfrak{g}) . We will now demonstrate that for certain Lie algebras satisfying \operatorname{fg}(\mathfrak{g})<\dim(\mathfrak{g}) , there exists an abelian magma (\mathcal{M},\delta) such that \mathfrak{g} is \mathcal{M} -magma-graded and the granularity is sufficiently fine so that the corresponding graph obtained by Algorithm 5 can resolve all necessary structural details and is enabling us, for instance, to determine whether the Lie algebra is solvable, among other properties. For this, see Example 102. Example: Graphs associated with non-graph-admissible Lie algebras Example 102. To establish the existence of non-graph-admissible Lie algebras and graph-admissible Lie algebras that are only redundant-graph-admissible Lie algebras, i.e., those Lie algebras with \operatorname{fg}(\mathfrak{g})<\dim(\mathfrak{g}) , we previously considered the family L_{\alpha}^{3} of real three-dimensional Lie algebras [56] (cf. Theorem 17 and Lemma 25). Here, \alpha is a real parameter, and the Lie algebra L_{\alpha}^{3} is spanned by the three elements \{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\} that satisfy the Lie brackets: \displaystyle[e_{1},e_{2}] \displaystyle=0,\; \displaystyle\;[e_{1},e_{3}] \displaystyle=-e_{2},\; \displaystyle\;[e_{2},e_{3}] \displaystyle=-\alpha e_{1}-e_{2}. In section II we have shown that for \alpha\leq-1/4 the Lie algebras L_{\alpha}^{3} are not minimal-graph-admissible, and for certain values of \alpha even non-graph-admissible. For this example, we want to focus on the case \alpha\leq-1/4 . It follows immediately that \operatorname{fg}(L_{\alpha}^{3})<3 . We can now choose the subspaces \mathfrak{g}_{1}:=\spn\{e_{1},e_{2}\} and \mathfrak{g}_{2}:=\spn\{e_{3}\} . It is straightforward to verify that \displaystyle\{0\}=[\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{1}] \displaystyle\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{2},\; \displaystyle\;[\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2}] \displaystyle=\mathfrak{g}_{1},\; \displaystyle\;[\mathfrak{g}_{2},\mathfrak{g}_{1}] \displaystyle=\mathfrak{g}_{1},\; \displaystyle\;\{0\}\neq[\mathfrak{g}_{2},\mathfrak{g}_{2}] \displaystyle\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{1}. Thus, L_{\alpha}^{3} admits an \mathcal{M} -magma-gradation, where \mathcal{M}=\{1,2\} and the binary operation \delta:\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M} is given by \delta(1,2)=\delta(2,1)=\delta(2,2)=1 and \delta(1,1)=1 . In Appendix E, we show furthermore that every \mathcal{M} -magma-gradation of L_{\alpha}^{3} with \operatorname{gra}(L_{\alpha}^{3},\mathcal{M})=\operatorname{fg}(L_{\alpha}^{3}) must be equivalent to this grading; see Propositions 117 and 118. The labeled directed graph G(V,E) constructed by Algorithm 5 is consequently: This graph can now be analyzed using the central results of this work, as if it were obtained via Algorithm 1. We find that: (i) Theorem 62 would indicate that the Lie algebra L_{\alpha}^{3} is solvable, since the graph G(V,E) contains no self-contained subgraph that is induced by a closed directed walk. (ii) Lemma 55 together with an appropriately modified Algorithm 2, would imply that the derived algebras of L_{\alpha}^{3} is: \mathcal{D}^{0}L_{\alpha}^{3}=L_{\alpha}^{3} , \mathcal{D}^{1}L_{\alpha}^{3}=\mathfrak{g}_{1} , and \mathcal{D}^{\ell}L_{\alpha}^{3}=\{0\} for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2} . The modification of Algorithm 2 parallels the adaption from Algorithm 1 to Algorithm 5, replacing vertex and edge labels from individual elements to subspaces. The modified Algorithm 8 (cf. Appendix B) generates the following derived graphs: (iii) Theorem 68 would imply that L_{\alpha}^{3} is not nilpotent, since G(V,E) contains a closed directed walk. (iv) Lemma 67 would imply that the lower central series of L_{\alpha}^{3} is given by \mathcal{C}^{0}L_{\alpha}^{3}=L_{\alpha}^{3} and \mathcal{C}^{\ell}L_{\alpha}^{3}=\mathfrak{g}_{1} for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1} . Here, as before, Algorithm 3 must be modified to account for the current setting. The modified Algorithm 9 is presented in Appendix B and generates the following sequence of graphs: (v) Lemma 74 would imply that the subspaces \mathfrak{g}_{1} and \mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2} are ideals of L_{\alpha}^{3} . (vi) Proposition 87 would imply that L_{\alpha}^{3} is not simple, as G(V,E) contains no closed directed walk that induces a subgraph G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) with V=\tilde{V} . (vii) Conjecture 92, if correct, would imply that L_{\alpha}^{3} is not semisimple, since the vertex labeled with the subspace \mathfrak{g}_{2} is never part of a closed directed walk that induces a self-contained subgraph. It is straightforward to verify that all of these claims hold. However, we do not claim that these hold in general, and one may conjecture that the results stated above will require minor modifications to ensure applicability in the current generalized setting. This example raises the interesting question: Q: Can the concept of magma-gradations be used to generalize the approach of associating labeled directed graphs with Lie algebras, in such a way that the structural properties of the Lie algebras are represented faithfully, without being restricted to graph-admissible or even minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras, but instead encompassing arbitrary Lie algebras? A clear prerequisite for this generalization is that the magma-gradation possesses sufficiently fine granularity. Based on this observation, one may conjecture the following: Conjecture 103. Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional Lie algebra. Then, there exists an abelian magma (\mathcal{M},\delta) such that \mathfrak{g} is \mathcal{M} -magma-graded and the graph G_{\mathfrak{g}}(V,E) associated with the pair (\mathfrak{g},(\mathcal{M},\delta)) , constructed via Algorithm 5, faithfully represents the inner structure of \mathfrak{g} , in the sense that the following assertions hold: (i) Solvability. The Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} is non-solvable if and only if G_{\mathfrak{g}}(V,E) contains a self-contained subgraph G_{W}\equiv G_{W}(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) that is induced by a closed directed walk W . (ii) Derived Series. The sequence of graphs obtained via Algorithm 8 can be associated with the Lie algebras of the derived series of \mathfrak{g} , in the sense that for every \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , the direct sum of the subspaces labeling the vertices of the graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G_{\mathfrak{g}}(V,E) coincide with derived algebras \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} . (iii) Nilpotency. The Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} is nilpotent if and only if G(V,E) contains no closed directed walk. (iv) Lower central series. The sequence of graphs obtained via Algorithm 9 can be associated with the lower central series of \mathfrak{g} , in the sense that for every \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} the direct sum of the subspaces labeling the vertices of the graphs \mathcal{C}^{\ell}G_{\mathfrak{g}}(V,E) coincide with Lie algebras \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} of the lower central series. (v) Ideals. If W\subseteq V is a subset that satisfies the ideal-graph-property, then the direct sum of the subspaces labeling the vertices in W span an ideal of \mathfrak{g} . (vi) Simplicity. If \mathfrak{g} is simple, then there exists a closed directed walk W that induces a subgraph G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) with V=\tilde{V} . (vii) Semisimplicity. If \mathfrak{g} is semisimple, then every vertex is part of a closed directed walk that induces a self-contained subgraph. There are important subtleties differentiating the modified approach from the original one. For instance, it is possible for an \mathcal{M} -magma-gradation to satisfy \{0\}\neq[\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{k}]\subsetneq\mathfrak{g}_{\delta(j,k)} for at least one pair (j,k)\in\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M} . Such cases must be carefully accounted for when interpreting the structure of the associated graph. Nevertheless, we can demonstrate that Conjecture 103 holds in certain settings, particularly in the low-dimensional case. This is shown in Proposition 113 (cf. Appendix E), where we consider every real Lie algebra of dimension four or less and construct suitable magma-gradations for each. Let us now turn our attention to complex semisimple Lie algebras. Any such Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} can be expressed as the direct sum of simple ideals: \mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus\mathfrak{g}_{j} , where each \mathfrak{g}_{j} is a simple ideal [111]. Moreover, every simple Lie algebra admits a fine group grading induced by a corresponding root system [71]. It is straightforward to verify that such gradings yield associated graphs that faithfully represent the structure of the Lie algebra in the sense of Conjecture 103. Let us consider the following Example 104: Example: Lie algebras with root-system-grading In this example, we explore the connection of a graph obtained via Algorithm 5 and the root space decomposition of a simple Lie algebra, when the gradation is provided by its corresponding root system. Example 104. Let us consider the complex special linear Lie algebra \mathfrak{sl}({3},{\mathbb{C}}) . This algebra admits the basis \{e_{11}-e_{22},e_{22}-e_{33},e_{12},e_{21},e_{13},e_{31},e_{23},e_{32}\} , where the non-trivial brackets are determined by [e_{jk},e_{\ell m}]=\delta_{k\ell}e_{jm}-\delta_{jm}e_{\ell k} [84]. In particular, for any diagonal element \lambda_{1}e_{11}+\lambda_{2}e_{22}+\lambda_{3}e_{33}\in\mathfrak{sl}({3},{\mathbb{C}}) , one has [\lambda_{1}e_{11}+\lambda_{2}e_{22}+\lambda_{3}e_{33},e_{jk}]=(\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k})e_{jk} for j,k\in\{1,2,3\} with j\neq k . This structure yields the root system of \mathfrak{sl}({3},{\mathbb{C}}) taken over the euclidean plane, denoted A_{2}:=\{\pm\alpha,\pm\beta,\pm(\alpha+\beta)\} , where \alpha:=(1,0)^{\mathrm{Tp}} , and \beta:=-(\sqrt{3},1)^{\mathrm{Tp}}/2 , see [84]. We define a G -gradation of \mathfrak{sl}({3},{\mathbb{C}}) , based on the root space A_{2} , via the following subspaces: \displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{0} \displaystyle:=\spn\{e_{11}-e_{22},e_{22}-e_{33}\}\, \displaystyle\;\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha} \displaystyle:=\spn\{e_{12}\},\; \displaystyle\;\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha} \displaystyle:=\spn\{e_{21}\},\; \displaystyle\;\mathfrak{g}_{\beta} \displaystyle:=\spn\{e_{31}\}, \displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{-\beta} \displaystyle:=\spn\{e_{13}\},\; \displaystyle\;\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha+\beta} \displaystyle:=\spn\{e_{32}\},\; \displaystyle\;\mathfrak{g}_{-(\alpha+\beta)} \displaystyle:=\spn\{e_{32}\}. These subsets clearly satisfy: [\mathfrak{g}_{\theta},\mathfrak{g}_{\varphi}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{\delta(\theta,\varphi)}\supseteq[\mathfrak{g}_{\varphi},\mathfrak{g}_{\theta}] for all \theta,\varphi\in A_{2}\cup\{0\} , where \delta is the usual binary group operation on roots. For more details, we refer to Table 1 (cf. Appendix F). We can now plot the associated graph G(V,E) obtained via Algorithm 5 and the root system A_{2} in a side-by-side comparison (see Figure 25). Figure 25: Depiction of the labeled directed graph associated with the simple Lie algebra \mathfrak{sl}({3},{\mathbb{C}}) obtained in Example 104, using the grading provided by its root system A_{2} , in panel (a), alongside the root system A_{2} shown in panel (b). It is immediately evident that a structural correspondence exists. However, to fully capture the relationship, we must distinguish between the cases, where [\mathfrak{g}_{\theta},\mathfrak{g}_{\varphi}]=\{0\} and those where \{0\}\neq[\mathfrak{g}_{\theta},\mathfrak{g}_{\varphi}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{0} . To reflect this distinction, we introduce a refinement of the zero element in the magma (A_{2}\cup\{0\},\delta) by splitting it into two distinct elements, denoted 0 and \bar{0} respectively. These are defined in a way that one has \delta(\varphi,\theta)=0 when \{0\}\neq[\mathfrak{g}_{\theta},\mathfrak{g}_{\varphi}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{0} and \delta(\varphi,\theta)=\bar{0} when [\mathfrak{g}_{\theta},\mathfrak{g}_{\varphi}]=\{0\} . The corresponding composition Table 2 is provided in Appendix F. This refinement allows us to interpret the graph G(V,E) as a direct visualization of the root system’s composition table modulo the \bar{0} elements. Note that this generalization, similar to the original approach, lacks uniqueness. Consequently, considering a single associated graph does not suffice to distinguish between a simple and a semisimple but non-simple Lie algebra. To achieve this, one must investigate all possible associated graphs, since any semisimple Lie algebra always admits a graph that decomposes into multiple unconnected components that can be associated with its simple ideals, whereas a graph associated with a simple Lie algebra cannot be decomposed further. Finally, we state a stronger version of Conjecture 103, which assumes that every graph generated by a magma-gradation with the finest granularity faithfully represents the structure of the Lie algebra. To be precise: Conjecture 105. Let \mathfrak{g} be a complex finite-dimensional Lie algebra and (\mathcal{M},\delta) an abelian magma such that \mathfrak{g} is \mathcal{M} -magma-graded, where the \mathcal{M} -gradation has the finest granularity. Then, the graph G_{\mathfrak{g}}(V,E) associated to the pair (\mathfrak{g},(\mathcal{M},\delta)) , obtained via Algorithm 5, faithfully represents the internal structure of \mathfrak{g} , in the sense that the following assertions hold: (i) Solvability. The Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} is non-solvable if and only if G_{\mathfrak{g}}(V,E) contains a self-contained subgraph G_{W}\equiv G_{W}(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) that is induced by a closed directed walk W . (ii) Derived Series. The sequence of graphs obtained via Algorithm 8 can be associated with the Lie algebras of the derived series of \mathfrak{g} , in the sense that for every \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , the direct sum of the subspaces labeling the vertices of the graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G_{\mathfrak{g}}(V,E) coincide with derived algebras \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} . (iii) Nilpotency. The Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} is nilpotent if and only if G(V,E) contains no closed directed walk. (iv) Lower central series. The sequence of graphs obtained via Algorithm 9 can be associated with the lower central series of \mathfrak{g} , in the sense that for every \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} the direct sum of the subspaces labeling the vertices of the graphs \mathcal{C}^{\ell}G_{\mathfrak{g}}(V,E) coincide with Lie algebras \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} of the lower central series. (v) Ideals. If W\subseteq V is a subset that satisfies the ideal-graph-property, then the direct sum of the subspaces labeling the vertices in W span an ideal of \mathfrak{g} . (vi) Simplicity. If \mathfrak{g} is simple, then there exists a closed directed walk W that induces a subgraph G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) with V=\tilde{V} . (vii) Semisimplicity. If \mathfrak{g} is semisimple, then every vertex is part of a closed directed walk that induces a self-contained subgraph. This conjecture can be verified in the low-dimensional case, see for example Proposition 119 in Appendix E, where we discuss the real Lie algebra L_{\alpha}^{3} . VI.5 Further remarks Finally, we want to devote this last section to a broader a reflection and prospective developments stemming from our study. Beyond the specific results established in the preceding sections, the framework of associating labeled directed graphs with Lie algebras opens several avenues for further exploration. These include both theoretical questions and potential applications, particularly in the classification of Lie algebras and their structural invariants. We now present a selection of open problems and conjectures that naturally arise from our findings. VI.5.1 Open questions and conjectures We begin by briefly discussing several open questions and potential directions for future research. These are presented in a logical sequence that mirrors the progression of the main text. We started by defining and investigating the notion of graph-admissibility with particular emphasis on the subclass of minimal-graph-admissibile Lie algebras. Among the general results established, which include the existence Theorems 17 and 24, we showed that every finite-dimensional nilpotent Lie algebra is graph-admissible. This naturally raises the following questions: Q: Is every nilpotent Lie algebra minimal-graph-admissible? If not, what are the structural differences between those that are minimal-graph-admissible and those that are not? To approach this question, let us consider the subclass of two-step nilpotent Lie algebras, i.e., Lie algebras \mathfrak{g} that satisfy [\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]\neq\{0\} and [\mathfrak{g},[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]]=\{0\} . Engel’s Theorem [94] implies that \operatorname{ad}_{x}^{2}=0 for all x\in\mathfrak{g} . Generally, one has even \operatorname{ad}_{x}\circ\operatorname{ad}_{y}=\operatorname{ad}_{y}\circ\operatorname{ad}_{y}=0 for all x,y\in\mathfrak{g} . This is because \operatorname{ad}_{x}(z)\in[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]\subseteq\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) for all x,z\in\mathfrak{g} , and consequently (\operatorname{ad}_{x}\circ\operatorname{ad}_{y})(z)=0 for all x,y,z\in\mathfrak{g} . Henceforth, the adjoint actions of any pair of basis elements commute. Motivated by the ability to construct a similarity transformation that brings a set of commuting normal matrices into their diagonalized form, one can conclude the following: if it were possible to construct a simultaneous similarity transformation that brings all adjoint operations into their respective Jordan normal forms, it would follow that every two-step nilpotent Lie algebra is minimal-graph-admissible. In fact, for certain pairs of commuting nilpotent endomorphisms, the existence of such a similarity transformation has already been established [92]. However, in the case of three-step nilpotent Lie algebras, it is generally no longer true that all adjoint actions commute. This observations motivates the following conjecture: Conjecture 106. Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional k -step nilpotent Lie algebra. Then, the following holds: (a) If k\leq 2 , then \mathfrak{g} is minimal-graph-admissible; (b) If k>2 , then \mathfrak{g} is not necessarily minimal-graph-admissible. In fact, there exists k -step nilpotent Lie algebras that fail to be minimal-graph-admissible. The focus on nilpotent Lie algebras further connects this work to ongoing efforts aimed at investigating such algebras through alternative graph-based approaches, as mentioned in the introduction (see for example, [129, 55]). A key difference between our framework and that in [129] lies in the interpretation of the Lie bracket. In [129], the bracket [x_{1},x_{2}]=x_{3} is represented by an edge originating from x_{1} , pointing towards x_{2} and being labeled by x_{3} . This encoding captures the sign if the bracket through the edge’s direction, since by the antisymmetry of the Lie bracket [x_{2},x_{1}]=-x_{3} . However, this approach enforces an even more restrictive condition on the bases elements, compared to the relations (7) or (8). Specifically, the Lie bracket of two basis elements can only yield one of three outcomes: either a single basis element, its additive inverse, or zero. This limitation excludes certain Lie algebras, for instance real Lie algebras that inherently involve rational coefficients. One notable example is the Lie algebra \mathfrak{N}(q) , first introduced in [152]. This real Lie algebra is spanned by the six basis elements \{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},y_{1},y_{2}\} , which satisfy the follwing nontrivial Lie bracket relations: \displaystyle[x_{1},x_{2}] \displaystyle=y_{1},\; \displaystyle\;[x_{1},x_{3}] \displaystyle=y_{2},\; \displaystyle\;[x_{2},x_{4}] \displaystyle=y_{2},\; \displaystyle\;[x_{3},x_{4}] \displaystyle=qy_{1}, where q\in\mathbb{Q} . All other brackets either vanish or follow from the antisymmetric property of the Lie bracket. Using the current framework, the associated graph for \mathfrak{N}(q) , according to our prescription, is given by: These two approaches, the one presented in this work and the one discussed in [129], share a notable feature: a single Lie algebra can, in general, be represented by multiple distinct graphs, which is demonstrated by Example 2.3 in [129]. Another interesting research direction emerges when considering Theorem 62, which provides sufficient and necessary criteria for solvability by analyzing closed directed walks within the associated graph. The only requirement imposed on these walks is that they have to be self-contained. It might therefore be worthwhile to improve the efficiency of this criterion, which is especially relevant for computer-based implementations, by further restricting the set of admissible walks. For instance, one could aim to establish conditions on the maximum length of such walks or determine how frequently a vertex must be revisited. These refinements would significantly reduce the number of walks that need to be considered, making the criterion computationally more traceable. Inspiration for such an investigation can certainly be drawn from the field of computer science, where graph-based methods are widely adopted [60]. One idea could be to incorporate Dijkstra’s algorithm to determine the shortest path between two vertices [63]. Such techniques might help identify minimal or optimal walks relevant to solvability. Currently, the number of possible walks is infinite, which poses practical challenges. Restricting this to a finite set would greatly simplify verification, as infinite loops do not contribute meaningfully to the analysis. This motivates the following question: Q: Is Theorem 62 still valid if one replaces “closed directed walk” with “closed directed trail”? If not, what are the structural differences in the associated Lie algebra? As emphasized earlier, the current framework does not guarantee uniqueness of the graphs associated with a given Lie algebra, and vice versa. This observation opens several intriguing questions. For instance, what is the relationship between Lie algebras that share an associated graph, particularly when considering minimal graphs? Conversely, what are the relationship between different graphs associated with the same Lie algebra? Furthermore, one could explore connections between Lie algebras of different dimensions that share the same graph, i.e., in cases where at least one graph is not minimal. Similarly, it would be interesting to investigate the interplay between minimal and redundant graphs associated with the same Lie algebra, and to examine how such relationships extend to other Lie algebras which admit these redundant graphs as minimal ones. These, as well as related questions point toward a broader structural theory that classifies Lie algebras not only by their intrinsic properties but also by the equivalence classes of graphs they admit. Such an approach might offer valuable insight into the investigation of Lie algebras. A related and equally promising direction is to ask whether this graph-based approach can be used to explore additional structural properties beyond those discussed in the preceding sections. In particular, one might consider the following question: Q: Does a relation exist between the nullity of a Lie algebra and the associated graphs? Recall, that the nullity of a Lie algebra is defined as the minimal number of elements necessary to generate a given Lie algebra under the Lie closure [87]. Exploring this connection could uncover specific connectivity conditions for the associated graphs, among other results. Such findings might include existence theorems for particular graph configurations and upper bounds on the nullity, thereby linking this framework to other research on the nullity of Lie algebras [112, 113, 151, 6]. Another potential direction relates directly to the question of whether the proposed framework can be useful for classifying Lie algebras, a topic we address in the following part. VI.5.2 Contributing to the Lie algebra classification effort When working with algebraic structures, a natural question to raise is whether a classification od such structures can be achieved. A prominent example of such an endeavor is the classification of all finite simple groups [13]. For Lie algebras, however, a full classification is not yet known. Nevertheless, the classification of every simple and therefore every semisimple Lie algebra over an algebraically closed field is well established [45, 16, 17, 18, 19]. Building on these foundational results, significant progress has been made [28, 119, 124] and current approaches are informed by the Levi-Mal’tsev theorem, which states that any complex Lie algebra can be decomposed into the semidirect sum of a semisimple component and its radical [115]. Despite this, major challenges remain, particularly in classifying all solvable Lie algebras and understanding the structure of the semidirect sum [140]. Contemporary research primarily focuses on low-dimensional solvable Lie algebras [143, 59, 57, 8, 48]. The framework introduced in this work can be connected to these efforts by attempting to classify all graph-admissible Lie algebras together with the set of graphs that represent them. This is especially interesting for minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras and their associated minimal graphs, as these present a faithfully representation and are, so far, better understood. Such a classification effort has the potential to complement and aid the broader goal of classifying all Lie algebras. Consequently, developing a systematic understanding of graph-admissible Lie algebras could yield valuable insights. One promising avenue would be to classify all labeled directed graphs that can serve as minimal graphs for minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras. This approach could reveal structural invariants and provide a combinatorial perspective on the classification problem. In a first step, one could attempt to determine the number of inequivalent and valid graphs with n vertices that can be associated with a minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra of dimension n as a minimal graph. Let us denote this number by S(n) . Calculating S(n) can be refined by introducing the number S(n,m) , which counts such graphs when the number of edges is fixed to m . Clearly, S(n)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}S(n,m) . Moreover, by Propositions 35 and 37, one has: \displaystyle S(n)=\sum_{m=0}^{n(n-1)/2}S(n,2m), since the number of edges must be even, due to the antisymmetry of the Lie bracket, and is bounded from above by n(n-1) . Thus, S(n,2k+1)=0 for all k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} . Additionally, one has S(n,0)=1 for all n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1} , as there exists only one abelian Lie algebra of dimension n . It is straightforward to verify that S(1)=1=S(1,0) , while S(2)=2 with S(2,0)=1 , and S(2,2)=1 . Furthermore, as shown in Theorem 38 and Corollaries 41 and 43, one has: S(3,0)=1 , S(3,2)=2 , S(3,4)=4 , and S(3,6)=3 , while S(3,m)=0 for all other m\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}\setminus\{0,2,4,6\} . Consequently, the number of inequivalent graphs that can be associated with a minimal-graph-admissible three-dimensional Lie algebra as a minimal graph is: S(3)=10 . On the contrary, one can also ask: Q: What is the number of graphs that can be associated with a particular Lie algebra, when restricted to minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras and minimal graphs? For example, each abelian Lie algebra can only be associated with one minimal graph, reflecting its trivial bracket structure. In contrast, as we have seen in Example 30, the special linear Lie algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}) can be associated with two distinct minimal graphs. VI.5.3 Generating Lie algebras for a provided graph Another potential research direction involves reversing the perspective adopted in this work. Instead of starting with a Lie algebra and associating a labeled directed graph to it, one could ask: Q: Can we generate new and interesting Lie algebras by first constructing a valid graph and then associating a Lie algebra with it? Pursuing this approach would require further developement of tools to identify valid graphs, particularly valid redundant graphs, building on the initial effort in Section III. Such a methodology would not only relate the current framework to similar approaches in the literature, where Lie algebras are also constructed from graphs [55, 146, 58], but could also yield valuable insight into the role of graph symmetries. Recall that in Section V.3 we observed that a subgraph depicted in Figure 23 possesses a \mathbb{Z}_{3} -symmetry, yet we were unable to trace this symmetry to its origin in the Lie algebra. It is conceivable that such symmetries do not correspond to any meaningful invariant of the Lie algebra, resulting in what might be termed a apparent symmetry. However, if these symmetries do reflect structural properties, their study could be highly informative. In particular, redundant graphs may offer greater potential for visualizing such symmetries. This line on inquiry also relates to the following question: Q: If \mathfrak{g} is a graph-admissible but not minimal-graph-admissible, what is the smallest overcomplete basis that satisfies (8)? Addressing this question would require investigating how and which elements can be added to an overcomplete basis to maintain validity. Furthermore, one could explore the connection between proper graphs and choice-dependent graphs, as well as the existence of proper but not proper-minimal graphs. For instance, in Theorem 38 each of the obtained proper graphs with three vertices can also be associated with a Lie algebra as a minimal graph, raising deeper questions about the interplay between minimality and redundancy. VI.5.4 Potential application to quantum physics Finally, we aim to highlight potential applications of this method in analyzing the dynamics of a quantum mechanical system and fostering a deeper intuition for the associated calculations. Such an approach could be especially beneficial as a first approach to better understand the subject. To proceed, we consider a complex Hilbert space \mathcal{H} that contains all physical states of the system. Let the initial state at time t_{0}=0 be denoted by |\psi_{0}\rangle{}\equiv|\psi(0)\rangle{}\in\mathcal{H} . The central objective in quantum dynamics is generally to determine the state |\psi(t)\rangle{} that describes the system at a later time t\in\mathbb{R} . This is achieved by solving the Schrödinger equation \displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}|\psi(t)\rangle{}=-i\hat{H}(t)|\psi(t)\rangle{},\quad\text{with}\quad|\psi(0)\rangle{}=|\psi_{0}\rangle{}, where \hat{H}(t) denotes the Hamiltonian governing the system’s evolution [50]. This equation can be reformulated by introducing the unitary time-evolution operator \hat{U}(t) , defined by \hat{U}(t)|\psi_{0}\rangle{}=|\psi(t)\rangle{} . This operator \hat{U}(t) is a one-parameter group that satisfies the differential equation \displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{t}}\hat{U}(t)=-i\hat{H}(t)\hat{U}(t)\quad\text{with}\quad\hat{U}(0)=\hat{\mathds{1}}, (28) where \hat{\mathds{1}} denotes the identity operator. In most settings, the Hamiltonian \hat{H}(t) admits a decomposition of the form \displaystyle\hat{H}(t)=-i\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}u_{j}(t)\hat{g}_{j}, where u_{j}(t) are real-valued (potentially) time-dependent functions and \hat{g}_{j} are time-independent skew-hermitian generators belonging to a particular Lie algebra. This representation emphasizes therefore the deep connection between quantum dynamics and Lie algebraic structures. The differential equation (28) can, in principle, be solved using formal expansions such as the Magnus series [30] or the time-ordered Dyson series [68]. However, these solution are often non-conductive to obtaining an exact closed-form expression for |\psi(t)\rangle{}=\hat{U}(t)|\psi_{0}\rangle{} , due to the lack of analytical control. Consequently, one often resorts to truncating these expansions to obtain approximate solutions [30, 108]. Moreover, these serieses are typically local in time and fail to provide global validity, even for relatively simple systems [126, 168]. An alternative approach, pioneered by Wei and Norman [166, 167], offers a constructive method for solving the time-dependent Schrödinger equation. This technique provides a local solution whenever the Hamiltonian Lie algebra \mathfrak{g}:=\langle\{\hat{g}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} is finite dimensional, and, importantly, yields a global solution when the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} is solvable [167]. The core idea is to select a basis \mathcal{B}=\{\hat{g}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathcal{K}} of \mathfrak{g} (with an appropriate ordering) and factorize the time-evolution operator as \displaystyle\hat{U}(t)=\prod_{k\in\mathcal{K}}\hat{U}_{j}(t)\qquad\text{with}\qquad\hat{U}_{j}(t)=\exp(-f_{k}(t)\hat{g}_{k}), where the real-valued time-dependent functions f_{j}(t) encode the dynamics. To determine these functions, one differentiates the factorized form of \hat{U}(t) and compares it with the differential equation (28), obtaining: \displaystyle\left(-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\hat{U}(t)\right)\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t) \displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}f_{k}(t)\right)\left(\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}\hat{U}_{j}(t)\right)\hat{g}_{k}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}\hat{U}_{j}^{\dagger}(t)\right)=i\hat{H}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}u_{j}(t)\hat{g}_{j}, where, without loss of generality, we assumed that \mathcal{K}=\{1,\ldots,m\} , \mathcal{J}=\{1,\ldots,n\} , and m\geq n . Thus, the functions f_{k}(t) are determined by a system of coupled differential equations, whose structure depends on the similarity transformations \hat{U}_{j}(t)\hat{g}_{k}\hat{U}_{j}^{\dagger}(t) . Let us now present an example to illustrate this approach. Consider the bosonic Hamiltonian of the displaced harmonic oscillator \displaystyle\hat{H}(t)=\omega\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}+\alpha(t)\hat{a}+\alpha^{*}(t)\hat{a}^{\dagger}=\omega\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}+\mathrm{Re}(\{\alpha(t)\})(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger})+i\mathrm{Im}(\{\alpha(t)\})(\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger}), obtained via the displacement operation introduced in [43]. Here, \hat{a}^{\dagger} and \hat{a} denote the bosonic creation and annihilation operators respectively, which satisfy the canonical commutation relation [\hat{a},\hat{a}^{\dagger}]=1 . For this system, we can choose the generating sets of operators as \mathcal{G}:=\{i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a},i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}),\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger}\} and a basis of \mathfrak{g}:=\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} is clearly given by \mathcal{B}=\{i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a},i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}),\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger},i\} . This Lie algebra is known as the Wigner-Heisenberg algebra \mathfrak{wh}_{2} [87]. Moreover, the basis \mathcal{B} satisfies the structural relations (7), confirming that the Lie algebra \mathfrak{wh}_{2} is minimal-graph-admissible. The framework developed offers three key applications in this context: 1. Visualization of the Lie algebra: Constructing the associated graph provides immediate insights into the structure of the Lie algebra and offers an intuitive starting point for understanding its properties. For example, for the Wigner-Heisenberg algebra \mathfrak{wh}_{2} with the basis \mathcal{B} , the corresponding graph clearly encodes the commutation relations among these generators. Such a visualization is particularly useful for identifying subalgebras, connectivity patterns, and structural features. The corresponding graph is illustrated in Figure 26. Figure 26: Depiction of the graph associated with the Wigner-Heisenberg algebra \mathfrak{wh}_{2} , constructed using the basis \mathcal{B}=\{i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a},i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}),\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger},i\} and Algorithm 1. Here, we used, for visual simplicity, the convention to depict vertices by black circles instead of writing the name of the vertex inside a circle that represents it, as would be the correct convention for this graph. 2. Solvability analysis: If the Hamiltonian Lie algebra \langle\{\hat{g}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} is graph-admissible, one can construct an associated graph and employ Theorem 62 to decide whether the algebra is solvable or not. This, in turn allows us to infer whether a global solution to the Schrödinger equation exists under the Wei-Norman approach. Returning to the example of the displaced harmonic oscillator and the Lie algebra \mathfrak{wh}_{2} , we observe that the graph depicted in Figure 26 contains no closed directed walks that induce self-contained subgraphs. Thus, the Lie algebra \mathfrak{wh}_{2} is solvable and a global solution of the time evolution can be obtained. 3. Similarity transformations: When calculating the time-evolution operator \hat{U}(t) using the Wei-Norman method, one needs to determine the real time-dependent function f_{k}(t) , which rely on computing multiple similarity relations. As explained in the literature [2], the following identity holds: \displaystyle\exp(-s_{j}\hat{g}_{j})\hat{g}_{k}\exp(s_{j}\hat{g}_{j}) \displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-s_{j})^{n}}{n!}\operatorname{ad}_{\hat{g}_{j}}^{n}(\hat{g}_{k}), where \operatorname{ad}_{x}^{n+1}(y)=[x,\operatorname{ad}_{x}^{n}(y)] , and one adopts the convention \operatorname{ad}_{\hat{g}_{j}}^{0}(\hat{g}_{k})\equiv\hat{g}_{k} . The associated graph provides a first intuitive interpretation of this relation: the similarity transformation \exp(-\hat{g}_{j})\hat{g}_{k}\exp(\hat{g}_{j}) can be visualized as an infinite walk through the graph. Starting at the vertex corresponding to \hat{g}_{k} , one iteratively traverses edges labeled by \hat{g}_{j} , reaching each time the resulting vertex. The contribution of each step in the walk is weighted by the constants \alpha_{jk} . To formalize this, let \mathfrak{g} be a complex graph-admissible Lie algebra with a (possibly overcomplete) basis \mathcal{B}=\{v_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}} satisfying relations (7) (or (8)), and let G(V,E) be its associated graph. Define the weight function \omega:E\to\mathbb{C} with concrete action \displaystyle\omega:e\mapsto\omega(e):=\alpha_{jk},\qquad\text{where}\qquad[\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)]=\alpha_{jk}\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e). To compute the similarity transformation \exp(-v_{j})v_{k}\exp(v_{j}) , one starts by determining the walk W=(w_{0},e_{0},w_{1},\ldots) , which starts at the vertex w_{0}=v_{k} and traverses the edge e_{0} labeled by v_{j} . Then, one repeats this process either indefinitely or until no further edge labeled by v_{j} exists that originates from the vertex reached in the previous step. The similarity transformation then reads: \displaystyle\exp(-v_{j})v_{k}\exp(v_{k}) \displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\left(\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}\omega(e_{j})\right)v_{n}=v_{k}+\omega(e_{0})v_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\omega(e_{0})\omega(e_{1})v_{2}+\frac{1}{6}\omega(e_{0})\omega(e_{1})\omega(e_{2})v_{3}+\ldots This graphical interpretation not only aids in developing intuition for these calculations but also simplifies practical computation, especially when truncating the series is sufficient for a given application when the error budget is non-vanishing. Furthermore, the finiteness of the graph and the edge rules established in Proposition 35 guarantee that the walk either terminates or loops after finitely many steps, where the former is a property that always holds for nilpotent Lie algebras. This makes the approach particularly useful for systems where the underlying algebra is nilpotent or solvable. Here, we also want to emphasize the implications of this approach when generalized to infinite-dimensional Lie algebras and infinite graphs. In such cases, a non-repeating and non-terminating walk clearly illustrates the challenges in analyzing convergence behavior of the series expansion. Moreover, this graphical perspective simplifies the computation of similarity transformations when approximating the dynamics by truncating the expansion: instead of explicitly calculating nested commutators, one can simply follow the corresponding walk in the graph and apply the appropriate weights. This not only reduces the computational complexity but also provides an intuitive framework for understanding the structure of the approximation. Example: Obtaining similarity relations using walks in the associated graphs Example 107. We now demonstrate how to compute the similarity transformations for the case of the Wigner-Heisenberg algebra \mathfrak{wh}_{2} . We use again the basis \mathcal{B}=\{i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a},i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}),\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger},i\} , and for completeness, we add the corresponding weights to the edges as a second label. This results in the graph depicted in Figure 27. Following the approach described above, we associate the walk W=(v_{0},e_{0},v_{1},e_{1},v_{2},e_{2},\ldots) , defined by: \displaystyle v_{2\ell}:=\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger},\quad e_{2\ell}:=(\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger},(ia^{\dagger}a,1),i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}),\quad v_{2\ell+1}:=i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}),\quad e_{2\ell+1}:=(i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}),(ia^{\dagger}a,-1),\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger}), for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , with the calculation of the similarity transformation \exp(-i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a})(\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger})\exp(i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}) . An illustration of the corresponding walk can be found in Figure 27. For the edge weights, we note that \omega(e_{2\ell})=1=-\omega(e_{2\ell+1}) for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , and consequently \displaystyle\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}\omega(e_{j})=(-1)^{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}. (29) This allows us to compute: \displaystyle\exp(-i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a})(\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger})\exp(i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}) \displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\left(\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}\omega(e_{j})\right)v_{n}. Splitting the series into terms of even and odd ordered yields together with the application of (29): \displaystyle\exp(-i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a})(\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger})\exp(i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}) \displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(2n)!}(-1)^{\lfloor\frac{2n}{2}\rfloor}v_{2n}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(2n+1)!}(-1)^{\lfloor\frac{2n+1}{2}\rfloor}v_{2n+1} \displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(2n)!}v_{0}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(2n+1)!}v_{1}. Substituting v_{0}=\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger} and v_{1}=i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}) , we obtain: \displaystyle\exp(-i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a})(\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger})\exp(i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}) \displaystyle=\cos(1)\left(\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger}\right)+i\sin(1)\left(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}\right)=e^{i}\hat{a}-e^{-i}\hat{a}^{\dagger}. This matches the expected result, as it is on agreement with the literature [87]. This example illustrates a walk that loops the same sequence indefinitely, which can be treated by splitting the series into the terms that have a common vertex label. To contrast the previous example, consider the similarity transformation \exp(-i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}))(i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a})\exp(i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger})) . Here, the corresponding walk W^{\prime} starts at the vertex v_{0}=i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a} . Following the edge labeled with i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}) , one reaches the vertex labeled by \hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger} . Continuing along the next edge, also labeled by i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}) , one arrives at the vertex labeled with i , where the walk terminates because no further edge originates from this vertex, especially no edge labeled by i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}) . This yields: \displaystyle\exp(-i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}))(i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a})\exp(i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger})) \displaystyle=i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}+\omega(e_{0})\left(\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\omega(e_{0})\omega(e_{1})i, where \omega(e_{0})=1 and \omega(e_{1})=2 . Substituting these values yields: \displaystyle\exp(-i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}))(i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a})\exp(i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger})) \displaystyle=i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}+\left(\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger}\right)+i. The corresponding walk is also depicted in Figure 27. Figure 27: Depiction of the graph associated with the Wigner Heisenberg algebra \mathfrak{wh}_{2} from Figure 26, now including the edge weights as secondary labels. The two walks W (shown in green) and W^{\prime} (shown in blue) from Example 107 are also illustrated. VII Conclusion The primary aspiration of this work was to develop a visualization framework that facilitates intuitive understanding of the structural properties of Lie algebras. It is in this spirit that we created a tool that allows to immediately identify fundamental algebraic properties, such as solvability, nilpotency, and the presence of ideals, by providing a graphical representation of the algebras. A strong motivation for our work lies in the programme recently initiated to study Hamiltonian Lie algebras that are key to quantum dynamics [40], together with the ongoing efforts to determine finite-dimensional Lie subalgebras of the skew-hermitian Weyl algebra \hat{A}_{n} generated by elements of significance to the dynamics of physical processes [87]. In this context, a visualization tool is expected to be helpful for understanding key aspects of the Lie algebras of interest. We started by restricting our attention to finite-dimensional Lie algebras that satisfy a constraint related to the simultaneous diagonalization condition for square matrices whereby the adjoint actions of certain elements must obey a specific sparsity constraint. Concretely, the Lie bracket of any two vertices must be proportional to a vertex, or vanish. Lie algebras fulfilling this requirement were termed “graph-admissible”, whereas those that did not were classified as “non-graph-admissible”. This condition is motivated by the idea of associating a labeled directed graph with a given Lie algebra and encoding the Lie bracket structure within the vertex-edge configuration. Graph-admissible Lie algebras admit a further subclassification: those Lie algebras that can be represented by a graph in which the number of nodes coincides with the dimension of the Lie algebra. These graphs provide a “faithful” representation and the corresponding Lie algebras were called “minimal-graph-admissible”. Conversely, those that cannot are referred to as “redundant-graph-admissible”, since their associated graphs exhibit redundancies: in particular, the vertex set forms only a spanning set rather than a basis. We demonstrated that the classification into non-graph-admissible, minimal-graph-admissible and redundant-graph-admissible (but not minimal-graph-admissible) is well founded by providing explicit examples of Lie algebras in each category. This was achieved by analyzing the family of solvable three-dimensional Lie algebras L_{\alpha}^{3} (cf. [56]). Furthermore, we established that a broad class of mathematically and physically significant Lie algebras, including all complex semisimple Lie algebras, all nilpotent Lie algebras, and all finite-dimensional Lie subalgebras of the Weyl algebra A_{1} , are graph-admissible. The graphs that can be associated with graph-admissible Lie algebras must satisfy specific general structural properties reflecting the antisymmetry of the Lie bracket, as well as the constraints imposed by the Jacobi identity. In this work we provided relevant preliminary results that restrict the number of admissible graphs. However, we were unable to determine the exact number of graphs that, for a given number of n vertices, can be associated with an n -dimensional Lie algebra, except for the case n\leq 3 . The conditions of interest were mostly derived for minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras associated with minimal graphs only, but not redundant graphs associated with redundant-graph-admissible Lie algebras. Moreover, they only provide a weak criterion for proper graphs, i.e., graphs that can be associated with a Lie algebra, dictated by the Jacobi identity. Further work is required to refine and generalize these criteria. We then provided a methodology to identify key structural properties of a given Lie algebra solely via their associated graphs. Specifically, we formulated graph-theoretic criteria for identifying abelian elements, solvability, nilpotency, the existence of ideals, as well as simplicity, semisimplicity, and reductiveness. The criteria for solvability and nilpotency were inspired by classical algebraic constructions: we mirrored the formation of the derived series and lower central series with corresponding sequences of graphs, and provided accessible pruning-algorithms to compute them. These constructions enabled us to establish strong necessary and sufficient conditions for solvability and nilpotency based exclusively on the initial graph, without requiring iterative graph reductions. These results present a significant step toward bridging algebraic and combinatorial perspectives as they allow structural classifications using purely graphical features. In order to illustrate the practical perspectives of these methods, we applied them to three important Lie algebras that are key also to physics: the Schrödinger algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{m} , the Lorentz algebra \mathfrak{so}(3;1)_{\mathbb{C}} , and a finite-dimensional subalgebra of the skew-hermitian Weyl algebra \hat{A}_{n} . These examples demonstrate the effectiveness of our framework in capturing deep structural properties through visualizations alone. We concluded our analysis by examining the strengths of the proposed approach and outlining potential generalizations of the framework. One particularly promising direction lies in exploring the relationship between graph-admissibility and the concept of Lie algebra gradings, which could lead to deeper structural insights, new classification techniques, and a broadening of the applicability of the current framework. In addition, we discussed several avenues for future research, presenting open questions and conjectures and highlighting their connections to broader mathematical efforts. Finally, we emphasized the practical significance of these results by considering the role of solvability in determining the time evolution of certain physical systems. In this context, our approach can assist in better understanding concepts such as similarity transformations, since it allows to map computations of sequences of nested commutators within algebras of interest to walks on the associated graphs. This perspective helps clarifying why factorization of the time evolution of the system into a sequence of basic operations is typically difficult to achieve within infinite-dimensional settings, offering a conceptual bridge between abstract algebraic theory and practical applications in physics. Appendix A Further remarks on choice-dependent graphs In section III we investigated the rules for distinguishing valid proper graphs, i.e., labeled directed graphs that can be associated with a given graph-admissible Lie algebra, from improper graphs, i.e., graphs that cannot. We demonstrated that certain graphs can only be associated with a given Lie algebra if edges of the form e=(v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}},v_{\mathrm{e}}) are translated in Lie brackets [v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}}]=\kappa v_{\mathrm{e}} with \kappa\in\mathbb{F}^{*} under the conditions that the coefficients \kappa satisfy specific constraints. This makes corresponding graphs coefficient-dependent. Importantly, this dependence on the proportionality constants is affected by the characteristic of the underlying field \mathbb{F} , since the Jacobi identity \displaystyle 0=[x,[y,z]]+[y,[z,x]]+[z,[x,y]] holds also when the right-hand side coincides with k\times\operatorname{char}(\mathbb{F}) additions of the unit element, for k\in\mathbb{N} . Consequently, any criterion that differentiates between choice-independent and choice-dependent graphs must account for this subtlety. To illustrate this point consider Example 108 below, which demonstrates how the validity of The Jacobi identity can hinge on the choice of coefficients and the field’s characteristic. Example: The derived algebra of a solvable Lie algebra Let \mathfrak{g} be a solvable Lie algebra over a field \mathbb{F} . If \operatorname{char}(\mathbb{F})=0 , then its derived algebra \mathcal{D\mathfrak{g}} is nilpotent [97]. However, when \operatorname{char}(\mathbb{F})\neq 0 , this property generally does not hold [97]. The following example illustrates a solvable yet graph-admissible Lie algebra whose first derived algebra fails to be nilpotent for \operatorname{char}(\mathbb{F})>0 . This example underscores the interplay between choice-dependent graphs and the selection of coefficients, demonstrating that these choices are inherently linked to the characteristic of the underlying field \mathbb{F} . Example 108. Let \mathbb{F} be a field of characteristic p , and let \mathfrak{g} denote the p -dimensional Lie algebra spanned by the basis elements \{x,y,e_{j}\}_{j=1}^{p} , which satisfy the following bracket relations, see [97] for reference: \displaystyle[x,y] \displaystyle=x,\; \displaystyle\;[x,e_{j}] \displaystyle=-e_{j+1}\text{ for }j\leq p-1,\; \displaystyle\;[x,e_{p}] \displaystyle=-e_{1},\; \displaystyle\;[y,e_{j}] \displaystyle=-(j-1)e_{j}. (30) Then, the Jacobi identity yields: \displaystyle[e_{j},[x,y]]+[x,[y,e_{j}]]+[y,[e_{j},x]] \displaystyle=e_{j+1}+(j-1)e_{j+1}-je_{j+1}=0, \displaystyle\text{for }j\leq p-1 (31a) \displaystyle[e_{p},[x,y]]+[x,[y,e_{p}]]+[y,[e_{p},x]] \displaystyle=e_{1}+(p-1)e_{1}=pe_{1}=0, \displaystyle\text{for }j=p. (31b) All other instances of the Jacobi clearly vanish identically. Thus, one can construct a graph for this Lie algebra using the basis \mathcal{B}=\{x,y,e_{j}\}_{j=1}^{p} . The corresponding graph is shown in Figure 28. However, if we change the field \mathbb{F} to another field \mathbb{F}^{\prime} with \operatorname{char}(\mathbb{F}^{\prime})=q\neq p , the Jacobi identity computed in (31b) would be violated. Consequently, the graph G(V,E) cannot be associated with a Lie algebra spanned by elements in \mathcal{B} which satisfy the bracket relations (30). This failure directly affects the parameter choices for translating edges e\in E into valid Lie bracket relations. Such a situation can never occur over a field of characteristic zero, since no nonzero multiple of the unit element ever vanishes. This observation emphasizes the choice-dependent nature of the graph representation, which is inherently tied to the characteristic of the underlying field. Figure 28: Illustration of the graph constructed in Example 108 along with the first derived graph and its lower central series shown for the case p=5 . This example motivates the following statement: Corollary 109. Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra over a field \mathbb{F} associated with the labeled directed graph G(V,E) . If G(V,E) contains no self-contained subgraph induced by a closed directed walk, but the derived graph \mathcal{D}G(V,E) does contain a closed directed walk, then \operatorname{char}(\mathbb{F})\neq 0 . It is furthermore clear that a graph described by the conditions in Corollary 109 is generally choice-dependent. Proof. This claim is a direct consequence Theorems 62 and 70, since Theorem 62 implies that \mathfrak{g} is solvable if G(V,E) contains no self-contained subgraph induced by a closed directed walk. Furthermore, Theorem 70 implies that the derived algebra \mathcal{D}\mathfrak{g} is not nilpotent, since the derived graph \mathcal{D}G(V,E) contains a closed directed walk. However, such a situation is prohibited if \mathfrak{g} is defined over a field \mathbb{F} of characteristic \operatorname{char}(\mathbb{F})=0 [97]. ∎ Appendix B Remaining algorithms This section is dedicated to the remaining algorithms mentioned in the main text. These algorithms complement the constructions introduced earlier and provide explicit procedures for generating graphs associated with the derived and lower central series of Lie algebras. Their inclusion ensures completeness and facilitates practical implementation of the theoretical framework developed in the main text. B.1 Generating labeled directed graphs associated with the derived series of a given Lie algebra In section IV.2 we introduced an approach for associating a labeled directed graph with the \ell -th derived algebra of a given Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} . The procedure starts with the construction of a graph G(V,E) using Algorithm 1 followed by iterative pruning rules that yield a graph \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) corresponding to \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} . These rules remove vertices and edges that fail to satisfy specific structural conditions. This construction is informed by the recursively defined index sets \displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell+1)}:=\delta\left(\left\{(j,k)\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\mid\;\tilde{\alpha}_{jk}\neq 0\right\}\right)\quad\text{for all }\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0},\quad\text{where}\quad\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(0)}:=\mathcal{M}. Consequently, one can directly employ these index sets to obtain a graph associated with any derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} , provided that \mathfrak{g} is graph-admissible and a suitable (possibly overcomplete) basis \mathcal{B} satisfying the relations (7) (or (8)) is known. A graph assocaited with \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} can then be constructed by applying Algorithm 1 to the basis \mathcal{B} when restricted to the elements with indices from \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)} . For completeness, this procedure is formalized in Algorithm 6. Algorithm 6 Algorithm for generating a minimal graph that represents the \ell -th derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} for a given finite-dimensional minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra Data: A (possibly overcomplete) basis \mathcal{B}=\{\tilde{x}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} of the graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , satisfying the Lie bracket relations (8) along with the determining matrix \boldsymbol{\alpha} , the index function \delta and a non-negative integer \ell . Result: A labeled directed graph associated with the derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} . 1 V \leftarrow \emptyset /* Initialize an empty vertex set */ 2 E \leftarrow \emptyset /* Initialize an empty edge set */ 3 \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}} \leftarrow \mathcal{M} /* Initialize the index set \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(0)} */ 4 j \leftarrow 0 /* Initialize a counting index */ 5 while j<\ell do /* Calculate the set \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)} */ 6 \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}} \leftarrow \delta(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}},\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}})\setminus\{0\} /* Compute \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(j+1)} using the previous set \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(j)} */ 7 j \leftarrow j+1 8 foreach j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}} do 9 V \leftarrow V\cup\{\tilde{x}_{j}\} 10 foreach j,k\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}} with j<k do /* Add all relevant edges to edge set E */ 11 if [\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{k}]=\tilde{\alpha}_{jk}\tilde{x}_{\delta(j,k)} with \alpha_{jk}\neq 0 then 12 E \leftarrow (\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{k},\tilde{x}_{\delta(j,k)})\cup(\tilde{x}_{k},\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{\delta(j,k)}) /* Add edge to edge set E ; each edge is an ordered triple: (start vertex, edge-label, end vertex) */ 13 return \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E)=G(V^{(\ell)},E^{(\ell)})\equiv G(V,E) /* Return labeled directed graph \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) associated with \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} */ It is straightforward to verify that the graphs produced by Algorithm 6 coincide with the graphs obtained via Algorithm 2, provided the former is applied iteratively for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} . This equivalence follows from the observation that \displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell+1)}=\delta\left(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)},\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\right)\setminus\{0\} for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , since \alpha_{jk}=0 if and only if \delta(j,k)=0 . B.2 Improved algorithm for generating derived graphs from redundant initial graphs The applyication of Algorithm 2, when working with redundant initial graphs, typically yields redundant graphs associated with the derived algebras. However, in certain cases Algorithm 2 may inadvertently remove redundant elements, resulting in a minimal graph. If the goal is to preserve the redundant nature of the representation, an improved procedure is required. Algorithm 7 addresses this issue by modifying the pruning strategy of Algorithm 2. Specifically, edges are removed at a later stage, after reintroducing vertices that belong to \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} but were previously eliminated. This ensures that the resulting graph remains a faithful representation of the derived algebra when redundancy is maintained. Unlike Algorithm 2, the improved algorithm does not discard edges assocaited with re-added vertices, as doing so would compromise the structural integrity of the representation. As discussed in Section IV.2 (see remarks following Algorithm 2), this approach guarantees that the resulting graph can be consistently associated with \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} . Algorithm 7 Algorithm for generating a directed graph associated with the \ell -th derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} for a given finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} Data: A (overcomplete) basis \mathcal{B}=\{\tilde{x}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} of elements \{x_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{N}} of the graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , satisfying the Lie bracket relations (7) Result: A sequence of labeled directed graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) associated with the derived algebras \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} of the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} for all \ell\geq 0 , constructed with respect to the given basis \mathcal{B} . 1 \mathcal{D} \leftarrow \emptyset /* Initialize the set contain all graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) associated with each derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} */ 2 V \leftarrow \mathcal{B} /* Initialize the vertex set for G(V,E) with the basis \mathcal{B} */ 3 E \leftarrow \emptyset /* Initialize the edge set for G(V,E) */ 4 foreach (j,k)\in\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M} with j<k do /* Add all relevant edges to edge set E */ 5 if [\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{k}]=\tilde{\alpha}_{jk}x_{\delta(j,k)} with \alpha_{jk}\neq 0 then 6 E \leftarrow E\cup(x_{j},x_{k},x_{\delta(j,k)})\cup\leftarrow E\simeq(x_{k},x_{j},x_{\delta(j,k)}) /* Add edge to edge set E ; each edge is an ordered triple: (start vertex, edge-label, end vertex) */ 7 \mathcal{D} \leftarrow G(V,E) /* Add initial graph associated with \mathfrak{g}=\mathcal{D}^{0}\mathfrak{g} to \mathcal{D} */ 8 foreach \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1} do 9 V_{\mathrm{r}} \leftarrow \emptyset /* Initialize set containing removed vertices */ 10 foreach vertex v\in V do 11 if there exists no edge e\in E such that \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)=v then 12 V \leftarrow V\setminus\{v\} /* Remove v from V */ 13 V_{\mathrm{r}} \leftarrow V\cup\{v\} /* Add v to v_{\mathrm{r}} */ 14 foreach vertex v\in V_{\mathrm{r}} do 15 if v\in\spn\{V\} then 16 V \leftarrow V\cup\{v\} Add v to v 17 foreach edge e\in E do 18 if v\in e then /* edge is of the form (\cdot,\cdot,v) or (\cdot,v,\cdot) or (v,\cdot,\cdot) */ 19 E \leftarrow E\setminus\{e\} /* Remove e from E */ 20 \mathcal{D} \leftarrow \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E)=G(V^{(\ell-1)},E^{(\ell-1)}) 21 return \mathcal{D} B.3 Modifications for graded Lie algebras In Section VI.4, we discussed a possible generalization of the graph-theoretic approach of representing Lie algebras using labeled directed graphs by considering Lie algebra gradations rather than bases that satisfy the requirements (7) or (8). In this extended setting, Algorithm 1 was adapted to accommodate gradations. We now present the corresponding modifications to Algorithms 2 and 3, which compute, under certain conditions, graphs associated with the derived and lower central series. We begin with the algorithm for generating graphs associated with the derived series under a grading structure. The recursive procedure defining the derived series naturally extends to this setting, thus allowing the construction of derived graphs by iteratively applying the pruning process. While this approach is immediately valid for certain classes of Lie algebras, its general applicability depends on Conjecture 105, which posits that the method works for any Lie algebra graded by an abelian magma of finest granularity. The modified procedure is formalized in Algorithm 8. It is conceptually analogous to Algorithm 2, but operates on graded components rather than individual basis elements. Specifically: • If no edge targets a vertex labeled by the subspace \mathfrak{g}_{j} , then \mathfrak{g}_{j}\cap\mathcal{D}\mathfrak{g}=\{0\} . • Conversely, if an edge targets \mathfrak{g}_{j} , then \mathfrak{g}_{j}\cap\mathcal{D}\mathfrak{g}\neq\{0\} , and \mathcal{D}\mathfrak{g} is contained in the direct sum of all such targeted subspaces. When restricted to Lie algebras \mathfrak{g} satisfying \operatorname{fg}(\mathfrak{g})=\dim(\mathfrak{g}) and graded by an abelian magma \mathcal{M} of finest granularity, the situation simplifies: [\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{k}]=\mathfrak{g}_{\delta(j,k)} or [\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{k}]=\{0\} , where each \mathfrak{g}_{j} is one dimensional. In this case, \mathcal{D}\mathfrak{g} coincides with the direct sum of all targeted vertices. Therefore, for the pruned graph to represent \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} faithfully, one must remove all vertices that are not targeted by any edge and all edges labeled by these vertices. This ensures consistency between the graph structure and the graded decomposition of the derived algebra. Note that this observation might indicate that Algorithm 8 is only applicable for gradations that satisfy that either [\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{k}]=\mathfrak{g}_{\delta(j,k)} or [\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{k}]=\{0\} , but not both for all j,k\in\mathcal{M} . However, as demonstrated in Appendix E for the real Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{3,2} , this condition is not strictly necessary for Algorithm 8 to produce graphs that faithfully represent the associated derived algebras. Algorithm 8 Algorithm for generating a labeled directed graph associated with the \ell -th derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} for an \mathcal{M} -magma-graded Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} Data: An abelian magma (\mathcal{M},\delta) and a Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} that is \mathcal{M} -magma-graded with \mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{M}}\mathfrak{g}_{j} and [\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{k}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{\delta(j,k)} . Result: A sequence of labeled directed graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) associated with the derived algebras \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} of the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} for all \ell\geq 0 , constructed with respect to the magma \mathcal{M} . 1 \mathcal{D} \leftarrow \emptyset /* Initialize the set containing all graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) associated with each derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} */ 2 V \leftarrow \{\mathfrak{g}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} /* Initialize the vertex set for G(V,E) with the subspaces \mathfrak{g}_{j} */ 3 E \leftarrow \emptyset /* Initialize an empty edge set */ 4 foreach (j,k)\in\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M} with j\leq k do /* Add all relevant edges to edge set E */ 5 if [\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{k}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{\delta(j,k)} and [\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{k}]\neq\{0\} then 6 E \leftarrow E\cup(\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{k},\mathfrak{g}_{\delta(j,k)})\cup(\mathfrak{g}_{k},\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{\delta(j,k)}) /* Add edge to edge set E ; each edge is an orderd triple: (start vertex, edge-label, end vertex) */ 7 \mathcal{D} \leftarrow \{G(V,E)\} /* Add initial graph associated with \mathfrak{g}=\mathcal{D}^{0}\mathfrak{g} to \mathcal{D} */ 8 foreach \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1} do 9 foreach vertex v\in V do 10 if there exists no edge e\in E such that \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)=v then 11 V \leftarrow V\setminus\{v\} /* Remove v from V */ 12 foreach edge e\in E do 13 if v\in e then /* i.e., edge is of the form (\cdot,\cdot,v) or (\cdot,v,\cdot) or (v,\cdot,\cdot) */ 14 E \leftarrow E\setminus\{e\} /* Remove e from E */ 15 V^{(\ell)}\equiv\mathcal{D}^{\ell}V\leftarrow V 16 E^{(\ell)}\equiv\mathcal{D}^{\ell}E\leftarrow E 17 \mathcal{D} \leftarrow \mathcal{D}\cup\{\mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E)=G(V^{(\ell)},E^{(\ell)})\} /* Add updated graph \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) */ 18 return \mathcal{D} /* Return set of all graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) associated with the derived algebras \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} for all \ell\geq 0 */ Finally, we present the modification of the algorithm that generates graphs associated with the lower central series when the Lie algebra is equipped with a grading structure. This procedure is formalized in Algorithm 9, and its justification follows analogously to reasoning for Algorithm 8. Algorithm 9 Algorithm for generating a labeled directed graph associated with \ell -th Lie algebra of the lower central series \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} for an \mathcal{M} -magma-graded Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} Data: An abelian magma (\mathcal{M},\delta) and a Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} that is \mathcal{M} -magma-graded with \mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{M}}\mathfrak{g}_{j} and [\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{k}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{\delta(j,k)} .
