Return the set of all graphs \mathcal{C}^{\ell}G(V,E) associated with the Lie algebras \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} of the lower central series for all \ell\geq 0 */ Appendix C Realizing the Schrödinger algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{m} within the Weyl algebra A_{m} It has been shown that the complex Schrödinger algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{1} admits a faithful realization within the complex Weyl algebra A_{1} , see [145]. Furthermore, it has been demonstrated that the real Schrödinger algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{1} can be faithfully realized within the skew-hermitian Weyl algebra \hat{A}_{1} , see [87]. In this section, we aim to prove that the real Schrödinger algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{m} can be faithfully realized within the skew-hermitian Weyl algebra \hat{A}_{m} , and, consequently, that the complex Schrödinger algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{m} admits a faithful realization within the complex Weyl algebra A_{m} . Finally, we comment on the physical significance of the real Schrödinger algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{m} in the context of quantum mechanics. Before constructing the realization, let us recall the most essential definitions. The bosonic creation and annihilation operators, denoted by \hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger} and \hat{a}_{j} , have the following canonical commutation relations77 7 Note that these commutation relations [A,B]:=AB-BA define a Lie bracket.: \displaystyle[\hat{a}_{j},\hat{a}_{k}] \displaystyle=0,\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{a}_{j},\hat{a}_{k}^{\dagger}] \displaystyle=\delta_{jk}\hat{\mathds{1}},\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger},\hat{a}_{k}^{\dagger}] \displaystyle=0, where \hat{\mathds{1}} is a central element, i.e., an element that commutes with every creation and annihilation operator. These operators span the Heisenberg algebra \mathfrak{h}_{m}=\langle\{\hat{a}_{j},\hat{a}_{k},\hat{\mathds{1}}\}_{j=1}^{m}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} , which is typically considered over either the real or complex numbers. The complex Weyl algebra A_{m} can be regarded as the universal enveloping algebra U(\mathfrak{h}_{m}) of the complex Heisenberg algebra \mathfrak{h}_{m} , under the restriction that \hat{\mathds{1}} acts as the multiplicative identity. This algebra is equipped with the hermitian conjugation (\cdot)^{\dagger}:A_{m}\to A_{m} with concrete action (\cdot)^{\dagger}:p\mapsto p^{\dagger} , which is a linear anti-automorphism satisfying: \displaystyle(\hat{\mathds{1}})^{\dagger} \displaystyle=\hat{\mathds{1}},\; \displaystyle\;(i\hat{\mathds{1}})^{\dagger} \displaystyle=-i\hat{\mathds{1}},\; \displaystyle\;(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger})^{\dagger} \displaystyle=\hat{a}_{j},\; \displaystyle\;(\hat{a}_{j})^{\dagger}=\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}, and reversing the order of multiplication, i.e., (\hat{p}_{1}\hat{p}_{2})^{\dagger}=\hat{p}_{2}^{\dagger}\hat{p}_{1}^{\dagger} . The Poincaré-Birkhoff-Witt theorem guarantees that the normal-ordered monomials \displaystyle\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m}(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger})^{\alpha_{j}}\right)\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m}\hat{a}_{j}^{\beta_{j}}\right)=\prod_{j=1}^{m}(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger})^{\alpha_{j}}\hat{a}_{j}^{\beta_{j}},\qquad\text{with}\qquad\alpha_{j},\beta_{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} form a basis of the Weyl algebra A_{m} , see [29]. However, since bosonic dynamics are governed by hermitian Hamiltonians \hat{H}(t) , where the time-evolution operator \hat{U}(t) satisfies the differential equation \displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{t}}\hat{U}(t)=-i\hat{H}(t)\hat{U}(t),\qquad\text{with}\qquad\hat{U}(0)=\hat{\mathds{1}}, it is natural to restrict attention to the skew-hermitian Weyl algebra \hat{A}_{m} . This algebra consists of all skew-hermitian polynomials in A_{m} , i.e., polynomials \hat{p}\in A_{m} satisfying \hat{p}^{\dagger}=-\hat{p} , and forms a real Lie algebra under the commutator bracket. A basis of \hat{A}_{m} can be described by the following elements: \displaystyle g+=i\left(\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m}(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger})^{\alpha_{j}}\hat{a}_{j}^{\beta_{j}}\right)^{\dagger}+\prod_{j=1}^{m}(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger})^{\alpha_{j}}\hat{a}_{j}^{\beta_{j}}\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad g_{-}=\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m}(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger})^{\alpha_{j}}\hat{a}_{j}^{\beta_{j}}\right)^{\dagger}-\prod_{j=1}^{m}(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger})^{\alpha_{j}}\hat{a}_{j}^{\beta_{j}}, where the integers \alpha_{j},\beta_{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} satisfy the following conditions: • g_{+} term: If there exists an index k\in\{1,\ldots,m\} such that \alpha_{k}<\beta_{k} , then there exists an index j\in\{1,\ldots,k-1\} such that \alpha_{j}>\beta_{j} and \alpha_{\ell}\geq\beta_{\ell} for all \ell\in\{1,\ldots,k-1\} . • g_{-} term: \alpha_{j}\neq\beta_{j} for at least one index j\in\{1,\ldots,m\} , and if there exists an index k\in\{1,\ldots,m\} such that \alpha_{k}<\beta_{k} , then there exists an index j\in\{1,\ldots,k-1\} such that \alpha_{j}>\beta_{j} and \alpha_{\ell}\geq\beta_{\ell} for all \ell\in\{1,\ldots,k-1\} . This concludes the repetition of the most essential definitions. More details can be found in the relevant literature [40]. Let us proceed with showing the following claim: Proposition 110. The real Schrödinger algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{m} can be faithfully realized within the skew-hermitian Weyl algebra \hat{A}_{m} . Proof. It has been shown that the following six elements of \hat{A}_{m} \displaystyle\hat{h}_{j} \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\hat{a}_{j}^{2}-(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger})^{2}\right),\; \displaystyle\;\hat{x}_{j} \displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\left(2i\left(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}+\frac{1}{2}\right)+i\left(\hat{a}_{j}^{2}+(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger})^{2}\right)\right),\; \displaystyle\;y_{j} \displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\left(2i\left(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}+\frac{1}{2}\right)-i\left(\hat{a}_{j}^{2}+(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger})^{2}\right)\right), \displaystyle\hat{p}_{j} \displaystyle=\hat{a}_{j}-\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger},\; \displaystyle\;\hat{q}_{j} \displaystyle=\hat{a}_{j}-\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger},\; \displaystyle\;\hat{z} \displaystyle=-2i\hat{\mathds{1}}, satisfy the commutation relations: \displaystyle[\hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{j}] \displaystyle=2\hat{x}_{j},\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{h}_{j},\hat{y}_{j}] \displaystyle=-2\hat{y}_{j},\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{j}] \displaystyle=\hat{h}_{j},\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{h}_{j},\hat{q}_{j}] \displaystyle=\hat{q}_{j},\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{h}_{j},\hat{p}_{j}] \displaystyle=-\hat{p}_{j}, \displaystyle[\hat{q}_{j},\hat{p}_{j}] \displaystyle=\hat{z},\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{x}_{j},\hat{q}_{j}] \displaystyle=0,\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{x}_{j},\hat{p}_{j}] \displaystyle=\hat{q}_{j},\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{y}_{j},\hat{q}_{j}] \displaystyle=\hat{p}_{j},\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{y}_{j},\hat{p}_{j}] \displaystyle=0, while \hat{z} commutes with every other element [87]. This extends naturally to the multi-mode case: \displaystyle[\hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{k}] \displaystyle=2\delta_{jk}\hat{x}_{j},\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{h}_{j},\hat{y}_{k}] \displaystyle=-2\delta_{jk}\hat{y}_{j},\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{k}] \displaystyle=\delta_{jk}\hat{h}_{j},\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{h}_{j},\hat{q}_{k}] \displaystyle=\delta_{jk}\hat{q}_{j},\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{h}_{j},\hat{p}_{k}] \displaystyle=-\delta_{jk}\hat{p}_{j}, \displaystyle[\hat{q}_{j},\hat{p}_{k}] \displaystyle=\delta_{jk}\hat{z},\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{x}_{j},\hat{q}_{k}] \displaystyle=0,\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{x}_{j},\hat{p}_{k}] \displaystyle=\delta_{jk}\hat{q}_{j},\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{y}_{j},\hat{q}_{k}] \displaystyle=\delta_{jk}\hat{p}_{j},\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{y}_{j},\hat{p}_{k}] \displaystyle=0, with \hat{z} being a central element. We can now define the three elements: \displaystyle\hat{h} \displaystyle:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\hat{h}_{j},\; \displaystyle\;\hat{x} \displaystyle:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\hat{x}_{j},\; \displaystyle\;\hat{x} \displaystyle:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\hat{y}_{j}. Then the commutation relations become: \displaystyle[\hat{h},\hat{x}] \displaystyle=2\hat{x},\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{h},\hat{y}] \displaystyle=-2\hat{y},\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{x},\hat{y}] \displaystyle=\hat{h},\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{h},\hat{q}_{j}] \displaystyle=\hat{q}_{j},\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{h},\hat{p}_{j}] \displaystyle=-\hat{p}_{j}, \displaystyle[\hat{q}_{j},\hat{p}_{k}] \displaystyle=\delta_{jk}\hat{z},\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{x},\hat{q}_{j}] \displaystyle=0,\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{x},\hat{p}_{j}] \displaystyle=\hat{q}_{j},\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{y},\hat{q}_{j}] \displaystyle=\hat{p}_{j},\; \displaystyle\;[\hat{y},\hat{p}_{k}] \displaystyle=0, where \hat{z} belongs to the center. Thus, comparing these bracket relations to those found in [161], one concludes \langle\{\hat{h},\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{p}_{j},\hat{q}_{j},\hat{z}\}_{j=1}^{m}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{}\cong\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{m}, which shows that the Schrödinger algebra can be faithfully realized within the skew-hermitian Weyl algebra \hat{A}_{m} . ∎ Corollary 111. The complex Schrödinger algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{m} can be faithfully realized within the Weyl algebra A_{m} . Proof. This is an immediate consequence of Proposition 110 and the observation that \hat{A}_{m} is a real subalgebra of the complex Weyl algebra A_{m} . ∎ Let us continue by discussing the physical relevance of this result. Bosonic systems, such as modes of the electromagnetic field [150], phononic excitations in crystals [14], or polaritons in many body physics [54], are typically modeled by a collection of bosonic harmonic oscillators. When analyzing the time evolution of such systems, one often performs a basis change that transforms the Hamiltonian into one describing a set of uncoupled harmonic oscillators [39]. A corresponding time-dependent Hamiltonian \hat{H}(t) for an m -mode bosonic system takes the form: \displaystyle\hat{H}(t) \displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\omega_{j}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}+s_{j}(t)\hat{a}_{j}^{2}+s_{j}^{*}(t)(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger})^{2}\right), (32) where \omega_{j}>0 are the oscillator frequencies, and s_{j}(t) complex-valued functions known as single-mode squeezing parameters [88, 72]. This Hamiltonian structure naturally involves quadratic combinations of creation and annihilation operators, i.e., precisely the same building blocks used in the realization of the Schrödinger algebra within the Weyl algebra presented in the proof of Proposition 110. The terms \omega_{j}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j} model a single free uncoupled harmonic oscillator, while the terms s_{j}(t)\hat{a}_{j}^{2}+s_{j}^{*}(t)(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger})^{2} correspond to active operations known as single-mode squeezing operators [72]. These operations are fundamental in continuous variable quantum information theory and quantum optics [2, 88]. The fact that this Hamiltonian models uncoupled oscillators is evident from the absence of cross-mode interaction terms, i.e., there are no products involving creation and annihilation operators from different modes. The Hamiltonian (32) can be expressed in terms of the operators \hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{j} , up to an additive term proportional to i\hat{\mathds{1}} (equivalently \hat{z} ), which corresponds to a physically irrelevant global phase factor. This phase does not affect observable quantities and can therefore be omitted in the analysis. The resulting expression is: \displaystyle i\hat{H}(t) \displaystyle=-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(2\mathrm{Im}(s_{j}(t))\hat{h}_{j}+\left(2\mathrm{Re}(s_{j}(t))+\omega_{j}\right)\hat{x}_{j}+\left(2\mathrm{Re}(s_{j}(t))-\omega_{j}\right)\hat{y}_{j}\right). For simplicity of exposition, we impose the assumptions s_{j}(t)=s_{k}(t)\equiv s(t) and \omega_{j}=\omega_{k}\equiv\omega for all t\in\mathbb{R} and j,k\in\{1,\ldots,m\} . These condition corresponds to a system of identical modes, which significantly reduces the complexity of the problem by introducing the symmetry among the oscillators. However, to broaden the applicability of the analysis, we incorporate displacement operator on each mode, as described in [72]. These operations introduce different mode-dependent shifts and are parameterized by complex displacement coefficients \alpha_{j} for each mode. This displacement operator acts as follows: \hat{\mathds{1}}\mapsto\hat{\mathds{1}} , \hat{a}_{j}\mapsto\hat{a}_{j}+\alpha_{j}\hat{\mathds{1}} , and \hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\mapsto\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}+\alpha_{j}^{*}\hat{\mathds{1}} . The resulting displaced Hamiltonian acquires additional linear terms in the operators \hat{p}_{j} and \hat{q}_{j} (resp. \hat{a}_{j} and \hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger} ) and reads consequently: \displaystyle i\hat{H}_{\mathrm{f}}(t) \displaystyle=-2\mathrm{Im}(s(t))\sum_{j=1}^{m}\hat{h}-\left(2\mathrm{Re}(s(t))-\omega\right)\hat{x}-\left(2\mathrm{Re}(s(t))-\omega\right)\hat{y}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\lambda_{j}(t)\hat{p}_{j}+\kappa_{j}(t)\hat{q}_{j}\right), modulo an irrelevant global phase factor proportional to \hat{z} . The real-valued functions \lambda_{j}(t),\kappa_{j}(t) depend on the displacement parameters \alpha_{j} , the squeezing parameter s(t) , as well as the frequency \omega . Their explicit form can be computed straightforwardly but is omitted here as it is not essential for the present discussion. Following the discussion in Sections V.3 and VI.5.4, the Lie algebra required to compute the time-evolution of this system via a factorization ansatz is generated by \mathcal{G}=\{\hat{h},\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{p}_{j},\hat{q}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m} , whose Lie closure satisfies, by the proof of Proposition 110, \langle\mathcal{G}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{}=\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{m} , underscoring the importance of the Schrödinger algebra in describing the dynamics of multi-mode bosonic systems. Moreover, it is clear that the simplifying assumptions s_{j}(t)=s_{k}(t)\equiv s(t) and \omega_{j}=\omega_{k}\equiv\omega for every t\in\mathbb{R} and j,k\in\{1,\ldots,m\} dramatically reduce the dimensionality of the problem by collapsing the system into a symmetric configuration. Without these assumptions, one would need to consider the full Lie algebra \langle\{\hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{j},\hat{p}_{j},\hat{q}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} , which is substantially larger (i.e., \dim(\langle\{\hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{j},\hat{p}_{j},\hat{q}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{})=5m+1 against \dim(\langle\{\hat{h},\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{p}_{j},\hat{q}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{})=2m+4 ) and more complex to handle computationally. However, using the graph-theoretic approach developed in this work it becomes evident that this Lie algebra is isomorphic to \displaystyle\langle\{\hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{j},\hat{p}_{j},\hat{q}_{j},\hat{z}\}_{j=1}^{m}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{}\cong\left(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{m}\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\right)\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{m}. (33) Let us explain this in more detail: First, one constructs the graph associated with \langle\{\hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{j},\hat{p}_{j},\hat{q}_{j},\hat{z}\}_{j=1}^{m}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} using the basis \{\hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{j},\hat{p}_{j},\hat{q}_{j},\hat{z}\}_{j=1}^{m} . This graph is minimal because the basis is not overcomplete. Next, one recognizes that, according to Lemma 74, Lemma 67 and Algorithm 3, the vertices labeled by the elements \{\hat{p}_{j},\hat{q}_{j},\hat{z}\}_{j=1}^{m} span a two-step nilpotent ideal, which is precisely the Heisenberg algebra \mathfrak{h}_{m} . Furthermore, Corollary 45, implies that the triples \{\hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{j}\} span, for every j\in\{1,\ldots,m\} , a simple three-dimensional subalgebra. Employing then the Bianchi classification [28], we know that there exist only two such algebras in the real case, namely \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}) and \mathfrak{su}(2) . However, Theorem 4.4 from [99] excludes the case \mathfrak{su}(2)\cong\langle\{\hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{j}\}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} , since these algebras can be realized within \hat{A}_{1} . Moreover, the commutator between two such algebras vanishes, i.e., [\langle\{\hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{j}\}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{},\langle\{\hat{h}_{k},\hat{x}_{k},\hat{y}_{k}\}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{}]=\{0\} if j\neq k , which shows that the subalgebra generated by all such triples is a direct sum of m copies of \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}) , i.e., \langle\{\hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{}\cong\bigoplus_{j=1}^{m}\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}). Since the graph is minimal and each algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}) is as simple and non-solvable Lie algebra, the Heisenberg algebra \mathfrak{h}_{m} forms the radical of the full Lie algebra \langle\{\hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{j},\hat{p}_{j},\hat{q}_{j},\hat{z}\}_{j=1}^{m}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} . This leads to the Levi-Mal’tsev decomposition stated in (33), as the radical does not form the center of the Lie algebra. Appendix D The Poincaré algebra The Poincaré group, also referred to as the inhomogeneous Lorentz group [169], comprises all coordinate transformations of Minkowski space that preserve the form of all physical laws [44]. This group is a ten-dimensional and non-abelian Lie group that is generated by the following operators: • The Hamiltonian operator H , • The momentum operators P_{1},P_{2},P_{3} , • The angular momentum operators J_{1},J_{2},J_{3} , • The three boost operators K_{1},K_{2},K_{3} . These ten elements span the Poincaré algebra \mathfrak{p}(1,3) [77], which is also denoted by \mathfrak{iso}(1,3) [82]. The commutation relations, and therefore the Lie bracket relations, among these basis elements are \displaystyle[J_{j},J_{k}] \displaystyle=i\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}\epsilon_{jk\ell}J_{\ell},\; \displaystyle\;[J_{j},K_{k}] \displaystyle=i\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}\epsilon_{jk\ell}K_{\ell},\; \displaystyle\;[K_{j},K_{k}] \displaystyle=-i\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}\epsilon_{jk\ell}J_{\ell}, \displaystyle[J_{j},P_{k}] \displaystyle=i\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}\epsilon_{jk\ell}P_{\ell},\; \displaystyle\;[K_{j},P_{k}] \displaystyle=-iH\delta_{jk},\; \displaystyle\;[K_{j},H] \displaystyle=-iP_{j}, while all other commutators vanish [169]. The corresponding graph G(V,E) is depicted in Figure 29. Figure 29: Depiction of the graph G(V,E) associated with the Poincaré algebra \mathfrak{p}(1,3) utilizing the basis \{H,P_{j},J_{j},K_{j}\}_{j=1}^{3} . When considered over the complex numbers, the subalgebra spanned by the three boost and three angular momentum operators is the Lorentz algebra \mathfrak{so}(3;1)_{\mathbb{C}}\cong\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}})\oplus\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}}) (cf. Section V.2). The application of Lemma 74 to the graph G(V,E) implies that the three vertices labeled with the three momentum operators P_{1},P_{2},P_{3} and the energy operator H span together an ideal of the Poincaré algebra \mathfrak{p}(1,3) . Furthermore, since the subgraph containing only vertices and edges labeled by these four operators contains no edges, it follows from Lemma 50, that the Lie algebra \langle\{P_{1},P_{2},P_{3},H\}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} is abelian. Moreover, because the graph G(V,E) is minimal and the algebra \mathfrak{so}(3;1) is semisimple, and therefore non-solvable, one has \langle\{P_{1},P_{2},P_{3},H\}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{}=\operatorname{rad}(\mathfrak{p}(1,3)) . Thus, we can conclude that \displaystyle\mathfrak{p}(1,3)=(\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}})\oplus\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}}))\ltimes\left(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{4}\mathfrak{a}\right)\cong(\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}})\oplus\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}}))\ltimes\mathbb{C}^{4}, where \mathfrak{a} denotes the one-dimensional abelian Lie algebra isomorphic to the complex numbers, i.e., \mathfrak{a}\cong\mathbb{C} . The Poincaré algebra plays a fundamental role in encoding the Lorentz invariance of inertial systems within the relativistic framework. However, in the classical limit (i.e., for velocities v that satisfy (v/c)^{2}\ll 1 , where c is the speed of light) this algebra must reduce to the Galileo group, thus capturing the Galileo invariance of classical mechanical systems. To understand this demand, one needs to reintroduce the relevant physical constants into the bracket relations, rather than working in natural units, where the speed of light is normally set to c\equiv 1 [96]. The correspondingly modified relations read then: \displaystyle[J_{j}^{\prime},J_{k}^{\prime}] \displaystyle=i\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}\epsilon_{jk\ell}J_{\ell}^{\prime},\; \displaystyle\;[J_{j}^{\prime},K_{k}^{\prime}] \displaystyle=i\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}\epsilon_{jk\ell}K_{\ell}^{\prime},\; \displaystyle\;[K_{j}^{\prime},K_{k}^{\prime}] \displaystyle=-\frac{1}{c^{2}}i\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}\epsilon_{jk\ell}J_{\ell}^{\prime}, \displaystyle[J_{j}^{\prime},P_{k}^{\prime}] \displaystyle=i\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}\epsilon_{jk\ell}P_{\ell}^{\prime},\; \displaystyle\;[K_{j}^{\prime},P_{k}^{\prime}] \displaystyle=-\frac{1}{c^{2}}iH^{\prime}\delta_{jk},\; \displaystyle\;[K_{j}^{\prime},H^{\prime}] \displaystyle=-iP_{j}, as discussed in [90], where the real form of this algebra is considered. This real form can be obtained, by multiplying each operator with -i . As shown in [90], taking the classical limit c\to\infty yields the Galileo algebra, which obeys the same bracket relations as above, except that [K_{j}^{\prime},K_{k}^{\prime}]=0=[K_{j}^{\prime},P_{k}^{\prime}] . In this case, the operators P_{j}^{\prime} represent spatial translations, J_{j}^{\prime} represent rotations, K_{j}^{\prime} correspond to Galilean boosts, and H represents time translations. Note that this Galileo algebra is also known as the reduced classical Galileo algebra A\bar{G}_{1}(3) [131]. The corresponding graph can be found in Figure 30. Figure 30: Depiction of the graph G(V,E) associated with the Galileo algebra utilizing the basis \{H^{\prime},P_{j}^{\prime},J_{j}^{\prime},K_{j}^{\prime}\}_{j=1}^{3} in the non-relativistic limit c\to\infty . This graph can again be analyzed using the graph-theoretic framework developed in this work. Corollary 45 guarantees that this Galileo algebra contains a simple complex three-dimensional Lie subalgebra spanned by the three rotation operators \{J_{j}^{\prime}\}_{j=1}^{3} . The only such algebra is \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}}) [87], i.e., \langle\{J_{j}^{\prime}\}_{j=1}^{3}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{}\cong\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}}) . Furthermore, the radical of this Galileo algebra, which we denote by \mathfrak{ga}:=\langle\{H^{\prime},P_{j}^{\prime},J_{j}^{\prime},K_{j}^{\prime}\}_{j=1}^{3}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} , is given by \operatorname{rad}(\mathfrak{ga})=\langle\{H^{\prime},P_{j}^{\prime},K_{j}^{\prime}\}_{j=1}^{3}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} , since the corresponding vertices span by Lemma 74 an ideal, which is by Theorem 70 nilpotent and therefore solvable. This ideal is furthermore the maximal solvable ideal of \mathfrak{ga} , since the graph is minimal and the three remaining vertices span a simple Lie algebra. Moreover, by computing the lower central series of the associated graphs, we find that the radical is a two-step nilpotent Lie algebra, where the first derived algebra is three-dimensional. The edge structure of the graph associated with the radical allows also a direct identification of \operatorname{rad}(\mathfrak{ga})=\mathfrak{tn}_{3,7_{A}} , where \mathfrak{tn}_{3,7_{A}} denotes the indecomposable two-step nilpotent Lie algebra labeled by 3,7_{A} in [155]. This identification follows because every edge terminating at a sinkhole either originates from the vertex labeled by H^{\prime} or is itself labeled by H^{\prime} . The Levi-Mal’tsev decomposition theorem allows therefore the conclusion that: \displaystyle\mathfrak{ga}\cong\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}})\ltimes\mathfrak{tn}_{3,7_{A}}. Appendix E Generalizing the approach using Lie algebra gradations This appendix is devoted to discussing claims presented in Section VI.4 of the main text, where the focus was on generalizing the graph-theoretic framework developed earlier to broader classes if Lie algebras using Lie algebra gradations by abelian magmas. In particular, we explore the validity of Conjecture 103 for real Lie algebras of low dimension as an illustrative case. The following proposition below formalizes a simple yet useful idea that will play a role in the verification strategy employed later in this appendix. Proposition 112. Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional Lie algebra. If \mathfrak{g} can be decomposed as the direct sum of graph-admissible Lie algebras, then \mathfrak{g} is graph-admissible. Furthermore, if \mathfrak{g} can be decomposed as the direct sum of minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras, then \mathfrak{g} is minimal-graph-admissible. Proof. The assertion is straightforward and can be verified directly from the given definitions. ∎ We now aim to verify Conjecture 103 for real Lie algebras in the low-dimensional case. Specifically: Proposition 113. Let \mathfrak{g} be a real Lie algebra with \dim(\mathfrak{g})\leq 4 . Then, there exists an abelian magma (\mathcal{M},\delta) such that \mathfrak{g} is \mathcal{M} -magma-graded and the graph G_{\mathfrak{g}}(V,E) associated with the pair (\mathfrak{g},(\mathcal{M},\delta)) , obtained via Algorithm 5, faithfully represents the inner structure of \mathfrak{g} , in the sense that the following assertions hold: (i) Solvability. The Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} is non-solvable if and only if G_{\mathfrak{g}}(V,E) contains a self-contained subgraph G_{W}\equiv G_{W}(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) that is induced by a closed directed walk W . (ii) Derived Series. The sequence of graphs obtained via Algorithm 8 can be associated with the Lie algebras of the derived series of \mathfrak{g} , in the sense that for every \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , the direct sum of the subspaces labeling the vertices of the graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G_{\mathfrak{g}}(V,E) coincide with derived algebras \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} . (iii) Nilpotency. The Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} is nilpotent if and only if G(V,E) contains no closed directed walk. (iv) Lower central series. The sequence of graphs obtained via Algorithm 9 can be associated with the lower central series of \mathfrak{g} , in the sense that for every \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} the direct sum of the subspaces labeling the vertices of the graphs \mathcal{C}^{\ell}G_{\mathfrak{g}}(V,E) coincide with Lie algebras \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} of the lower central series. (v) Ideals. If W\subseteq V is a subset that satisfies the ideal-graph-property, then the direct sum of the subspaces labeling the vertices in W span an ideal of \mathfrak{g} . (vi) Simplicity. If \mathfrak{g} is simple, then there exists a closed directed walk W that induces a subgraph G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) with V=\tilde{V} . (vii) Semisimplicity. If \mathfrak{g} is semisimple, then every vertex is part of a closed directed walk that induces a self-contained subgraph. This proposition can be verified by considering every real Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} with \dim(\mathfrak{g})\leq 4 . For this purpose, we employ Mubarakzyanov’s classification of low-dimensional real Lie algebras and adopt the same notation [130, 140]. In particular, we denote the basis elements of the corresponding n -dimensional Lie algebras as e_{1},\ldots,e_{n} and only adduce non-trivial Lie brackets. Proof. Establishing the existence of an abelian magma (\mathcal{M},\delta) for grading a Lie algebra in the following decompositions is straightforward: it can be constructed by assigning a suitable collection of basis elements (and, therefore, the corresponding subspace) of the Lie algebra to a distinct element of \mathcal{M} , and defining \delta according to the Lie bracket structure. This ensures that the grading accurately reflects the structure of the algebra. * One-dimensional case. In this case, only the abelian Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{1}\cong\mathbb{R} exists [28]. This Lie algebra is clearly minimal-graph-admissible, and the claim follows directly from Proposition 98. * Two-dimensional case. In this case there exist only two distinct real Lie algebras: the abelian Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{l}_{1}\cong\mathbb{R}^{2} and the affine Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{2,1}\cong\mathfrak{aff}(1,\mathbb{R}) [9]. The former is trivially minimal-graph-admissible, while the latter satisfies the non-trivial relation [e_{1},e_{2}]=e_{2} [9], making it minimal-graph-admissible as well. Therefore, analogous to the one-dimensional case, the claim follows from Proposition 98. * Three-dimensional case. In this case, the procedure can be dived into the following nine subcases: • \mathfrak{l}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{l}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{l}_{1} : This Lie algebra is abelian and therefore clearly minimal-graph-admissible. The claim follows consequently directly from Proposition 98. • \mathfrak{l}_{2,1}\oplus\mathfrak{l}_{1} : This Lie algebras is, by Proposition 112, obtained as the direct sum of minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras that are themselves minimal-graph-admissible. Thus, the claim follows from Proposition 98. • \mathfrak{l}_{3,1} : This Lie algebra is known as the Heisenberg algebra \mathfrak{h}_{1} [81, 103] and admits a basis \{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\} such that [e_{2},e_{3}]=e_{1} . Thus, \mathfrak{l}_{3,1} is minimal-graph-admissible, and the claim follows from Proposition 98. • \mathfrak{l}_{3,2} . This Lie algebra admits a basis \{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\} such that [e_{1},e_{3}]=e_{1} , and [e_{2},e_{3}]=e_{1}+e_{2} . Thus, we can choose \mathcal{M}=\{1,2\} , \mathfrak{g}_{1}:=\spn\{e_{1},e_{2}\} , \mathfrak{g}_{2}:=\spn\{e_{3}\} , and define \delta:\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M} , such that [\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{1}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{2} , [\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2}]=[\mathfrak{g}_{2},\mathfrak{g}_{1}]=\mathfrak{g}_{1} , and [\mathfrak{g}_{2},\mathfrak{g}_{2}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{1} , where [\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{1}]=\{0\}=[\mathfrak{g}_{2},\mathfrak{g}_{2}] . The resulting graph G_{\mathfrak{l}_{3,2}}(V,E) therefore reads: We can now verify the assertions laid out before: (i) The labeled directed graph G_{\mathfrak{l}_{3,2}}(V,E) contains no self-contained subgraph that is induced by a closed direct walk, and therefore the Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{3,2} is solvable [140], thus conforming the validity of claim (i) in this case. (ii) The derived graphs of G_{\mathfrak{l}_{3,2}}(V,E) are given by: Statement (ii) now claims that the derived graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G_{\mathfrak{l}{{}_{3,2}}}(V,E) of G_{\mathfrak{l}{{}_{3,2}}}(V,E) correspond to the derived series of \mathfrak{l}_{3,2} . Specifically: \mathcal{D}^{0}\mathfrak{l}_{3,2}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}=\mathfrak{l}_{3,2} , \mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{l}_{3,2}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\cong\mathfrak{l}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{l}_{1}\cong\mathbb{R}^{2} , and \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{l}_{3,2}=\{0\} . This is straightforward to verify. (iii) The graph G_{\mathfrak{l}_{3,2}}(V,E) contains a closed direct walk and the Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{3,2} is non-nilpotent, since [\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2},\mathfrak{g}_{1}]=\mathfrak{g}_{1} implies that \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{l}_{3,2}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{1} for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1} . (iv) The lower central series of the graph G_{\mathfrak{l}_{3,2}}(V,E) is given by: Statement (iv) now claims that the lower central series of graphs \mathcal{C}^{\ell}G_{\mathfrak{l}{{}_{3,2}}}(V,E) correspond to the algebras of the lower central series of \mathfrak{l}_{3,2} . Specifically: \mathcal{C}^{0}\mathfrak{l}_{3,2}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}=\mathfrak{l}_{3,2} , and \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{l}_{3,2}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\cong\mathbb{R}^{2} . This is correct and easy to verify. (v) The only subsets of V that satisfy the ideal graph property are \{\mathfrak{g}_{1}\} and \{\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2}\} . It is straightforward to verify that the spaces \mathfrak{g}_{1} and \mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2} are ideals of \mathfrak{g}_{3,2} , confirming the validity of claim (v) in this case. (vi) There exists no closed directed walk W in G_{\mathfrak{l}_{3,2}}(V,E) that induces a subgraph G_{\mathfrak{l}_{3,2}}(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) with \tilde{V}=V , and thus \mathfrak{l}_{3,2} is not simple [140], confirming the validity of claim (vi) in this case. (vii) The vertex labeled with the space \mathfrak{g}_{1} is never part of a closed directed walk and thus never part of a closed directed walk that induces a self-contained subgraph G_{\mathfrak{l}_{3,2}}(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) . Moreover, \mathfrak{l}_{3,2} is not semisimple, as it is not a simple Lie algebra [140], confirming the validity of claim (vii) in this case. • \mathfrak{l}_{3,3} : This Lie algebra admits a basis \{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\} such that [e_{1},e_{3}]=e_{1} and [e_{2},e_{3}]=e_{2} . Thus, \mathfrak{l}_{3,3} is minimal-graph-admissible and the claim follows from Proposition 98. • \mathfrak{l}_{3,4}^{(\alpha)} : In the special case \alpha=-1 , this Lie algebra coincides with the Poincaré algebra \mathfrak{p}(1,1) [132], and this family generally admits a basis \{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\} such that [e_{1},e_{3}]=e_{1} and [e_{2},e_{3}]=\alpha e_{2} with -1\leq\alpha<1 and \alpha\neq 0 . Thus, \mathfrak{l}_{3,4}^{(\alpha)} is minimal-graph-admissible, and the claim follows from Proposition 98. • \mathfrak{l}_{3,5}^{(\beta)} : This family of Lie algebras admits a basis \{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\} such that [e_{1},e_{3}]=\beta e_{1}-e_{2} and [e_{2},e_{3}]=e_{1}+\beta e_{2} with \beta\geq 0 . Thus, we can choose \mathcal{M}=\{1,2\} , \mathfrak{g}_{1}:=\spn\{e_{1},e_{2}\} , \mathfrak{g}_{2}:=\spn\{e_{3}\} , and define \delta:\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M} , such that [\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{1}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{2} , [\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2}]=[\mathfrak{g}_{2},\mathfrak{g}_{1}]=\mathfrak{g}_{1} , and [\mathfrak{g}_{2},\mathfrak{g}_{2}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{1} , where [\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{1}]=\{0\}=[\mathfrak{g}_{2},\mathfrak{g}_{2}] . The resulting graph G_{\mathfrak{g}_{3,5}^{(\beta)}}(V,E) is consequently equivalent to the graph G_{\mathfrak{l}_{3,2}}(V,E) obtained earlier for the Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{3,2} . Therefore, we only need to confirm that the same assertions as for the Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{3,2} hold: (i) The Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{3,5}^{(\beta)} is solvable [140], confirming the validity of claim (i) in this case. Specifically: \mathcal{D}^{0}\mathfrak{l}_{4,7}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{3}=\mathfrak{l}_{4,7} , \mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{l}_{4,7}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2} , \mathcal{D}^{2}\mathfrak{l}_{4,7}=\mathfrak{g}_{1} , and \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{l}_{4,7}=\{0\} for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 3} . It is straightforward to verify this. (iii) The graph G_{\mathfrak{l}_{4,7}}(V,E) contains at least one closed directed walk and is not nilpotent, since [\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{3},\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}]=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2} implies that \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{l}_{4,7}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}\neq\{0\} for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1} . (iv) The lower central series of the graph G_{\mathfrak{l}_{4,7}}(V,E) is given by: Statement (iv) now claims that the lower central series of graphs \mathcal{C}^{\ell}G_{\mathfrak{l}_{4,7}}(V,E) correspond to the algebras of the lower central series of \mathfrak{l}_{4,7} . Specifically: \mathcal{C}^{0}\mathfrak{l}_{4,7}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{3}=\mathfrak{l}_{4,7} and \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{l}_{4,7}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2} for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1} . This is straightforward to confirm. (v) The only non-empty subsets of V that satisfy the ideal-graph-property are: \{\mathfrak{g}_{1}\} , \{\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2}\} , and \{\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2},\mathfrak{g}_{3}\} . It is straightforward to verify that the corresponding spaces \mathfrak{g}_{1} , \mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2} , and \mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{3} are ideals of \mathfrak{l}_{4,7} . (vi) There exists no closed directed walk W in G_{\mathfrak{l}_{4,7}}(V,E) that induces a subgraph G_{\mathfrak{l}_{4,7}}(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) with \tilde{V}=V , and thus \mathfrak{l}_{4,7} is not simple, as it is solvable. This confirms the validity of claim (vi) in this case. (vii) The vertex labeled with the space \mathfrak{g}_{3} is never part of a closed directed walk, and thus never part of a closed directed walk that induces a self-contained subgraph G_{\mathfrak{l}_{4,7}}(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) . Moreover, \mathfrak{l}_{4,7} is not semisimple, as it is a solvable Lie algebra, confirming the validity of claim (vii) in this case. • \mathfrak{l}_{4,8}^{(\beta)} : This family of Lie algebras admits a basis \{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4}\} such that [e_{2},e_{3}]=e_{1} , [e_{1},e_{4}]=(1+\beta)e_{1} , [e_{2},e_{4}]=e_{2} , and [e_{3},e_{4}]=\beta e_{3} , where -1\leq\beta\leq 1 . These Lie algebras are consequently minimal-graph-admissible and the claim follows by Proposition 98. • \mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)} : This family of Lie algebras admits a basis \{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4}\} such that [e_{2},e_{3}]=e_{1} , [e_{1},e_{4}]=2\alpha e_{1} , [e_{2},e_{4}]=\alpha e_{2}-e_{3} , and [e_{3},e_{4}]=e_{2}+\alpha e_{3} , where \alpha>0 . Thus, we can choose \mathcal{M}=\{1,2,3\} , \mathfrak{g}_{1}:=\spn\{e_{1}\} , \mathfrak{g}_{2}:=\spn\{e_{2},e_{3}\} , \mathfrak{g}_{3}:=\spn\{e_{4}\} , and define \delta:\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M} such that [\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{3}]=\mathfrak{g}_{1}=[\mathfrak{g}_{3},\mathfrak{g}_{1}] , [\mathfrak{g}_{2},\mathfrak{g}_{2}]=\mathfrak{g}_{1} , [\mathfrak{g}_{2},\mathfrak{g}_{3}]=\mathfrak{g}_{2}=[\mathfrak{g}_{3},\mathfrak{g}_{2}] , while [\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{k}]=\{0\} for all remaining pairs of (j,k)\in\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M} . The resulting graph G_{\mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)}}(V,E) associated with \mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)} is consequently: We can now verify the following assertions: (i) The labeled directed graph G_{\mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)}}(V,E) contains no self-contained subgraph that is induced by a closed direct walk, and thus the Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)} is solvable [140], confirming the validity of claim (i) in this case. (ii) The derived graphs of \mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)} are: Statement (ii) now claims that the derived graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G_{\mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)}}(V,E) of G_{\mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)}}(V,E) correspond to the derived series of \mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)} . Specifically: \mathcal{D}^{0}\mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{3}=\mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)} , \mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2} , \mathcal{D}^{2}\mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)}=\mathfrak{g}_{1} , and \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)}=\{0\} for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 3} . This is straightforward to verify. (iii) The graph G_{\mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)}}(V,E) contains at least one closed directed walk and is not nilpotent, since [\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{3},\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}]=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2} implies that \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}\neq\{0\} for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1} . (iv) The lower central series of the graph G_{\mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)}}(V,E) is given by: Statement (iv) now claims that the lower central series of graphs \mathcal{C}^{\ell}G_{\mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)}}(V,E) correspond to the algebras of the lower central series of \mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)} . Specifically: \mathcal{C}^{0}\mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{3}=\mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)} and \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2} for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1} . It is straightforward to confirm this. (v) The only non-empty subsets of V that satisfy the ideal-graph-property are: \{\mathfrak{g}_{1}\} , \{\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2}\} , and \{\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2},\mathfrak{g}_{3}\} . It is straightforward to verify that the corresponding spaces \mathfrak{g}_{1} , \mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2} , and \mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{3} are ideals of \mathfrak{l}_{4,7} . (vi) There exists no closed directed walk W in G_{\mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)}}(V,E) that induces a subgraph G_{\mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)}}(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) with \tilde{V}=V and thus\mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)} is not simple, as it is solvable. This confirms the validity of claim (vi) in this case. (vii) The vertex labeled with the space \mathfrak{g}_{3} is never part of a closed directed walk, and thus never part of a closed directed walk that induces a self-contained subgraph G_{\mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)}}(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) . Moreover, \mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(\alpha)} is not semisimple, as it is a solvable Lie algebra, confirming the validity of claim (vii) in this case. • \mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(0)} : This Lie algebra admits a basis \{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4}\} such that [e_{2},e_{3}]=e_{1} , [e_{2},e_{4}]=-e_{3} , and [e_{3},e_{4}]=e_{2} . Therefore, the Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{4,9}^{(0)} is minimal-graph-admissible and the claim follows from Proposition 98. • \mathfrak{l}_{4,10} : This Lie algebra admits a basis \{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4}\} such that [e_{1},e_{3}]=e_{1} , [e_{2},e_{3}]=e_{2} , [e_{1},e_{4}]=-e_{2} , and [e_{2},e_{4}]=e_{1} , making it minimal-graph-admissible and the claim follows from Proposition 98. ∎ To better understand Conjecture 105, we now consider, as an illustrative example, the family of real Lie algebras L_{\alpha}^{3} , spanned by the three basis elements \{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\} that satisfy the following Lie bracket relations: \displaystyle[x_{1},x_{2}] \displaystyle=0,\; \displaystyle\;[x_{1},x_{3}] \displaystyle=-x_{2},\; \displaystyle\;[e_{2},e_{3}] \displaystyle=-\alpha x_{1}-x_{2}, (34) where \alpha\in\mathbb{R} is a real parameter. Our objective is to determine all possible labeled directed graphs G(V,E) that can be associated with L_{\alpha}^{3} , under the condition that the corresponding \mathcal{M} -magma-grading exhibits the finest granularity. Let us begin with the preliminary case \alpha=0 . Proposition 114. The real Lie algebra L_{0}^{3} satisfies \operatorname{fg}(L_{0}^{3})=3 , and every \mathcal{M} -magma-gradation of L_{0}^{3} that satisfies \operatorname{gra}(L_{0}^{3},\mathcal{M})=\operatorname{fg}(L_{0}^{3}) produces, via Algorithm 5, a graph that is equivalent to one of Type III, V, VII, or XI. Proof. For \alpha=0 , the bracket relations (34) read [x_{1},x_{2}]=0 , [x_{1},x_{3}]=-x_{2} , and [x_{2},x_{3}]=-x_{2} , making the Lie algebra L_{0}^{3} minimal-graph-admissible. By Lemma 101, one has \operatorname{fg}(L_{0}^{3})=\dim(L_{0}^{3})=3 , and thus we need to consider bases \mathcal{B} of L_{0}^{3} that satisfy the relations (7). Since L_{0}^{3} is clearly non-abelian, the basis \mathcal{B} must contain at least one element \tilde{x}_{2}\in\mathcal{B} such that \tilde{x}_{2}\propto x_{2} . This follows from the observation that [x,y]=0 or [x,y]\propto\tilde{x}_{2} for all x,y\in L_{0}^{3} , while [L_{0}^{3},L_{0}^{3}]=\spn\{x_{2}\} . Consequently, if G(V,E) denotes the corresponding labeled directed graph, then \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=\tilde{x}_{2} for all e\in E\neq\emptyset , which excludes graphs of Type I, IV, VI, VIII, IX, and X. This leaves only graphs of Type II, III, V, VII, and XI. We now examine these cases individually: • Type II: This would require that the Lie bracket of \tilde{x}_{2} and the two remaining basis elements \tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{3}\in\mathcal{B} vanishes identically. However, for \mathcal{B} to span L_{0}^{3} , at least one of these elements must have non-trivial support in \spn\{x_{3}\} . In that situation, the Lie bracket of \tilde{x}_{2} and the corresponding element does not vanish, leading to a contradiction. Therefore the graph G(V,E) cannot be of Type II. • Type III: Consider the basis \tilde{x}_{1}:=x_{3} , \tilde{x}_{2}:=x_{1}-x_{2} , and \tilde{x}_{3}:=x_{2} . This basis produces, via Algorithm 1, a graph of Type III, because the Lie brackets satisfy: \displaystyle[\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}] \displaystyle=0,\; \displaystyle\;[\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{3}] \displaystyle=-\tilde{x}_{3},\; \displaystyle\;[\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}] \displaystyle=0. • Type V: Consider the basis \tilde{x_{1}}:=x_{3}+2x_{1}+x_{2} , \tilde{x}_{2}:=x_{3}+x_{1}+2x_{2} , and \tilde{x}_{3}:=x_{2} . These elements yield, via Algorithm 1, a graph of Type V, since: \displaystyle[\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}] \displaystyle=(1+2-2-1)=0,\; \displaystyle\;[\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{3}] \displaystyle=-\tilde{x}_{3},\; \displaystyle\;[\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}] \displaystyle=-\tilde{x}_{3}. • Type VII: Consider the basis \tilde{x_{1}}:=x_{3} , \tilde{x}_{2}:=x_{1} , and \tilde{x}_{3}:=x_{2} . The resulting graph is of Type VII, because: \displaystyle[\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}] \displaystyle=-\tilde{x}_{3},\; \displaystyle\;[\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{3}] \displaystyle=-\tilde{x}_{3},\; \displaystyle\;[\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}] \displaystyle=0. • Type XI: Consider the basis \tilde{x_{1}}:=x_{3} , \tilde{x}_{2}:=x_{3}+x_{1} , and \tilde{x}_{3}:=x_{2} . This basis produces a graph of Type XI, as: \displaystyle[\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}] \displaystyle=-\tilde{x}_{3},\; \displaystyle\;[\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{3}] \displaystyle=-\tilde{x}_{3},\; \displaystyle\;[\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}] \displaystyle=-\tilde{x}_{3}. ∎ Proposition 115. The real Lie algebra L_{\alpha}^{3} for \alpha>-1/4 and \alpha\notin\{0,1\} , satisfies \operatorname{fg}(L_{\alpha}^{3})=3 . Moreover, every \mathcal{M} -magma-gradation of L_{\alpha}^{3} for which \operatorname{gra}(L_{\alpha}^{3},\mathcal{M})=\operatorname{fg}(L_{\alpha}^{3}) produces, via Algorithm 5, a graph that is equivalent to one of Type VI. Proof. From the proof of Theorem 17 we know that L_{\alpha}^{3} is minimal-graph-admissible when \alpha>-1/4 . Consequently, by Lemma 101, one has \operatorname{fg}(L_{\alpha}^{3})=\dim(L_{\alpha}^{3})=3 for all \alpha>-1/4 . We therefore consider a basis \mathcal{B}=\{\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}\} satisfying the relations (7). Since L_{\alpha}^{3} is solvable [56], the corresponding labeled directed graph G(V,E) cannot, according to Theorem 62, be of Type XI or X. Moreover, because L_{\alpha}^{3} is not nilpotent, Theorem 70 excludes graphs of Type I and II. For latter convenience, define \mathfrak{v}:=\spn\{x_{1},x_{2}\} . Since \mathcal{B} is a basis of L_{\alpha}^{3} , we may assume, without loss of generality, that \tilde{x}_{1}=x_{3}+v_{1} , where v_{1}\in\mathfrak{v} . Suppose another element y\in\mathcal{B} also satisfies y\notin\mathfrak{v} . In this case, we can likewise assume that \tilde{x}_{2}=x_{3}+v_{2} , where v_{2}\in\mathfrak{v} . By inspection of the bracket relations (34), it is clear that \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{1}}(L_{\alpha}^{3})\subseteq\mathfrak{v}\supseteq\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}(L_{\alpha}^{3}) , and consequently \tilde{x}_{3}\in\mathfrak{v} . Moreover, every edge e\in E must satisfy \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=\tilde{x}_{3} . Additionally, one has [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{3}]\neq 0\neq[\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}] , where [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{3}]\propto\tilde{x}_{3} and [\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}]\propto\tilde{x}_{3} . hence, the corresponding graph can only be of Type V or XI. Let us consider these cases separately: • Type V: This case requires [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}]=0 . We can express the corresponding elements as \tilde{x}_{1}=x_{3}+c_{11}x_{1}+c_{12}x_{2} and \tilde{x}_{2}=x_{3}+c_{21}x_{1}+c_{22}x_{2} , where c_{11},c_{12},c_{21},c_{22}\in\mathbb{R} . We the Lie bracket of these elements and impose that it vanishes, obtaining \displaystyle[\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}] \displaystyle=c_{21}x_{2}+c_{22}(\alpha x_{1}+x_{2})-c_{11}x_{2}-c_{12}(\alpha x_{1}+x_{2})=0. Since the set \{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\} forms a basis of L_{\alpha}^{3} , it follows that c_{22}=c_{12} (because \alpha\neq 0 ), and consequently also c_{21}=c_{12} . This, however, would imply that \tilde{x}_{1}=\tilde{x}_{2} which is not allowed. Therefore, the corresponding graph cannot be of Type V. • Type XI: We begin by noting that the vectors v_{\pm}:=-\lambda_{\mp}x_{1}+x_{2} , with \lambda_{\pm}:=(1\pm\sqrt{1+4\alpha})/2\neq 0 are the two simultaneous eigenvectors of \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{1}}|_{\mathfrak{v}} and \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{3}}|_{\mathfrak{v}} when restricted to the space \mathfrak{v} . This can be verified by checking the the characteristic polynomial of both \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{1}}|_{\mathfrak{v}} and \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}|_{\mathfrak{v}} is given by X^{2}-X-\alpha . The roots of this polynomial are \lambda_{\pm} . The form of the eigenvectors can be verified by a straightforward computation, where it is helpful to notice that 1-\lambda_{\pm}=\lambda_{\mp} and \alpha=-\lambda_{+}\lambda_{-} . Since [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{3}],[\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}]\in\mathfrak{v}\setminus\{0\} , one must have that \tilde{x}_{3} is an eigenvector of \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{1}}|_{\mathfrak{v}} and \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}|_{\mathfrak{v}} , in order for \mathcal{B} to satisfy (7). As noted earlier, the Lie bracket [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}] vanishes only if \tilde{x}_{1}=\tilde{x}_{2} , which is prohibited by the condition that \mathcal{B}=\{\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}\} forms a basis of L_{\alpha}^{3} . Thus, one must have [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}]=\kappa\tilde{x}_{3} with \kappa\in\mathbb{R}^{*} for \mathcal{B} to satisfy (7). Writing \tilde{x}_{1}=x_{3}+c_{11}x_{1}+c_{12}x_{2} , \tilde{x}_{2}=x_{3}+c_{21}x_{1}+c_{22}x_{2} for real coefficients c_{11},c_{12},c_{21},c_{22}\in\mathbb{R} , we have \displaystyle[\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}] \displaystyle=\alpha(c_{22}-c_{12})x_{1}+(c_{21}-c_{11}+(c_{22}-c_{12}))x_{2}. Matching this result with \kappa(-\lambda_{\pm}x_{1}+x_{2}) yields the two equations \alpha(c_{22}-c_{12})=-\lambda_{\pm}\kappa and c_{21}-c_{11}=(1+\lambda_{\pm}/\alpha)\kappa . However, since \mathcal{B} forms a basis, the equation \kappa_{1}\tilde{x}_{1}+\kappa_{2}\tilde{x}_{2}+\kappa_{3}\tilde{x_{3}}=0 can only have the trivial solution \kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}=\kappa_{3}=0 . Expanding the elements \tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3} in terms of the three basis elements x_{1},x_{2},x_{3} yields the following system of equations: \displaystyle\left\{\begin{matrix}[l]0&=\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2},\\ 0&=c_{11}\kappa_{1}+c_{21}\kappa_{2}-\lambda_{\pm}\kappa_{3},\\ 0&=c_{12}\kappa_{1}+c_{22}\kappa_{2}+\kappa_{3},\end{matrix}\right. which can be rewritten in matrix form as follows: \displaystyle\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}1&1&0\\ c_{11}&c_{21}&-\lambda_{\pm}\\ c_{12}&c_{22}&1\end{pmatrix}}_{=:\boldsymbol{M}}\begin{pmatrix}\kappa_{1}\\ \kappa_{2}\\ \kappa_{3}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\ 0\\ 0\end{pmatrix}. Thus, \det(\boldsymbol{M})=0 is required. However, explicit calculations yield the following expression for the determinant: \displaystyle\det(\boldsymbol{M}) \displaystyle=c_{21}+\lambda_{\pm}c_{22}-(c_{11}+c_{12}\lambda_{\pm})=(c_{21}-c_{11})+\lambda_{\pm}(c_{22}-c_{12})=\left(1+\frac{\lambda_{\pm}}{\alpha}\right)\kappa-\frac{\lambda_{\pm}^{2}}{\alpha}\kappa=-\frac{\kappa}{\alpha}\left(\lambda_{\pm}^{2}-\lambda_{\pm}-\alpha\right) \displaystyle=-\frac{\kappa}{\alpha}(1-\alpha). To obtain this result, one needs to recall that the eigenvalues \lambda_{\pm} are the solutions to the equation x^{2}-x-1=0 . Thus, \det(\boldsymbol{M})=0 only if \alpha=1 , which is not allowed in this case. Therefore, the corresponding graph cannot be of Type XI. This leaves us with the case where \tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}\in\mathfrak{v} . In this situation, it is clear that [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}]\neq 0\neq[\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{3}] , while [\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}]=0 . Since \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{3}}(L_{\alpha}^{3})\subseteq\mathfrak{v} , the remaining possibilities are: (a) [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}]\propto\tilde{x}_{2} and [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{3}]\propto\tilde{x}_{3} ; (b) [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}]\propto\tilde{x}_{3} and [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{3}]\propto\tilde{x}_{2} ; or (c) [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}]\propto\tilde{x}_{3} and [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{3}]\propto\tilde{x}_{3} (or [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}]\propto\tilde{x}_{2} and [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{3}]\propto\tilde{x}_{2} ). Case (c) is impossible. To see this, one needs to recognize \tilde{x}_{3} is an eigenvector of \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{1}}|_{\mathfrak{v}} corresponding to a non-zero eigenvalue, and \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{1}}|_{\mathfrak{v}}^{2}(\tilde{x}_{2})\propto\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{1}}|_{\mathfrak{v}}(\tilde{x}_{2})\propto\tilde{x}_{3} . However, since \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{1}}|_{\mathfrak{v}} is invertible, when restricted to \mathfrak{v} , one must have \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{1}}|_{\mathfrak{v}}(\tilde{x}_{2})\propto\tilde{x}_{2}\propto\tilde{x}_{3} , which is prohibited by Definition 16. Case (b) can be ruled out by a similar argument: a direct calculation shows that \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{1}}|_{\mathfrak{v}} and \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{1}}|_{\mathfrak{v}}^{2} share the same eigenvectors. Thus, if \tilde{x}_{2} is an eigenvector of \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{1}}|_{\mathfrak{v}}^{2} , as demanded in case (b), it is also an eigenvector of \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{1}}|_{\mathfrak{v}} , which leads to a contradiction, as it would follow \tilde{x}_{2}\propto\tilde{x}_{3} , which is impossible by Definition 16. Therefore, the graph can only by of Type VI. A suitable basis exists; for example: \tilde{x}_{1}:=x_{3} , \tilde{x}_{2}:=-\lambda_{+}x_{1}+\alpha x_{2} and \tilde{x}_{3}:=-\lambda_{-}x_{1}+\alpha x_{2} . Here, it is important to note that \lambda_{+}\neq\lambda_{-} , since \alpha\neq-1/4 . ∎ Proposition 116. The real Lie algebra L_{1}^{3} , satisfies \operatorname{fg}(L_{1}^{3})=3 . Moreover, every \mathcal{M} -magma-gradation of L_{1}^{3} for which \operatorname{gra}(L_{1}^{3},\mathcal{M})=\operatorname{fg}(L_{1}^{3}) produces, via Algorithm 5, a graph that is equivalent to one of Type VI or Type XI. Proof. This proof follows the exact same reasoning as the proof for Proposition 115, with one key exception: the argument excluding the possibility of an \mathcal{M} -magma-gradation of L_{\alpha}^{3} that produces, via Algorithm 5, a graph that is equivalent to one of Type XI does not apply when \alpha=1 . In this case, \det(\boldsymbol{M})=0 , which allows for the existence of suitable choices of \tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3} exist that generate the desired graph. ∎ Proposition 117. The real Lie algebra L_{\alpha}^{3} for \alpha<-1/4 satisfies \operatorname{fg}(L_{\alpha}^{3})=2 . Moreover, every \mathcal{M} -magma-gradation of L_{\alpha}^{3} for which \operatorname{gra}(L_{\alpha}^{3},\mathcal{M})=\operatorname{fg}(L_{\alpha}^{3}) produces, via Algorithm 5, a graph that is equivalent to the one depicted in Example 102. Proof. Suppose \alpha<-1/4 . In Lemma 25 and the proof of Theorem 17, we have established that L_{\alpha}^{3} is not minimal-graph-admissible in this case. Consequently, by Lemma 101, one has \operatorname{fg}(L_{\alpha}^{3})<3 . Consider now a vector space decomposition L_{\alpha}^{3}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2} that provides \mathcal{M} -magma-grading of L_{\alpha}^{3} . Then, we can, assume without loss of generality, that \dim(\mathfrak{g}_{1})=2 and \dim(\mathfrak{g}_{2})=1 . Clearly, [\mathfrak{g}_{2},\mathfrak{g}_{2}]=\{0\} , due to the bilinearity and antisymmetry of the Lie bracket. Assume now that [\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{1}]\neq\{0\} . In this case, dictated by the bracket relations (34), there must exit an element y\in\mathfrak{g}_{1} such that y=\kappa x_{3}+v_{1} , where \kappa\in\mathbb{R}^{*} and v_{1}\in\spn\{x_{1},x_{2}\} . For simplicity, we denote the space \spn\{x_{1},x_{2}\}=:\mathfrak{v} and assume, without loss of generality, that \kappa=1 . Furthermore, there must exist an element z\in\mathfrak{g}_{1} such that z\notin\spn\{y\} and z=z_{1}+z_{2} , where z_{1}\in\spn\{x_{3}\} and z_{2}\in\mathfrak{v} . According to the requirement [\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{1}]\neq\{0\} and z\notin\spn\{y\} , we may assume without loss of generality z_{2}\neq 0 , otherwise one can exchange the roles of y and z . We now compute the bracket [y,z] to obtain information about [\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{1}] . Before doing so, note that all eigenvalues of the adjoint map \operatorname{ad}_{y} are either zero (corresponding to the eigenspace spanned by y itself) or imaginary, since the characteristic polynomial of the map \operatorname{ad}_{y} is given by -X(X^{2}-X-\alpha) . The discriminant of the polynomial X^{2}-X-\alpha is \Delta=1+4\alpha which is negative if \alpha<-1/4 [52]. Thus, the only real eigenvalue of \operatorname{ad}_{y} is given by zero corresponding to eigenspace spanned by y . Hence, z is not an eigenvector of \operatorname{ad}_{y} and [y,z]\notin\spn\{z\} . Consider now the two cases z_{1}\neq 0 and z_{1}=0 separately, in ordered to detect a contradiction: • 1. Case: z_{1}\neq 0 . Here, we can assume, without loss of generality, that z_{1}=x_{3}+z_{2} . Since z\notin\spn\{y\} , one has y-z=v_{1}-z_{2}\in\mathfrak{g}_{1} , where y-z\notin\spn\{y\} , y-z\neq 0 , [y,y-z]=-[y,z]\neq 0 , and y-z\in\mathfrak{v} . Thus, we can replace z with y-z , reducing the problem to the second case. • 2. Case: z_{1}=0 . In this case, it is straightforward to verify that [y,z]\in\mathfrak{g}_{2} . To see this, recall that z is not an eigenvector of \operatorname{ad}_{y} , that \operatorname{ad}_{y}(L_{\alpha}^{3})\subseteq\mathfrak{v} , and that z\in\mathfrak{v} , while y=x_{3}+v_{1} with v_{1}\in\mathfrak{v} . This implies that [y,z]\notin\mathfrak{g}_{1} . However, by the grading structure one has either [\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{1}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{1} or [\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{1}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{2} . Hence, [\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{1}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{2} , which requires \mathfrak{g}_{1}\subseteq\mathfrak{v} . By the same logic, one must have [\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{1} , as any non-zero x\in\mathfrak{g}_{2}\setminus\{0\} satisfies, according to the relations (34), [y,x]\neq 0 . However, x is not an eigenvector of \operatorname{ad}_{y} . Thus [y,x]\in\mathfrak{g}_{1} . Next, compute the eigenvalues of \operatorname{ad}_{y}^{2}|_{\mathfrak{v}} . Its characteristic polynomial reads: \displaystyle X^{2}-(2\alpha+1)X+\alpha^{2}=\left(X-\frac{1+2\alpha+i\sqrt{|1+4\alpha|}}{2}\right)\left(X-\frac{1+2\alpha-i\sqrt{|1+4\alpha|}}{2}\right), which has no real roots. Therefore, \operatorname{ad}_{y}^{2}|_{\mathfrak{v}} has no real eigenvectors88 8 In the context of Lemma 25, it is essential to realize that \operatorname{ad}_{y}^{\ell}|_{\mathfrak{v}} has real eigenvalues for some \ell\geq 3 and certain values of \alpha<-1/4 .. Thus, 0\neq[y,[y,x]]\notin\mathfrak{g}_{2} , although [\mathfrak{g}_{1},[\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2}]]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{2} , which is a contradiction, as it would imply that x is an eigenvector of y with a non-zero eigenvalue. This analysis shows that the initial assumption was incorrect. Hence, [\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{1}]=\{0\} . Inspection of the defining relations (34), one concludes that \mathfrak{g}_{1}=\mathfrak{v} , since \mathfrak{g}_{1} is two-dimensional. The space \mathfrak{g}_{2} must consequently be spanned by an element \tilde{y}=x_{3}+v with v\in\mathfrak{v} . The resulting \mathcal{M} -magma gradation satisfies [\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{1}]=[\mathfrak{g}_{2},\mathfrak{g}_{2}]=\{0\} , [\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2}]=[\mathfrak{g}_{2},\mathfrak{g}_{1}]=\mathfrak{g}_{1} . This completes the proof, as we have also shown that \operatorname{fg}(L_{\alpha}^{3})\geq 2 . Combined with the earlier result \operatorname{fg}(L_{\alpha}^{3})<3 , we conclude \operatorname{fg}(L_{\alpha}^{3})=2 when \alpha<-1/4 . ∎ Proposition 118. The real Lie algebra L_{-1/4}^{3} satisfies \operatorname{fg}(L_{-1/4}^{3})=2 . Moreover, every \mathcal{M} -magma-gradation of L_{-1/4}^{3} for which \operatorname{gra}(L_{-1/4}^{3},\mathcal{M})=\operatorname{fg}(L_{-1/4}^{3}) produces, via Algorithm 5, a graph that is equivalent to the one depicted in Example 102. Proof. First, note that L_{-1/4}^{3} not minimal-graph-admissible, as established in the proof of Lemma 25. Therefore, by Lemma 101, one has \operatorname{fg}(L_{-1/4}^{3})<3 . The remainder of this proof is a similar to approach of the proof for Proposition 117. Assume a gradation L_{-1/4}^{3}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2} , where \mathfrak{g}_{1} is two-dimensional and \mathfrak{g}_{2} one-dimensional. Clearly, [\mathfrak{g}_{2},\mathfrak{g}_{2}]=\{0\} . Suppose now that [\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{1}]\neq\{0\} . Then, there exists an element y\in\mathfrak{g}_{1} such that y=x_{3}+v_{1} with v_{1}\in\spn\{x_{1},x_{2}\}=:\mathfrak{v} . Furthermore, there must exist an element z\in\mathfrak{g}_{1} which satisfies z\notin\spn\{y\} . It follows that [y,z]\neq 0 , leaving two possibilities: (a) [y,z]\in\mathfrak{g}_{1} and consequently [\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{1}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{1} ; or (b) [y,z]\in\mathfrak{g}_{2} and consequently [\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{1}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{2} . Examine these two cases separately: (a) \boldsymbol{[\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{1}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{2}}. Since [y,z]\in\mathfrak{v}\setminus\{0\} and [y,[y,z]]\in(\mathfrak{v}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{1})\setminus\{0\} , it follows [y,z] lies in an eigenspace of \operatorname{ad}_{y} corresponding to the non-zero eigenvalue 1/2 . This element is consequently proportional to the element z_{2}:=-x_{1}/2+x_{2} . Moreover, one must have that \mathfrak{g}_{1}=\spn\{y,z_{2}\} . Now let x\in\mathfrak{g}_{2} , which implies that x\notin\spn\{z_{2}\} , and henceforth 0\neq[y,x]\notin\mathfrak{g}_{2} . This implies [y,x]\in\mathfrak{g}_{1} . However, since \operatorname{ad}_{y}(L_{-1/4}^{3})\subseteq\mathfrak{v} and y=x_{3}+v_{1} , one must have [y,x]\in\spn\{z_{2}\} . This would clearly require x=\kappa_{1}y+\kappa_{2}z_{2} , leading to a contradiction, as the grading requires \mathfrak{g}_{1}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{1}=\{0\} . (b) \boldsymbol{[\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{1}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{2}}. This implies that \mathfrak{g}_{2}=\spn\{[y,z]\} , since 0\neq[y,z]\in\mathfrak{g}_{2} . We can now consider the following two subcases: ( \alpha ) \boldsymbol{[\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{2}}. In this case, one must have that \mathfrak{g}_{2} is the eigenspace of \operatorname{ad}_{y} , since [y,z]\in\mathfrak{v}\setminus\{0\}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{2} and [y,[y,z]]\in\mathfrak{g}_{2}\setminus\{0\} . Two possibilities arise in this case: either (i) z\in\mathfrak{v} or (ii) z\notin\mathfrak{v} . In case (i), one needs to have [y,z]=\operatorname{ad}_{y}|_{\mathfrak{v}}(z)\propto-x_{1}/2+x_{2} . Since \det(\operatorname{ad}_{y}|_{\mathfrak{v}})=1/4\neq 0 , the linear map \operatorname{ad}_{y}|_{\mathfrak{v}} is invertible. Thus z\propto(\operatorname{ad}_{y}|_{\mathfrak{v}})^{-1}(-x_{1}/2+x_{2})\propto-x_{1}/2+x_{2} , contradicting the assumption that \mathfrak{g}_{1}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{2}=\{0\} . ( \beta ) \boldsymbol{[\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{1}}. Here, one has [y,[y,z]]\in\mathfrak{g}_{1}\setminus\{0\} , since [y,z]\in\mathfrak{g}_{2}\setminus\{0\} and [y,v]\neq 0 for all v\in\mathfrak{v}\setminus\{0\} . Moreover, because \operatorname{ad}_{y}(L_{-1/4}^{3})\subseteq\mathfrak{v} , one has [y,[y,z]]\in\mathfrak{v} , and hence \mathfrak{g}_{1}=\spn\{y,[y,[y,z]]\} , since y=x_{3}+v_{1} with v_{1}\in\mathfrak{v} . However, this also implies [y,[y,[y,[y,z]]]]\propto[y,[y,z]] , showing that [y,[y,z]] is an eigenvector of \operatorname{ad}_{y}^{2}|_{\mathfrak{v}} with a non-zero eigenvalue. A straightforward calculation reveals that the eigenvectors of \operatorname{ad}_{y}^{2}|_{\mathfrak{v}} and \operatorname{ad}_{y}|_{\mathfrak{v}} coincide, both to non-zero eigenvalues. Moreover, since [y,[y,z]]\in\mathfrak{v} , one must have that [y,[y,z]] is eigenvector of \operatorname{ad}_{y} to a non-zero eigenvalue. Consequently [y,[y,z]]\in\mathfrak{g}_{2} , which is forbidden by the condition that \mathfrak{g}_{1}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{2}=\{0\} . Therefore, both subcases ( \alpha ) and ( \beta ) are impossible. We conclude that [\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{1}]=\{0\} . The remaining steps follow analogously to the proof of Proposition 117. ∎ We can now combine these results to verify Conjecture 105 in case of the real Lie algebra L_{\alpha}^{3} : Theorem 119. Let L_{\alpha}^{3} be the real three-dimensional Lie algebra defined by the bracket relations (34) and (\mathcal{M},\delta) an abelian magma such that L_{\alpha}^{3} is \mathcal{M} -magma-graded, where the \mathcal{M} -gradation has the finest granularity. Then, the graph G_{L_{\alpha}^{3}}(V,E) associated with the pair (L_{\alpha}^{3},(\mathcal{M},\delta)) , obtained via Algorithm 5, faithfully represents the internal structure of L_{\alpha}^{3} , in the sense that the following assertions hold: (i) Solvability. The Lie algebra L_{\alpha}^{3} is non-solvable if and only if G_{L_{\alpha}^{3}}(V,E) contains a self-contained subgraph G_{W}\equiv G_{W}(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) that is induced by a closed directed walk W . (ii) Derived Series. The sequence of graphs obtained via Algorithm 8 can be associated with the Lie algebras of the derived series of L_{\alpha}^{3} , in the sense that for every \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , the direct sum of the subspaces labeling the vertices of the graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G_{L_{\alpha}^{3}}(V,E) coincide with derived algebras \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} . (iii) Nilpotency. The Lie algebra L_{\alpha}^{3} is nilpotent if and only if G(V,E) contains no closed directed walk. (iv) Lower central series. The sequence of graphs obtained via Algorithm 9 can be associated with the lower central series of L_{\alpha}^{3} , in the sense that for every \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} the direct sum of the subspaces labeling the vertices of the graphs \mathcal{C}^{\ell}G_{L_{\alpha}^{3}}(V,E) coincide with Lie algebras \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} of the lower central series. (v) Ideals. If W\subseteq V is a subset that satisfies the ideal-graph-property, then the direct sum of the subspaces labeling the vertices in W span an ideal of L_{\alpha}^{3} . (vi) Simplicity. If L_{\alpha}^{3} is simple, then there exists a closed directed walk W that induces a subgraph G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) with V=\tilde{V} . (vii) Semisimplicity. If L_{\alpha}^{3} is semisimple, then every vertex is part of a closed directed walk that induces a self-contained subgraph. Proof. This theorem is a direct consequence of the preceding Propositions 114, 115, 117, and 118 which classify all possible \mathcal{M} -magma-gradation of L_{\alpha}^{3} satisfying \operatorname{gra}(L_{\alpha}^{3},\mathcal{M})=\operatorname{fg}(L_{\alpha}^{3}) in terms of their associated graphs. Therefore, the claim can be verified by examining each possible graph individually. The case \alpha\leq-1/4 is addressed in Example 102. For \alpha>-1/4 ,the assertions (i)-(vii) follow immediately from the results in the main text: (i) Solvability: Theorem 62; (ii) Derived Series: Lemma 55; (iii) Nilpotency: Theorem 68; (iv) Lower central series: Lemma 67; (v) Ideals: Lemma 74; (vi) Simplicity: Proposition 87; (vii) Semisimplicity: Follows by considering the possible graphs and Theorem 62, since any solvable Lie algebra is not semisimple. ∎ Appendix F Table for the group grading of \mathfrak{sl}({3},{\mathbb{C}}) In this appendix, we present two tables summarizing the composition rules for the magma structure arising from the group grading of the Lie algebra \mathfrak{sl}({3},{\mathbb{C}}) . These gradings are based on its root system of type A_{2} [84]. The binary operation \delta:(A_{2}\cup\{0\})\times(A_{2}\cup\{0\})\to A_{2}\cup\{0\} satisfies the symmetry property \delta(x,y)=\delta(y,x) for all x,y\in A_{2}\cup\{0\} , which allows us to omit redundant entries in the tables. The first table corresponds to the standard grading on the set A_{2}\cup\{0\} , where 0 denotes a neutral element.
